WOMEN'S CHAT.

The End of the Fashionable Fortune-tenon—Society will be at length deprived of one of its amusements, by the verdict in the Palmistry trial, which means the final suppression of the fashionable fortune-teller. Both his and her disappearance will be viewed with mixed feelings as many people have a firm belief in palmistry and several of the Royal Princesses are known to have consulted, some of the leading professors of the art, but the public will agree that the law should be the same for the fashionable fortune teller, as for the gipsy. Palmistry apparently. will now be reduced to a harmless drawing-room diversion—nothing more than one of the recognised "parlour tricks "—by which one may say nice or nasty things to another as fancy may dictate, but it is said the professional palmists will now remove "bag and baggage" to France, so they will still be attainable to those prepared to make a journey. The Countess of Warwick.—Lady Warwick is taking an active interest in the support of the candidature of Labour representatives in Parliament, and her appear- ance on the platform in support of Mr. Pete Curran, the Labour candidate for Jarrow, is to be followed next month by a similar service on behalf of Mr. Walter Hudson, the Labour candidate for Newcastle-on-Tyne. It seems strange that the Countess, who is one of the recognised leaders of Society, should appear in such a cause, but the Countess prefers to combine the useful with the gilded life, and manifested her interest in Labour politics by her volume on "Joseph Arch's Reminiscences," which is quite the most important literary work she has undertaken. Old Letters.—A great writer, touching upon the sub- ject of keeping letters, sayerthat the only ones worth preserving are those of childhood, and schooldays. But there are others which might with advantage be kept, such as a letter that is singularly characteristic of a person dear or respected, or those written during the usually high spirited period most girls enjoy, between the ages of fifteen and twenty-one. For the billets-doux of an affair of the heart, past and gone, the bonfire is the better place, and every letter that reveals some other person's private affairs, or some items that would cause pain to another if made public, should be instantly des- troyed. As a general rule, it is better to destroy all letters as soon as read and answered, and it is a rule which, happily, largely obtains nowadays. The period when a woman gloried in bundles of old love letters— tied with faded ribbons—which she spent perchance sad hours in perusing, has passed by. We have healthier ideas, and no time for such unprofitable and melancholy self-imposed tasks. Shopping by Post.—Once upon atime, the woman, far from the haunts of the fashionable dress-maker and tailor, had to be content with pictures and sketches,and patterns of the latest toilettes, but nowadays all lead- ing firms send out to their customers a regiment of dolls' dressed down to the most minute detail, in the very latest styles. The fabrics employed are the same precisely that will be utilised for the toilette, and the price for each is quoted. The idea is taken from the mannequin, who, decked out in exquisite raiment, walks up and down the salon of the dressmaker's establishment, before the critical gaze of the customer. When the customer beholds a beautiful gown worn by a beautiful and graceful girl, she frequently finds it difficult to resist giving an order for a copy of it, and the mannequin therefore wins the day.

An Undesirable Mode.—Compared with even two or three summers back, there has been little "make up" in evidence during the past season, and the man who was asked in the time of Louis XV. what he thought of women, and replied that he was no judge of painting, would find little opportunity for such a taunt nowadays. either in France or in these Islands. Undoubtedly it is the more healthy lives we lead that has brought about this happier state of affairs. The outdoor recreations we indulge in paint natural roses on our cheeks, brighten our eyes, and give us a clear, complexion, and "make up "is an undesirable item of the past. Concerning Hair Dressing.—Hair is to be dressed high, this coming winter, and the majority of us will appreciate this decree, as a high dressing is—generally speaking—very mach more becoming than a low one. Only the loosest and most natural looking waves are permitted, anything stiff, formal, and artificial looking in this way being in the worst of taste. Hair dyes are still used, as indeed they always will be, but the usual thing just now is to remain faithful to the natural tint, rather than seek after another colour. The woman who changes the colour of her hair is rare. Instead she has the grey "touched up" as it comes, and thus preserves as far as possible, the natural tint. A Novel Fitted Trunk.—Many of the best travelling trunks are divided into compartments which save the jumble, and sometimes chaos which w often ensues when the trunk has been stood on end and upside down, and generally flung about in the manner approved by porters. But these fitted trunks are costly, and the same end may be attained by getting cardboard boxes which tailors use when sending out their wares. The one placed in first should as nearly as possible fit the trunk, others for blouses, fronts, and airy fairy items, head- gear, &c., being of% shape that will fill it without wast- ing an inch of room. With each box labelled, there is no confusion when unpacking, and it is easy to find any garment without hunting for it. Table Decoration.—The latest fad of the Society woman is to undertake the working out of her, dinner table schemes—an occupation that has indeed been raised to the position of a fine art. Taken as a whole simplicity rather than elaboration is the order of the day, and this state of affairs is probably due to his Majesty's objection to anything in the way of ruinous and ridiculous outlay, where table decoration is con- cerned. The first thing a hostess sees to is, that her table cloths and serviettes are well and carefully dressed, and that her menus and name cards are correctly carried out. Silk -or satin centres, tawdry horrors in chiffon, or art muslin, she dispenses with. A favourite device is a wall of maidenhair fern, the lamps, candles, or elec- tric lights being fitted with white, or yellow shales. Other hostesses just now are pinning their faith to heather, bracken, moss, ivy, and variegated creepers, while yet another idea is to depend solely upon the brown and green field grasses. Costly hothouse flowers and ferns are quite out of favour. Cheap Fruit.—lt would almost appear that a fine summer is worse for the fruitgrowers than a wet one. For months past housewives have rejoiced in cheap and abundant fruit, but from all parts of the country com- plaints come from the producers, of the ruinous prices obtained owing to the too abundant crops. In many parts, the prices realised for apples, pears and plums are said not to have paid for the picking, and consequently large quantities have been left to rot on the trees and ground. It seems a pity that some means cannot be devised for bringing the producer and the consumer together, so that the public could reap the advantage of this profusion, to a much greater extent than is the case. Retail prices it is true have been very low, but they are still many times as much as what the fruit growers say they can obtain for their produce from the market dealers.

AfADGE.

THIRTY MILES AN HOUR AT

GRAPPENHALL

Fine Inflicted and Licence Endorsed.

Before Mr. J. C. Parr and other magistrates at the Daresbury PettY Sessions on Tuesday Ernest A. Rosen- heim, 1, Croxteth-road, Liverpool, was summoned for having on the 11th September driven a motor-car on the Knutsford-road, Grappenhall, furiously, to the danger of the public. P.C. Williams stated that at 1.5 in the afternoon in question, he was on duty in Knutsford-road, G rappen- hall, when he saw a large motor car numbered K A 7 being driven in the direction of Warrington. It was going at a furious speed—at least 30 miles an hour. The defendant was the only occupant of the car. Witness shouted to him to stop, but he took no notice of him, and drove off at a great speed. Thomas Ireland; Lvy Cottage, Knutsford-road Grappen- hall, retired publican, expressed the opinion that defen- dant was driving at the rate of from 28 to 30 miles an hour. He had driven express and passenger trains, and therefore knew something about speed. Defendant objected to the question of speed being raised by the police. With regard to the speed the police must show-- The Clerk: The question is whether you were driv- ing recklessly. Defendant: But how can they get an estimate of • the speed? The Clerk repeated that it was a question whether the defendant was driving to the danger of the public. James Henry Daniels, Grappenhall, said that hearing the noise he rushed out of his house and went at once to the gate, but he could scarcely discern the car as it was "'nearly out of sight. While witness walked twelve yards the car had travelled 276 yards. P.C. Mottersall, stationed at Runcorn, said he went to Liverpool at the request .of Superintendent Ennion and saw the defendant with regard to the present charge. He told him that he had been sent by the Superintendent and that he (defendant) would be reported for furious driving.. Defendant said "Yes, I was the driver."

Defendant denied that that was a correct version of what occurred. He admitted that he was alone in the car, but he was not at the point named at 1.5 p.m. His car was on a trial trip. Witness was engineer to the Road Carrying Company Limited, and he emphatically denied that he was driving furiously to the danger of the pubic. The police had "got hold of the wrong car." The Bench retired to consider their decision, and on their return into Court the Chairman said the magis- trates were fully satisfied that there had been furious driving, and the offence had been aggravated by the defendant not stopping when the officer held up his hand. Some of the magistrates thought the penalty ought to be heavier than the one which would be inflicted namely £3 and costs, but this reckless driving must be stopped. A heavier penalty would be put on if a similar case came before the Bench again. Defendant's licence was endorsed, the magistrates being advised that such a course was compulsory.

A WALKING MATCH TROPHY.

42 Miles in Nine Hours.

Between Liverpool and Rhyl lies a stretch of road forty-two and a half miles in length, and a Liverpool cotton sampler named Wm. E. Page, now staying at 216, North-street, Leeds, has been awarded a silver medal by the Liverpnol Cotton Trade Association for walking this in the short time of nine hours, five minutes. We give herewith a reproduction of both sides of the medal.

The most interesting and im- lressiv& fact in connection with \is feat is that a short time before tdertaking his long walk, Page Is so weak from indigestion and )bility that he could not walk a -ea's length, and to Chas. Forde's n beans belongs the sole credit restoring his strength so com- ely. "One day when walking to the )atre, I felt so very dizzy that I to return home," says Mr. Page. •rom that time I began to have digestion. Food made me sick, rind collected, and caused great Jain. I had constipation, frequent ieadache, and became very de- ressed. No ms,tter what food I Jok, the wind and the pain and the ickly feelings followed it. I went A) a doctor, but as he failed to

tried another. .The second doctor vous dyspepsia, but he also failed to

treated me" benefit me. A third and fourth were consulted, with the same disheartening result. My doctor's bills and the cost of medicine amounted to £26. Having spent that, I found myself too weak to work, too, ill to move about, and I had wasted from ten stone six pounds to eight stone.

"Such was my state when I -began to take bile beans. The first box had scarcely any effect whatever, but as I persevered with them, slowly but surely I began to perceive a change for the better. I bought more of the beans, and continued to take them. The constipation was first cured, then the flatulence ceased to trouble me ; the acute pains after food in back and chest be- came less severe, and the overwhelming fits of exhaus- tion were no longer so frequent nor so powerful. In all I took six large boxes of bile beans, and by that time I was quite restored to my former excellent state of health. Tn proof of this I entered the Cotton walk and won a medal."

Daresbury Petty Sessions.

TCESDA.Y.—Bafore Mr. J. C. Parr (in the chair), Colonel Ridgway, and,Messrs. H. Neild, John White, F. Monks and J. Mahn Edelsten. MINOROFFESCES.—Howard Brereton,Latchford With- out, summoned for allowing two horses to stray on the highway in Acker's-lane, was ordered to pay the cost of the summons;and Henry Clayton, Porter-street,Waxring- ton, was, fined in the costs for obstructing the free passage of the highway at Latchford Without, by allowing a horse and cart to remain on it for an un- reasonable time.—A fine of is. 6d. was imposed upon James Garrity for making use ot obscene language.in Appleton-road on the 2nd October.—The following persons were fined as under for keeping dogs without licences : Walter Pearse,los. ; Thomas Hardman,Harry Short, and Harry Cook, 3s. 6d. each; and William Arthur Jones, ss.—Oswald Ball, of Antrobus, labourer, was fined 10s. for assaultin Birchall Frith.—Daniel Taylor was fined ss. and costs for having been drunk in charge of a horse and trap in Victoria-road, Stockton Heath, on the 14th September. THEFT Aar ACTON BRIDGE.—Thomas Threlfall pleaded guilty to stealing two hand saws, and two leather belts, of the value of 65., at Acton Grange. on September IBM—From a statement made by Superintendent Ennion, it appeared that on the day in question a DUIII ber of men bad been engaged in putting up telegraph wires. They left work at 5.30, Zlnd put the tools, &c., in a box on the roadside. The next morning the articles were missing. Information was given to the police and suspicion fell upon the prisoner, who was apprehended. —Prisoner was sent to gaol for three months.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 15, 1904.

AVER LONG YEARS :

A RED CROSS STORY.

BY GEORGE E. MANSFIELD.

Anthor of "Hospital ;Tenn," "The Viscount's Sisters," " Marie, " "Born to Blush Unseen, "

" Who is she ? " asked Nurse Simpson. - "More than I know," answered young Dr. Grey. "Knows her work, anyhow, " remarked Nurse Martin.

"hat she does," said the surgeon. It was at the Base Ho,pital at Hopetown, on the route to Kimberley. The speakers' eyes were turned towards a tall, aristocratic lady wearing the badge of the Red Cross Society. The person under discussion was bending over one of the beds, on which lay a wounded soldier. . The Boers had given Lord Methuen a hard fight for it. Every inch of the road had been contested. Two stubborn battles had already been fought, and there had been skirmishing night and day. On every ridge the English had found the enemy posted in strong positions. The Medical Staff had been exceptionally busy, and the Base Hospital at Hopetown was full of the wounded of both parties. Nurse Fleming, about whom her friends were talking, was. as I have said, a tall, aristocratic lady, somewherehetween fifty and sixty. A few grey locks peeped from beneath her cap. Time had left its finger on her brow; yet there was a nobility in the careworn face, and a certain poise of the fine shoulders, which told that the nurs,., one of our most indefatigable workers, was of gentle birth, and had been reared in a refined circle.

"They say she could as well be called Lady Fleming ' as Nurse Fleming," remarked one of the previous speakers. "Is she married ? " asked Dr. Grey. "Yes," said Nurse Martin; "at least, there's a wedding-ring on her finger." "So ! " ejaculated the surgeon. "She's probably some officer's widow who has volunteered for the work."

Just at that moment Dr. Henderson, our chief, entered hastily. "I say, Grey," he called, "there's been a battle at the Modder River. The Kimberley Light Horse came out this morning, and tried to effect a junction with Methuen."

"What " cried Nurse Simpson. "The Kimberley Light Horse? Captain Weldon's boys, doctor ? " "Yes," said Dr. Henderson. "Then it's sure to have been a victory," said Nurse Simpson, rubbing her hands gleefully. "Paul Weldon has never been defeated."

• "He's the man for the Boers," cried Nurse Martin. "He has done more for the defence of ,Kimberley than any other man." "Yes," observed the junior surgeon; "Weldon's name is in evirybody's mouth up this way. I'd like to see the man; both friends and foes have a 'good word to say for him." , "Well," said Dr. Henderson, "I suppose the 'hospital will be moved up to Kimberley as soon as .the town 'is in Methuen's hands, so perhaps your curiosity, Grey, and that of our nurses, will be ,gratified. I know their heads are fairly turned about young Weldon."' . "What naene did you say, docfor ? " asked a trembling voice at his side. . "Weldon, Nurse Fleming," said Dr. Henderson, ;turning round. "Captain Paul Weldon, of the Kimberley Light Horse. His name is in every person's mouth. He seems to lead a charmed life. Nothing daunts him. He assaults the most desperaOre positions; he has been in the jaws of death a score of times, and has come out unscathed." "Weldon, did you say ? " asked our old nurse again. We were all standing round the group. Every eye was turned to ;sTurse Fleming, and I could see that she was deeply agitated. - "Yes," said Dr. °Henderson. "They tell me he's a young, fair fellow about five-and-twenty. He is the hero of the hour, that's certain. The me4senger who has just come in says that but for him and his Light Horse, we should have lost the day." "I guess there will be some cheering when he comes into camp," said young Nurse Martin. "Yes," said the doctor; "he deserves it. He's a brave young fellow. His folk will. be proud of him. What—nurse Catch her, Grey!" For Nurse Fleming had dropped to the floor. "Fainted!" cried the assistants, rushing to the spot . "Stand back! Give her air!" commanded the chief surgeon. We raised the old lady, and placed her on one of the beds. Restoratives were applied, and in a few minutes her eyes opened. "Why, nurse," said Dr. Henderson, cheerily, "whatever's the matter? This isn't like you at all." • "Oh, I'm all right, doctor," she replied, in a low voice. "It was just a turn." "Was it anything I said ? " asked our chief surgeon. "Perhaps you know this Captain Weldon? Is he some friend of yours ? " The answer was faint, the words came slowly; but if she had a secret, Nurse Fleming kept it. "No," she answered, in a whisper. "No, doctor, II think not."

i'aul Weldon, of the Kimberley Light Horse, deserved all the praises which his friends bestowed on. him. At the beginning of the war he had levied the troop largely at his own expense, and his men had borne the brunt of the siege. Not only had they displayed the greatest heroism in defending the place, they had also taken part in every sortie from the town. ' Their leader, especially, had distinguished himself in many a rough skirmish. Nobody knew much of Weldon' s antecedents. He had come out to South Africa five years before, when he was only twenty years of age. Daring and cool-headed, he soon made his mark. Although a man of iron sinews and unflinching will, his manners were gentle and courteous. He was the idol of his men, all of whom would have followed him to death.

The story of Methuen's famous advance to Kim- berley is well known. After the Battle of Graspan, the English General had opened heliographic com- munication with the beleaguered town. Using the sun's rays to flash his message, Methuen had informed the besieged of the route by which he was advancing, and the probable date of his arrival. When the sun's rays flashed Methuen's message, all the bells of Kimberley were set ringing. The officers immediately held a council, and it was Paul Weldon who proposed that another sortie should be made, partly to divert the Boers' attention, also to form a junction with the relieving column. The project was carried out. Early on the morn- ing when our story opens, the Kimberley Light Horse had sallied forth from the beleaguered town. The men were all Britons by birth, though they wore the slouched hat, and had the bronzed faces of South Africa. For weeks they had defended the English flag on the frontier against an over- whelming host, and now that the Guards were advancing to their rescue, Paul Weldon and his bold lads determined to make a dash through the Boer lines, and assist the English column up the . Dawn was just breaking ,when the company of horsemen rode ottt of the town. The Boers com- manded the main roa.d south. Their artillery was placed in a strong position, but Paul Weldon hoped, by skirting round one of the ridges, to be able to break through the enemy's line at a weaker spot. "Six o'clock, Merrick," he said to his lieutenant, as the company wheeled to the left. "At this very moment time. Guards will be. setting out on their last stage." "Yes," replied Jack Merrick, "and it's'a tough bit of road they have at the end of their journey. Eighteen miles steady uphill, and itn enemy strongly posted at the end." "That's the worst of it," rejoined Paul Weldon. "Methuen's column will be fagged out before the fight begins. We must try and force a way for them."

The Light Horse accomplished the circuit of the ridge without raising the alarm. The little company only comprised three hundred men—a mere handful to the seven thousand armed Boers who were known to be lying close at hand. "Halt!" cried their- leader, as the company came to the foot of a little knoll. "Take the com- mand, Harris," said Weldon to a sturdy, bronzed figure on his right. "Merrick and 1 are going forward to reconnoitre. You had better bid the lads dismount and give the horses a rest. -We may have to wait here a bit." "Right, Captain," answered Harris. Waving his hand to his followers, Paul Weldon, accompanied by Jack Merrick, tode forward in advance. Tethering their horses when they had half-climbed the knoll, they made the rest of the ascent on their hands and feet. Coming to the

top, both threw themselves upon • tne ground and 'canned the horizon. "See," whispered our hero. "Yes," said Merrick, grimly, The place where our two friends were lying cornrn na,nded a wide view towards the south. The Slodder River flowed half-a-mile below, and captain Weldon could see the road along which the Guards would come. But more than they could see that road. Not ighty yards from where they were concealed, two :ong guns were placed in position, with their nuzzles in the direction of the Modder River. A !orce of at least .a thousand Boers was drawn up by :he battery, and Weldon could see their officer aosting his men in the most advantageous places, m assault Methuen's column when it began the Ascent.

"See that, Jack ? "

"Their scouts have brought them word," whispered Merrick to his companion. "Yes," said Captain Weldon, "that was to be expected, of course." "What do you think of doing ? " asked Jack. "No good attacking at present," replied the leader. "That would only bring the whole Boer force down on us, and we should be surrounded. Go back, and bid our men breakfast. We'll wait till Methuen appears ; then, when the action begins, we'll make a rear attack and try to silence these guns." Merrick hurried back to the troops with Captain Weldon's instructions. All alone, our hero lay among the thick grass on the top of the knoll, watching the enemy taking their positions, and forming his own plan of assault. His brow grew dark as the rumbling of wheels fell on his ear. Turning his eyes to the right, he saw another company of Boers marching down the hill with three more pieces of artillery. "All depends on us, Jack,' companion rejoined him. "See a cloud of dust far in the south ? " said Merrick, putting the glass to his eye. "Can that be the advance guard of our troapi "Yes," said Paul Weldon, after a careful look. "Methuen is up to time. They're hurrying forward by forced marches." • At that moment a bugle sounded close at hand. It was the Boer signal. The enemy had also observed the .advancing column. Ten minutes later the English troops could be clearly discerned. _ _ _ _ _ _ _

For at the word of command, the enemy, of whom at least two thousand were now in position, had, dropped under cover behind the rocks. Branches of trees were spread over the muzzles of the ckinnon, to hide them from the advancing English. "They know how to lay a trap, by whispered Jack Merrick

Merrick hastened away. Lord Methuen's force was now, less than a mile away, and was advancing steadily along the Modder River. Paul Weldon could see the Boer gunners busy loading their cannon. The Commander himself was stationed at the battery. Our hero could hear his voice as he issued his commands. A minute later, and the first gun boomed forth. The English trumpets rang out below. As soon as the smoke cleared away, Weldon could see that the column had halted. "We're ready, Captain," whispered. a voice behind him. Merrick had returned. "The Guards are preparing for the assault," said our hero.

No need of the glass now. With the naked eyE Merrick could see that the advancing column had reached the foot of the incline which led up to du Boer position. For over half-a-mile our troops would have to ascend in the teeth of a murderous fire. But undeterred by the broken ground, un- daunted by the fact that they were tired and foot- sore, the Naval Brigade and Grenadiers advanced to the attack.

"Quick, now!" whispered Captain Weldon. Harris had brought up the troop close at hand. "Ready, lads ? " shouted Paul Weldon, as he and Merrick leaped on their horses, and prepared tc lead the way. "Aye, Captain," cried the men. "Forward, then ! " With a shout, the Kimberley Light Horse swepl round the knoll, and before the Boers knew of theil presence, they rode headlong into the battery. Sabres flashed in the air, pistols were fired. "Down with them!" cried Paul Weldon, as he and his men cut their way through the Boer ranks.

- Once! Twice! Thrice! tie Light Hors( charged along . the ridge. On cannon had beer hurled from its position. A second boomed forth. but with a ringing cheer, the Guards and Sailor Boys came dashing up the hill. "Back again !" shouted the leader of the Light Horse. Wheeling round, he and his men for the fourth time took the Boers in flank. It was a wild, helter-skelter ride. The Kimberley Light Horse were leaping pell-mell among the rocks, behind each of which lay a crafty enemy-. Many a steed stumbled and fell, many- a brave rider met hi( death. Harris had been shot right at the beginning of the engagement. Jack Merrick had received an ugly cut on the left arm, but, nothing daunted, he pressed on at his leader's side. Wherever the fight was thickest, there Weldon's voice was heard urging on his men, there his sword.gleamed, deal- ing death to the Boer. "Forward ! " he cried again, as thirty of the enemy appeared on a piece of even ground, evidently prepared to make a stand. The Boer Coinmander was there in person. He had gathered a party round the last piece of artillery, which the gunners were reloading in quick haste. It was pointed against the line of Guards, who were now well up the ridge. "Down with them!" shouted Captain Weldon, digging his spurs into his horse. Fifty yards, forty yards, .not more than thirty yards lay between the Kimberley Riders and their foes. If the Light Horse were in time ! If they could cut down the party, and prevent that piece being fired, it meant the lives of a hundred English- men. Merrick's horse stuitibled over a rock.

"Steady, Jack!" cried the Captain, clutching .at his friend's bridle.

"Right !" shouted sack, regaining his saddle. "Forward, lads !" cried Weldon. Ten yards! Six yards! "Down with thew !!' cried the Light Horse.

"Halt !" shouted their leader, suddenly, holding up his sword. "They've hoisted the 'white flag." The horses were pulled up on their haunches. Merrick could hear the Guards and the Naval Brigade cheering not fifty yards altay. It was the basest piece of treachery in the whole war. A. white handkerchief waved over the Boer cannon, but a second later it belched forth its death message, and a withering rifle fire was poured into the Kimberley Light Horse.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

There is quite a rage for all shades of yellow from the palest saffron to a deep rich orange, and this colour is introduced on all sorts of hats and toque.‘ for immediate wear, ranging from the plain felt for morning use to the picture hat for afternoon and evening wear. In the former a narrow band of folded velvet or a smart quill emphasise the note of colour, while in the latter the elaboration is greater as ruchings and rosettes and clusters of shaded yellow roses and autumnal tinted foliage ap- pear sometimes on one and the. same model. A lovely picture hat of soft white felt with a furry surface looked most distinguished on the

head of a brunette, the only trimming being a long and very handsome ostrich feather which shaded from a soft creamy yellow to a rich orange tint. Yellow, however, in any shade is not a colour which should be worn by any

A MORNING TOILETTE.

woman with a sallow complexion as it is not in such a case a becoming colour. It requires that the skin of the wearer should be perfectly clear, whether the cheeks are white or rose tinted is not such a matter of importance. In Paris the orange yellow of a rich deep tone is the most popular, and indeed in some of the newest models the shade almost merges into a tomato red.

_ It is used in piping the strappings of some of the very smartest serge and tweed gowns, and the morning toilette of very dark blue given in my first sketch is finished in this manner, while the collar, cuffs, and revers are of the orange red cloth. The smart little coat which is made in somewhat the style of a Norfolk jacket is secured round the waist by a soft leather belt and finished by ornamental but- tons. The skirt is cut to clear the ground all 'round. Most of the smartest cloth gowns for useful everyday wear have a skirt of this description, for short skirts are still considered correct wear. They are not, however, quite so short as they were last season, for it is not every woman who possesses sufficiently imma- culate feet and ankles to stand such a severe test. In selecting a short walking skirt of the kindillustrated let me counsel that the skirt is of the same length all the way round. This prevents my possibility of the unsightly droop at the back which disfigures many an otherwise, smart toilette. Indeed, our French sisters find that it is more becoming to have

The short accordion pleated skirt of black watch tartan looks ad- mirable for morning wear in conjunction with a plain little sac coat of scarlet cloth finished at the throat by a narrow turn-over linen collAr and a stock tie of silk.

Sac coats of all kinds are amongst the most popular modes and my second sketch gives a charming model in grey cloth with handsome revers and cuffs of white cloth embroidered in black and silver. The deep and wide cuffs are filled in by a double frill of lace, and the coat itself is lined in with soft white satin so that it is sufficiently dressy not only to do duty for afternoon wear over a dainty blouse, but to serve as a wrap for theatre or restaurant wear, particularly useful for those who live in the suburbs and have a short railway journey into town. It is of so loose a style as to be easily slipped on over a dainty frock without serious- ly crushing it and can be worn either optn or closed. I should suggest the addition of ruffles of really good lace for evening use should one be the fortunate possessor of any heirlooms of this nature. One can never be sufficiently grateful to one's. grandmothers and aunts, etc., for so tenderly caring for their treasures of lace and embroidery as they are almost invaluable nowadays to their feminine descendants, especially to all those with a limited dress allowance.

N HAT.

My last sketch depicts a very charming hat of soft brown chenille which, by the way,is one one of the prettiest styles of hat for immediate wear. The only trimming used are a couple of yellowish red wings, for the material of the hat is of itself so pretty that it would be a mistake to conceal it by any elaborate trimmings. The shape is a very smart one. It is a mistake to buy too inexpensive a hat when chenille is in question as in the inferior ones the chenille is only indifferently sewn on to the frame and when the hat is taken into wear it parts com- pany and displays the fonndation in a most unsatisfactory fashion. Velvet trimmed with feathers is used on the very large hats of the picturesque order and the all black hai: still holds its own and is, if possible, this season larger than ever before. . English velveteen is said to be very popular at the present time with French people. At one time nothing. but the rich- est of silk velvet left the looms, but now the demand for velveteen is so great that the Lyons manufacturers have been obliged to bow to the public requirements, and French velveteen in all colours is to _be seen every-

It was evening of the same day. In the Base Hospital at Hopetown diin lights were burning, all was hushed. The British victory had been com- pletf, but it had cost a terrible price. Our glorious Grenadiers and the Naval Brigade had suffered heavily. Over a hundred men were reported dead: All afternoon the wounded had .been carried in ; every bed was full. Couches had been placed on the ground—it was scarcely possible to pick one's way among them, the hospital tent was so _ _

"Can't possibly take any more," said Dr. Hen- derson, going to the door, as a voice , craved admittance.' A party of horsemen had just ridden up. "But you _must, doctor," exclaimed their officer. "Can't be done," said our chief. "There's not en inch of room." "Lord :Methuen's special orders soldier. "It's---" I could not catch the name : but I saw old Hen- derson put his hand to his brow. "Come, Grey ; come. Ackland," he called. Following him n outside, we lifted the stretcher which lay before the door. "Lord Methuen requests that Captain Weldon shall receive your immediate attention, doctor," said the officer in command. : "Everything shall be done," answered our chief. We bore the wounded man inside the tent. A glance told us that it was a desperate case. Lifting him carefully- from the stretcher, we placed him on the table, , and our chief, Grey, and myself, began to search for the wounds. Captain Weldon had been shot in four places. His left arm was shattered, there was a bullet in his leg. Something had grazed his cheek, and from his breathing, we feared that a ball had touched his lung. His hair was all clotted with blood, and his face smeared. "Bring your sponge here, 'Norse Fleming," cried our chief. • With light, soft step, the old lady came forward. She had the gentlest.of woman's hands, and the touch of the cool spongo must have done young • A, ,111. Wined tin' pood from hi‘g

"Geoige ! George ! " she exclaimed, bending over him, while tears burst from her eyes.

"it's Captain Weldon, nurse, "'said our chief. ait's my son," she cried.

"Your son, Nurse Fleming ?" "Yes, Dr. Henderson." "Then he's a brave young fellow.," replied the doctor.

My eyes had been fastened on the wounded man. His face had been deathlr.pale, but when he heard her voice a flush passed over it. A faraway look was in his eye's. He seemed to be recalling some- thing. Was it a distant scene, some peaceful English home„ left, perhaps, years ago ? "George," said the old lady again. "George, don't_ you know me ? "

"Mother ! " he replied, trying to raise himself up on his arm.

"Yes," she sobbed. "Yes, dear. It's me." "I thought you were far away," said the wounded man, in a tone of wonder.

"No, George, lam here. Your father is dead. He forgave you before the end. The old home was never the same after you went away. But you'll come Lack, dear ? You'll come back with me to England, George? Doctor, you'll save, my boy !" It was a desperate case. -Old Henderson, Grey, and myself, stood by his side for hours. Nurse Fleming never left us. But by the morning the crisis was past, the balls had been extracted, there was hope. We learnt the whole story afterwards. Young Fleming's father, an ex-Colonel of the Guards, had been a sharp, quick-tempered man. Doubtless, he meant George well. But along with his mother's gentleness the son had inherited a touch of his sire's spirit. Young and impetuous, chafirg it restraint, he had left home in his eighteenth year for some trifling quarrel. Old Colonel Fleming must have been a bit to blame, for, as Lady Fein- ing let out, he had never been the same man :Jter- wards. On her husband's death, she herself, with a great sorrow at her heart, had tried to forget it in labouring for the British soldiers, among whom her life had been passed. It was a desperate case, as I have said, but thanks to our chief's skiTh and to a mother's. love. discharged the Captain from Hopetown Hospit;l two days ago. He and his mother are now on lteir way to England. [ThE END.]

Methodist Free Church, Buckley-street.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

At Sunday's meeting there was an average 'attendance of members present. Nine new members joined, making the total nnmber 534. The Secretary gave them a very hearty welcome, and congratulated the members upon bringing so many new friends, 50 having joined in about four weeks.—Mr. William Farrar presided, and spoke upon "Sympathy." One must possess sympathy to be able to impart it, and imparting sympathy helped the receiver of it to bear his burdens, and overcome his difficulties.—Mr. A. 0. Bidgood, of Manchester, was the speaker, taking as his subject" The Bible." He spoke of the authority of the Bible, and its continuity and great influence upon the world. Many objectors and critics pointed out what they considered errors in the Bible, but many of them were errors of the copyists only. The word it gave was inspired, the holy men writing as they were moved to do so. God's word, like God, was the uplifter, the helper, and the inspirer of men. By it the individual was blest; the nation exalted. No other book met the needs of man like the Bible—the great and living testimony of Christ's love, work and redemption.—Mr. A. Cotterill sang in a pleasing manner "The River of Years," Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., presiding at the organ. Messrs. Miles and Bannister also took part in the meeting.

Rev. J. F. Matthews and Salvation Army Work.

At Latchford Baptist church on Sunday evening the Rev. J. F. Matthewsipreached on" General Booth as a social and religious force." He said that one of the great features of post-pentecostal days, was that the old men became dreamers of a goldenfage in which a new kingdom should be established. It was to differ from every other kingdom in form, spirit, method and life. It was to be a spiritual domain of which Christ was the supreme ruler and men after God's own heart were to be the citizens thereof. Among the dreamers and visionaries of to-day none was greater than William Booth, who in 1878, started in its present form the Salvation Army, a great Christian brotherhood with a distinct .military organisation. The two features of his work were of a social and religious nature. These terms were, perhaps, a little arbitrary, for all true social work was in its essence religious. In his shelters, farm colonies, over-sea colonies, and all the varied and multiform agencies of his vast work, be kent ever before himself and his colleagues both the redemption of society and the individual. The" teach- ing of the Cross" the " conversion of the sinner," were conspicuous doctrines of the Salvation Army. Always and everywhere its officers and workers brought the holiest influences to bear upon the lazy, the outcast, and the vicious. The same method was advocated in the General's scheme for eliminating the tramp. Even in his new suggestion for founding ' a Humanitarian University," to study by scientific methods hOw best to deal with the grave social problems of our time, he desiderated that the students should be men and women who love their fellows. As a social and religious force General Booth had largely helped in the nobler and higher interpretation of. Christ's teaching, that that which obtain9d forty years ago. The typical Christian Church was no longer satisfied with holding two ser- vices on Sunday, and a mid-weekly prayer meeting. Everywhere there was a desire to subserve the highest interest of man in all its forms by the steady and strenuous application of the ethics and mind of Christ

THE " TRADE " AND THE LICENSIN ACT.

Mr. W. J. Shaw and the Compensation Question.

TRUST COMPANIES CRITICISED.

Mr. W. J. Shaw, of Warrington presided at a general meeting of delegates from beer and wine trades associa- tions in various parts of Lancashire and Cheshire held at Salford, and in the course of his address congratulated the retailer on his substantial position created by the passing of the Licensing Act, 1964, which he said, had only been brought about by attention and I careful watching of the executive, who had been well I informed, and had laboured hard to see that the retailer I had no advantage taken of him either by the teetotal Iparty or the brewer. The Compensation Bill was very satisfactory, inasmuch as the holder of a licence with a, tenure of one year or under paid nothing to the compen- I sation fund, and received full value of his interest, lin the event of his house or licence being taken I away under the Bill. .He was very sorry that a time limit had cropped up in the Bill, but at the same time they must be thankful that it did not apply to the exist- ing-licences—only to new licences that might be granted by the licensing bench forany term not exceeding seven years, altogether inadequate. This in his opinion' was the thin end of the wedge leading to more serious inter- ference with the licensing trade of the country by trying to establish a time limit to all licences. The opponents of their trade were a class that were never satisfied and never acknowledged defeat, but came smiliitgly up every year with old ideas in a different form, to place every obstacle they could to hinder the trade being carried on, no matter what the conditions might be. Why did they not charge and fire at the trust com- panies? He knew of a certain house held by a trust company that had discharged managers through profits not being large enough. In this particular case the refreshment played a very poor and shabby part for the profits of the establishment. If there was not sufficient sale Of food to declare a dividend the profit I must come from somewhere, the only course open being the drink department, and these profits not being what the management required the manager was dismissed Did not that shoW plainly that the trust companies,who were the teetotal party, would never attack the part of the trade that they were in particular connected with ? , Why did the licensed victuallers, as a powerful body, not oppose the trust, and show the Lord Grey party ' that they did not intend to be further punished' by the course of the United Kingdom Alliance Or any other body, who could not and would not act with fairness to both sides ? 1

CHESHIRE JUNIOR LEAGUE.

WALKER'S BREWERY (WARRINGTON) V. WIDNES RANGERS.—This match was played at Widnes on Saturday. The visitors were the first to be seen to advantage, Lloyd and Roscoe being to the fore in some sterling play. For an infringement in the scrum, the Rangers were penalized and Roscoe made no mistake with the penalty kick. This put the locals on their mettle, and the forwards invaded the visitors' territory, but their stay was all to brief, Hewitt sending them to the rightabout. Lloyd gathered nicely from a scrum, and tricking the opposing halves, gave to Roscoe, who registered an unconverted try. After a spell, of mid- field play Lloyd again showed his opponents his heels and had an open line, but he preferred to pass to Timmins, who scored for Roscoe to improve. Half-time: Walker's, ten points; Rangers. nil. On resuming, the Widnesians pressed and from a forward rush Massey scored a try which was improved upon. Lloyd again opened out the game and Hewitt crossed but the points were disallowed. Final : Walker's Brewery, ten points; Widnes Rangers, five points.

NORTHERN UNION COUNTY CHAMPIONSHIP.

CHESEIRE v. CUMBERLAND.

This important county fixture took place on the grand of the Birkenhead Club, at Prenton Park, on Thursday afternoon. elorious weather prevailed, but there was only a fair attendance. Cumberland won the toss, and set their opponents to face a powerful sun. Walker kicked off on behalf of Cheshire, who at once forced a way into their opponents' half. A free kick followed the preliminary scrimmaging, and then Eagers took a free kick for the visiting county, Houghton getting in his kick in fine style. Hard play followed but, with the Cestrians pressing, some exciting moments followed on the Northerners' line. The pressure was relieved by Crane, and again the ball was scrummaged at the centre flag, the backs being very evenly matched. Ritson, Owens, and Templeton did good work for Cumberland, but Price and Butterworth responded. Half-time—Cumberland, 2 points; Cheshire, nil. Following the change of ends, Cheshire went down in great style with fine forward work, but Eagers repulsed and, ofter a rapid visit to the other end, a long rush by Cheshire resulted in Richardson getting over, Molyneux kicking a goal and giving the Cestrians the lead. Interesting play followed, but Cheshire were short of Price. The game was interesting to the finish. Result —Cheshire, 5 points; Cumberlane, 2 points.

HOW TO GROW SKIN,

A New and Wonderful Discovery.

One of the greatest triumphs of the new century is the discovery of a unique preparation for growing new healthy skin. Indeed,accidents and skin diseases are so common to-day that there is no reader who will not feel that the discovery meets a real and distinct need in ho me, factory, and field. Ever since man acquired the instinct to rub a plaoe that hurts, attempts have been made to find the right article for him to rub with. In the times of Ancient Greece, warriors and gladiators used a secret balm upon the injuries they received in the arena or in battle. The Roman charioteers, too, did not emerge from their mad races without some severe bruise or gaping wound; and to anoint each hurt with their favourite balm was a necessary part of the day's programme. These articles, like the cheap oint- ments, salves, and embrocations sold to-day, were very crude • and unreliable. Until recently there was one fatal objection to this class of preparation—that they were too coarse for the pores of the skin to absorb their healing and soothing ingredients; or that the presence of rancid animal fat and mineral poison rendered them not only inefficient, but even dangerous for theaverage skin. To overcome these long-standing objections, an eminent chemist commenced fresh investigations, turn- ing his attention to the rare medicinal herbs growing beyond the borders of civilization. All available knowledge was gleaned about these plants; and it was discovered how some of them could be made to yield gums and juices possessing healing. soothing, and antiseptic properties of a wonderful order. Costly experiments were made with mixtures or blendinp of these juices, and at last a combination with astonishing powers as a healer and antiseptic was secured. To this unique preparation the name of Zam-buk was given, and this is the great healer which is to-day generally regarded as a household necessity. Zam-buk has more than fulfilled the expectations of its discoverer, and many medical men to-day freely extol its merits. It possesses a wide range of usefulness, being especially recommended for cuts, bruises, burns, scalds, sprains, stiffness, weak ankles, swollen joints, bad legs, blind and bleeding piles, running sores, eczema, pimples, boils, rash, raw chapped hands, scalp irritation, sore heads, barber's rash, raw chin after shaving, insect-bites, nettle-stings sore throat and chest, sore and aching feet, c'.ilblains cold-sorest festering sores, poisoned wounds, and other diseased, injured, and inflamed or irritated conditions of the skin. Zarn-buk as an antiseptic is unrivalled. Not only does it soothe pain and subdue inflammation, but it positively prevents or arrests festering and sup- puration. Rubbed well into the parts affected, Zam-buk also gives great relief from neuralgia, toothache,sciatica, lumbago, rheumatism, chest and back pains, and colds. A box of Zam-buk may be had of any chemist for one- and-three-halfpence, or two-and-nine for a large family size (which contains neerly four times the quantity in the one-and-three-halfpenny) ; or post free for the same price direct from the Zam-buk co., Redcross-street London, B.C. This balm is. Nature's true healer, and every home needs it.

(Copyright.)

THE INTERESTING TENOR,

BY RALLI WEST, Author of "Found Out, by a Dream," "The Sweetest Spell of All," &c.

At Laxton, a remote seaside place little fre- quented by summer visitors, lived Lucinda Sinclair, a maiden lady of uncertain age and moderate fortune. She was intensely proud of the fact that her "dear mamma's" maternal uncle had been an Earl. He had long since been gathered to his fathers, but the reflected glory of such a connec- tion was the one bright spot in Miss 'Sinclair's colourless life, in spite of the fact that his aristo- cratic descendants had a provoking way of com- pletely ignoring her existence. Her pretty niece Nora, who shared her home, found the getter days" of which her aunt was so fond of talking a terrible stumbling-block to sociability; for as the Laxtonians for the most part were hovering mid- way between a future of elegant leisure and a past of struggling poverty and sordid care, it was, of course, quite an impossibility for a lady of Miss Sinclair's antecedents to visit with them. Genteel poverty such as her own was one thing; but vulgar sufficiency—no, one must draw the line somewhere. "I really cannot, my love; it is out of the. ques- tion. Consider the different sphere in which my poor dear mamma moved as an equal," she would say when Nora sometimes pleaded to be allowed to go to tea with a friendly neighbour. The vicar's wife was of course an exception; but then Nora did not take to her. Chronic want of means appeared to have had a bad effect on her temper, poor woman, judging from the tart speeches to which her conversational powers were as a rule limited. The vicar—the Rev. Frank Dale—was a cheery old man, with a temper as sweet as his wife's was sour; but then he never concerned him- self about the petty details of household expenses. He had enough to do in his scattered parish, and left to Mrs. Dale the daily grind of striving to make both ends meet on an income far too small to allow of the feat being possible. Nora had money of her own, and would long ago have left Laxton but that. she knew Miss Sinclair would have had a difficulty in keeping on the villa without her contribution towards the home expenses; and after the loving care which had been bestowed upon her in childhood, when she was left an orphan, and Lucinda was considered by the rest of the family to be the only one of them who had sufficient leisure to look after a child, it seemed cruel to go when she really could be of use and repay the kindness of former years. Perhaps Harry Mayne, a young curate working hard in the London slums, had something to do with the girl putting up as contentedly as she did with the dull- ness of Laxton. They were waiting till he had a better curacy before commencing their matrimonial career, and meanwhile Nora had taken an engage- ment as daily governess to the doctor's children. She had been spending the Easter holidays with Harry's parents, and had not intended returning until an evening train, but, being "compelled to suddenly alter plans to suit her host's convenience, arrived at Miss Sinclair's prim little villa consider- ably earlier than expected. Letting herself in— nobody kept their doors fastened at Laxton—she was much astonished to hear a tenor voice—and a remarkably good one, too—warbling "My Pretty Jane." Who ever could it be? Not a soul on visiting terms with Miss Sinclair possessed a voice like that. She waited until the last notes had died away, then softly opened the door and went in. Her aunt and Sophy, their one servant, were busily engaged making garments for the poor; and standing at the piano, turning over a pile of music, was a tall, thin man, whose pale, cadaverous- looking face made Nora wonder how ever he managed to sing as he did. A violin lay on the table, and beside it was a tray with an empty cup and plate. Miss Sinclair gave a little scream when she caught sight of her niece. "Nora! Who ever would have thought of seeing you so early ? " Then, as the girl glanced inquir- ingly at the stranger, who stood in the background, surveying them with large, melancholy-looking eyes, she added hurriedly: "My dear, let me intro- duce you to Mr. Marmaduke Browne, a gentleman who has done me the honour to cheer my lonely afternoons by coming to sing to me." Nora returned Mr. Browne's low bow rather coldly. Something in the expression of his face repelled her, though she could not have said why. "I will not disturb you, aunt, for I know how much you enjoy music, especially your favourite old songs. Sophy, will you take up my bag, please? Who is Mr. Browne ? " she asked, as the girl followed her upstairs. "A street playing gentleman, miss," was the unexpected reply. "What do you mean ? " said Nora, quickly. "Why, min, the day you left, it turned out a wretched afternoon—the wind something terrible. Soon after dinner we heard a man singing outside. It was one of the mistress's favourite songs—made her think of the dear old days, she said. Instead of giving me the money to take out, as she does to the organ men, she made me bring Mr. Browne into the hall, and had a long talk with him there. The next day and the next lie came, and sang, I must say, beautiful. At last mistress said it was a cruel shame to let a delicate man stand about in the cold, so she asked him if he would come and sing in the drawing-room—he being superior like; and he has come every afternoon since." "But whatever makes him stay on in a little place like Laxton ? " said Nora. "He can't possibly make it worth his while to sing in the streets, when there are so few pers-ms to listen." But Sophy could tell her nothing more, so she had to wait until her aunt was at leisure to expatiate on what had proved quite an event in her monotonous life.

"A most sad, most romantic story, my dear," she began. "Like myself, Mr. Browne has seen better days; indeed, he as much as told me that he was connected with the aristocracy. Such a strange coincidence! It appears that he married for love. His wife was pretty, but poor, and of low origin—a youthful folly, le himself owns. A summer idyll, for before another summer came round his young wife and her baby lay in their grave. He lost all heart then; was nearly mad with grief; and for months was too ill to work. An illness on the nerves, I fancy; at any rate, the doctors said it was imperative for him to live in the South of France—Monte Carlo,.Monaco—thotte kind of places, you know. At last he was seized with an intense longing to revisit the spot where he first met his wife—a village not far from here. By that time he had hardly any money left. He had made a little by singing at dinners, and com- posing valses and trifles of that description; but lie is ambitious. He is writing an opera, and felt that he must have absolute quiet. in which to com- pose what he believes will be a masterpiece. So he took a room near here, and there he has been ever since, absorbed in his opera. He has played me bits of it, and I should say it would indeed be a masterpiece. Some of the airs reminded me so much of many that I heard in the old days—dear mamma and I were constantly at the opera then —but when I told him so he seemed quite over- come—said it was so wonderfully encouraging. It is strange, though, that since then he has never played me a single air; he says I shall judge so much better when I hear the whole. He is work- ing tremendously hard at it." "But meanwhile, what does he live on ? " asked practical Nora. "Ah ! that is the sad part of it. He worked on and on, never noticing how little money was left, until one day he found there were only a few shillings in his store. He felt he must stay on in this neighbourhood; that the spot hallowed by the memories of his sweet young wife was the only place where he could compose the music with which he trusts one day to electrify the world • so in despair he took his violin and went out to play in the streets. Just fancy, my love, a member of the aristocracy playing in *the streets! It was surely destiny that bid him pause before my door, for though I am too poor to help much, still I can offer some slight pecuniary aid in return for the musical treat he gives me." "And does Mr. Browne come every afternoon ?" asked Nora.

"Yes, dear. You see, it is such a treat to me to hear some really good music again; besides the comfortable feeling of knowing I am helping genius to spread its wings." Nora felt mistrustful of the tenor, in spite of his romantic history, but knew that it was useless to remonstrate with her aunt, who was evidently inktuated with her protege. She had to return to her engagement the next morning, and it was not until a fortnight later that she spent an afternoon with Mr. Browne, though she had seen him two or three times for a few minutes each day before he left. Of course, Ha'rry had been told all about him. "It is a strange tiling," Nora had written, after the second interview. "find shows how careful

, one ought to be betore saying a girl mates up. You know you are always telling me not to have uncharitable thoughts of anyone. Had this tenor been a woman, I should have declared he painted., but a man can have no object in painting, so I must be mistaken."

Harry was an earnest, simple-minded young man, who took life intensely, and was desperately anxious to find good in everything and everybody. Nora, more practical, received numerous lectures on her hardness of heart and want of faith; which lectures, being young and very much in love, she imeived with dutiful submission. But to return to the tenor. On the afternoon on which she enjoyed listening to his fine voice, she was disagreeably impressed by the familiarity of his manner to her aunt, and the nervous, almost coquettish way in which his attentions were received.

"Thank you, Mr. Browne, that is very charm- ing," the girl had remarked when "Love's Young Dream" came to an end.

"Ah ! those days are gone for ever as far as I am concerned," sighed the elder lady. "Nay, dear madam," cried Mr. Browne, bend- ing forward and laying his band on hers. "A woman is never older than she wishes to be. When I see you so rapt in Heaven's divinest gift —music—l say to myself: Behold a kindred soul!' Oh, it is sweet to feel oneself understood!"

Nora began to feel uncomfortable. Had her aunt been younger, she could have declared that Mr. Browne was making love to her; but as it was —surely she must fancy more than his manner implied. Still, she determined to be present as often as possible at the musical afternoons, and try and put a stop to his lover-like speeches. The next few days she was busy teaching, so had no opportunity of making further observations. When at last she was at leisure, she was horrified to find what a perfect understanding appeared to exist between her aunt and the tenor. He spoke constantly of the peace and comfort of home life; how delightful it would be to pass one's days with a congenial companion interested in all one's pur- suits. Miss Sinclair simpered, looked down, and once, at a plainer speech than usual, blushed. About a week after, during an interval of rest for the musician, the conversation turned on wills in general and the will of the late Mr. Sinclair in particular; and Miss Lucinda lamented to the vicar's wife—who happened to have dropped in to tea and muffins—that her highly-esteemed and deeply-lamented papa had left her money so tightly tied up that she could not touch a penny of the capital, but was compelled to live on the interest alone.

Was it a trick of the light, or did a look of baffled rage pass over Mr. Browne's face? Nora was inclined to believe that her eyes had not deceived her; but then Harry had exhorted her only that morning to guard against the uncharitable thoughts she entertained respecting the tenor. After the vicar's wife had left, Miss Sinclair deprecatingly suggested to Mr. Browne that the following week he should come one evening and sing to a select audience of her particular friends. "Excepting the vicar we shall be all ladies—your devoted admirers," she concluded, entreatingly. Mr. Browne, after some hesitation, consented, and promised to bring his violin. in case his chest —which had been very weak lately—should uravent his singing.

Miss Sinclair made elaborate preparations for her concert, and was in a flutter of excitement when the evening drew near. Mr. Browne came punctual to the moment, but was full of apologies. His chest was so weak, he greatly feared he should only be able to sing two songs. Would they there- fore allow him to commence with a few variations on the violin?

They were all enjoying the performance, when the vicar—the only gentleman present—received an urgent summons to a dying parishioner living some distance off.

"It is strange," said M. Dale ruefully. "The man was so much bettez yesterday. This bad turn must have come very suddenly.' However the messenger—a woman much muffled up—represented matters as being so urgent that the kind-hearted old man set off immediately. Soon after he had gone Mr. Browne began to sing "The Pilgrim of Love." His voice was very weak, but he sang with so much expression that his audience was enchanted. Suddenly he stopped, and sank into a chair, gasping for breath and pressing his handkerchief to his lips. The ladies were in con- sternation, and Nora was just going to fetch the doctor's assistant (the doctor himself was away for a few days) when Mr. Browne was heard to murmur an entreaty that no one would trouble about him. It was only one of his usual attacks, for which perfect rest was alone needful. If Miss Sinclair would alit v him to rest quietly on the sofa in the upstaisitting-rocm for an hour, he was sure he could by then return to his lodgings. This appeared so reasonable a request that be was straightway conducted to the sofa, supplied with restoratives in case of his feeling faint, and then left to the absolute repose which he declared the physician he had lately consulted had ordered whenever an attack came on. Then his would-be nurses joined the other ladies in the drawing-room, and, music being out of the question, sat down to a round game, Nora meanwhile helping Sophy pre- pare the light supper, with which Miss Sinclair's little evenings always concluded. They were always the same, these little evenings, thought the girl with a sigh—she and Sophy busy laying the table, the others absorbed in their cards. It was tire- some that the music, which would have made some change, had turned out a failure. Poor Mr. Browne! Perhaps, after all, Harry was right, and she had been hard on him. - .

She was roused from her reflections by the gas suddenly going out. Then came a piercing scream from Sophy, the crash of a dish on the floor, hitrried footsteps, and the banging of a door. Rushing upstairs, she found the hall in darkness. From the drawing-room proceeded a clamour of feminine voices, rising one above another in shrill confusion, mingled with cries of "Nora! Sophy ! What are you doing? What is the matter? The gas has gone out, and the door is locked 1 " "It's robbers, miss!" moaned Sophy's voice in her ear. "One knocked me down and then rushed out with a big bag, and another has locked himself in the dining-room. Don't you hear him moving about? "

"Run for a policeman!" cried Nora, and she hurried back to the kitchen for lights, and then called to her aunt to get out of the window, which was not far from the ground. By the time she had assisted the last of the trembling ladies to alight, Sophy came back with a burly policeman, an especial friend of hers. • "Look! He is trying to get out ! " she cried, pointing to the dining-room window, where a man was seen in the dim light making feeble efforts to escape. He appeared to be hurt in some way, for he made but faint resistance to the policeman's ener- getic handling. "Oh, the wretch! The villain!" cried the ladies in a chorus, as the man was dragged into the hall. The light from the lantern flashed on his face, and showed a wound on the forehead, from which the blood was flowing profusely. Ho lay quite still on the floor, ghastly, and unconscious of all that was passing around him. With a wild cry, Nora flung herself on her knees beside him, and strove to staunch the blood, for in the sup- posed thief she had recognised, to her horror—her lover, Harry Mayne! Now, it so happened that no one present besides herself had over seen Harry. He had only paid one flying visit to Laxton, and at that time Miss Sinclair was suffering from gout in the eyes; so, although she had interviewed, she had not actually seen, Harry. It was in vain, therefore, that poor Nora pleaded and expostulated. No one believed but that the unconscious man was a thief, caught red-handed in the act of escaping from the scene of his guilt. The wound on his forehead, they considered, must have been caused by his en- deavouring to escape through the window, the broken glass of which bore traces of blood. By this time two more policemen had arrived, and, in spite of her entreaties, Harry was lifted into a fly and conveyed to the police-station by Sophy's burly friend, while his colleagues remained to search the house. Just then the vicar arrived, in a great state of mind at having been sent on a fruitless errand. His sick parishioner, far from being at death's door, was considerably better, and had never thought of summoning his spiritual adviser. Evidently the supposed message was a ruse to get him out of the way. "Where is Mr. Browne?" he asked, as they followed the policemen upstairs. Miss Sinclair started. In her agitation she had fdrgotten all about the tenor, and now hurried into the morning-room, half-fearing that the noise and confusion must have brought on another faint- ing _fit.

Mr. Browne had vanished—vanished also was the clock ; but in its place was a letter addressed to Miss Sinclair. With trembling fingers the poor lady. who had by this time been told that every-

EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
THE UNEMPLOYED QUESTION.

Seldom has the winter started under, more unfavourable auspices, and already the ques- tion of the unemployed is looming large on the national horizon. Money is tight, _trade depressed, and in almost every department of commerce there is. a slump. The conse- quence is that already the pro- portion of labourers, both skilled and un- skilled, out of work is abnor- mal for the season of the year, and it is doubtful if the state of the labour market and the promise of distress has been so serious since the winter of 1893. In various London districts t h e

figures are very

depressing-. In West Ham it is estimated that at least ten thousand men are out of work, and in Poplar six thousand, while Stepney, South- wark, and Mile End are in an equally bad way. Altogether it is calculated that in the

ountry some 650,000 men are unemployed, and that by Christmas the number will have risen to 800,000. Fortunately, however, the various authorities concerned are showmt themselves alive to the needs of the situation. Mr. Walter Long, the president of the Local Government Board, has summoned a con- ference of the various Guardians to meet on Friday at the offices of the Board to discuss the position and to offer suggestions, and it is hoped that practical good may be the outcome of this wise step. The Poplar Borough Council have gone still further by drawing up a petition which is to be sent direct to the King begging him to summon a special session of , Pax liament to devise measures for coping with the distress, which would be a step without precedent in its far-reaching effects. Again, the Poor Law Guardians of Islington have invited a joint conference of Borough Councils and Guar- dian Boards to meet next Monday in the Islington Town Hall, and this conference will have the advantage of the proposals discussed at ti e Local Government Board offices on Friday. The repbrt of the Mansion House Committee OR the unemployed, which has been considering the question since last winter, is expected within a few days, and may help to throw light on the situation. The distress is already with us, but the authorities are showing unusual activity, and it is to be hoped that they will be able to devise a practical plan to cqpe with this serious national trouble.

• "*. 140 7/ "..: MR. WALTER LONG.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
THE THANET ELECTION RESULT.

The bye-election ,at Thanet caused by the death of Mr. J. Lowther has ended in the return of Harry H. Marks, the Conservative candidate, by a majority of 382 over the Liberal nominee, Mr. Joseph King. The con- test has been the most exciting in the his- tory of the constituency, chiefly because the victor had to fight against some few members of the Conservative party who were not of opinion that Mr. Marks was a desirable candi- date. Speaking after the declaration of the poll, Mr. Marks thanked the electors for choosing him to represent the division. "The result," he said, "I regard as a triumph, first for the Conservative cause, to which Thanet has been consistently loyal; secondly, I regard it as a triumph for the cause of

MR. JOSEPH KING. MR. HARRY MARKS. Fiscal Reform • and I am happy to think That Thanet will have an important voice in deciding that question; thirdly, I regard our triumph as a vindication of fair and clean methods of conducting political warfare, and a rebuke to the methods of the muck-rake which has been all too freely employed. Elected, I shall consider myself the repre- sentative not of one party, but of all parties in the Isle of Thanet." Mr. King the van- quished was by no means pessimistic as regards the future. Speaking after Mr. Marks, he .said • "I think we have had a very good fight arid I am per- fectly sure that none of us has anything whatever to be ashamed of. I feel myself that I should like a little holiday, and then I don't know what will come after. But of this I am sure, that we must not let the attack we have made on the Conservative citadel be relaxed." Mr. King could give no promise to contest the constituency again.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
PRETTY POLLY BEATEN.

The idol of the moment both of England and France is Pretty Polly, and no one is more proud of her than her owner, Major Eustace Loder, who has given up the army for the fascination of the racecourse. Not only is he her owner, but it was he who bred her at his stud farm in Ireland, and this fact adds to his pride and satis- faction to no sm all degree. Her dam, Ad- miration (by Saraband), was well bred, but useless on a racecourse, and her sire, Galli- nult, was only a second-rater, though a great success at the stud. At the beginning of MAJOR EUSTACE LODEa, last year she was sent over from Ireland to Newmarket, where she waS placed under the charge of Mr. Purcell Gilpin at Clarehaven Lodge, and it was some time before it was suspected how good she was. Her first race was at Sandown Park, and she won with such ridiculous ease—literally "by a street," to use a metaphor of turf slang— that the weighing-room was bombarded with inquiries as to whether it was not a false start. Since then Pretty Polly has won four- teen other races in England, worth 3T,492, and never once has she even been extended. So ridiculously easy have been, all her vic- tories, that no one knows how good she is. Unfortunately she was not entered in the Derby, the Two Thousand Guineas, or the "Ten Thousand Pounders," all of which would have been at her mercy, and enabled

her to establish equine recoicis. The One ' Thousand Guineas, the Oaks, and the St. Leger give her a sort of :aminine "triple crown,' but her non-entry in other races has naturally been a disappointment. So Major Eustace Loder decided to send her over to France to take the measure of the French form in the Prix du Conseil Municipal, and she was sent from Newmarket last Thursday insured for i 40,000. The weather delayed the crossing till Friday, but she took it with her usual placidity and good temper, and arrived at Longchamps well and on her feed. Her form, however, in the race was most dis- appointing, for she was beaten by a horse that cannot have any pretensions to classic quality. Indeed, the winner—a bay colt by Reuil, named Presto ll.—ran in a selling race last year, and was sold for 120. Her defeat was a great blow to the many' British sportsmen who had travelled to Paris spf daily for the race.

PASTOR AND THE WORKING MAIL

The attack made upon the British work- man by the Rev. R. J. Campbell, pastor of the City Temple, High Holborn, led to an extraordinary scene on Sunday morning last outside the above named place of worship. Before the service concluded several hundreds of men gathered

on Holborn Via- duct outside the

building. When

the con grega-

tion had left and

the big doors of the build-

in g were closed the crowd rushed

down the steps

into Farringdo-n Street, and thence to Plum

Tree Court,

where the rear entrance to the

City Temple is

situated. A one

horse brougham guarded by six policemen,

J. CAMPSELT

stood at the docr, and, so soon as Mr. Campoell —who was accompanied by two friends— appeared and stepped into the vehicle, a roar of hooting and booing burst from the crowd. Mr. Campbell's article, it may be mentioned, appears in the "National Review" for the current month and it deals with Sunday observance, and it is only incidentally that he makes the indictment regarding " a con- siderable part of our working class population." which has aroused so much indignation. One part of the article is worthy of quotation, and is as follows "So far from being the day of rest, Sunday is becoming the most boisterous day of the week ; it is the day when .the rich man gives his choicest parties, and the day when the poor man gets drunk in the company of his boon companions. It is the day of social intercourse, from golfing engagements to prayer-book parade. In fact, it is the day of amusement par excellence, and that it is not more so is due less to public sentiment than to the existing state of the law." The protest against the reverend gentleman's remarks are So strong that a demonstration is threatened in Hyde Park or at the City Temple, whilst on the other hand Mr. Campbell has *ought it fit to make a special reference to the subject at a midday service held in mid-week. With much warmth he suggested that the remarks he had made had been misrepresented in the Press, but he declared that he would neither modify nor withdraw any part of the article c cm- plained of.

IRISH REFORM MOVEMENT.

The-principal political subject of the moment is Lord Dunraven's scheme of "Devolution," as an alternative to Home Rule; on every side it is attracting criticism, favourable and hostile. At the recent meeting of the Irish Reform Asso- ciation in Dublin, a report of some recommen- datons were put forward for the consideration of the government and the public. • The main proposition was the formation of a new body, consisting of the Irish representative Peers, the Irish Members of Parliament and the Members of the Financial Council, which should act as a kind of National Council for Ireland, to meet in Dublin and transact certain purely Irish affairs. The body was not to be -called a

separate Legislature, though it was to have certain legislative functions, after the fashion of Grand Committees, the Board of Agriculture and Technical Instruction and the Boards whicla

proceeded by provisional orders. It did n. further define the business to be delegated or the extent and limits of such delegation, con- sidering them matters with which Parliament itself must deal. The object of such a Council, it is claimed, is not to encroach or touch in the sliglAtest degree upon the supremacy of Parlia- ment—merely to accomplish a certain adminis- trative and legislative Devolution. Mr. George Wyndham, in his capacity as Chief Secretary for Ireland, replied at once disowning the proposition officially, and offering criticisms to which Lord Dunraven has in his turn replied. Lord DunraVen looks upon this scheme of devolu- tion as a meeting point for the moderate men of all parties, both Unionists and National- ists, and asks for tolerant consideration of the practical value of the proposal. This, it is certain, will willingly be accorded on all sides to such a practical effort to solve a thorny political problem.
