NEW LAWN TENNIS CHAMPION.

• Last week, Queen's Club, London's famous athletic ground, was the scene of the Covered Court Lawn Tennis Championship, which Droved to be very interesting. In the final the holder. A. W. Gore, was defeated by the French champion, Max Decugis, by three sets to two. The

winner played very steadily in the first set and

won. it by six games to two:

but in the

s•cond and third sets he

distincty went to pieces and

allowed the

holder to win

easily, making blunder after blunder. Then

suddenly M. Decugis seemed

too wake up, smashing an d volleying with MR. M. J. G. RITCHIE, great brilliancy CONQUEROR OF DOHERTY. a n d generally outplaying his

opponent: and, winning the last two sets, he landed the championship for France. However, the sensation ot the tournament did not lie in the final, but in the defeat of H. L. Doherty, the Open Champion of all England, who was beaten for the first time for several years: He had kept his record unspoilt ever since be first won the Open Championship, until it had become the ambition of all tennis- playerF to "beat Doherty and die." Now at last it has fallen to the lot of M. J. G. Ritchie to accomplish the proud feat, though he himself was subsequently knocked out of the tournament. Needless td say, the match was a fine one and eagerly followed by the spectators, who expected an exciting game as Ritchie showed last April in the champion- ship that he was capable of extending Doherty, who only won on that occasen by a supreme effort. On this occasion Ritchie showed him- self determined to force the pace and the score, taking the net and driving his rival to the back line time after time. These ag- gressive tactics won him the first two sets; and the third seemed within his grasp, when Doherty made a . brilliant effort and just got home. Then the champion won the next set in his old style, only losing one game. The final was hotly fought to "four all," Doherty appearing the. more fagged of the two—and then Ritchie made a last effort and won the last two games. It was the crowning moment of his tennis career.

THE NORTHERN UNION.

NORTHERN LEAGUE.

First Division.

This ever-popular fixture was brought off at Warring- ton on Saturday, before 5,000 people and in beautiful weathsr. Runcorn were handicapped by the absence of their regular wings, but found good substitutes in Harrison and Hough. Warrington tried Short on the right in place of Harris. Mr. W. M'Cutcheon of Oldham was referee, and the teams lined up as follows : Warrington—Hallam, back; Fish, Isherwood, Dicken- son, and Short, three-quarter backs; Hockenhall and D. Davies, half backs; Thomas, Shugars, Cook, Jolley, Boardman, Belton, Preston, and Naylor, forwards. Runcorn—S. Houghton, goal; Harrison, Hesketh, Price and Hough, three-quarter backs; Butterworth and J. Richardson, half backs; Walker, Moulton, A. Richardson, Padbury, C. Houghton, Kennedy, Tomlin- sontand Dobson, forwards.. _

Play opened directly in favour of the home men, but by easy stages the visitors cleared out the" wirepullers" and Butterworth and Price were pulled up within a few yards of the line. Warrington tried the passing game and Fish was held up twice at the corner. Strong forward play by Walker and Padbury gave Hallam trouble, and from some wild passing by the home backs Harrison made an opening for Padbury to score. Im- mediately afterwards Warrington pushed to the other end. Belton when a certain scorer was obstructed and from a penalty kick Fish placed a goal, Runcorn lead- ing by three points to two at the interval. Turning round Warrington made strong play at the Runcorn end, but Houghton repeatedly cleared effec- tively. The "wirepullers " would not be denied, and the forwards rushed the scrummages, and Boardman completed an effort in which Preston was the prime mover. Runcorn next had a chance, only to see Richardson lose his head, when practically all opposition had been overcome. Result :—Warrington 1 goal 1 try (5 points), Runcorn 1 try (3 points).

Played at Bradford, the Batley ground being under suspension. Play in the first half was uneventful. Thompson (St. Helens) scored an unimproved try. Half- time: St. Helens, 1 try (3 points) ; Batley, nil. On 'resuming, Batley scored by Davies (two) and Fitzgerald (2), goals being scored by Turton (three) and Oakland (one). Result—Batley, 4 goals 4 tries (20 points) ; St Helens, 2 goals 2 tries (10 points).

WAKEFIELD TRINITY V. LEEDS.

At Wakefield. In the first half Leeds played with the wind, but the only tangible point they were able to score was a dropped goal by Moseley. In the second half, Wakefield attacked continually, but they lacked finish to their play. Time after time they looked like scorers, but the defence was sound. Result :—Leeds, goal (2 points) ; Wakefield, nil.

Cash also registered a try for Oldham. In the second half Salford again had slightly the best of matters, and Heath ran over. Lomas failing. Result—Salford, 2 goals 2 tries (10 points); Oldham, 1 try (3 points).

UP-TO-DATE METHODS

For Purifying and Beautifying the Skin, Scalp, Hair and Hands.

Socrates called beauty a short-lived tyranny, Plato a privilege of nature,. Theocxitus, a delightful prejudice, Theophrastus a silent cheat, Carneades a solitary kingdom, Homer a glorious gift of nature, Ovid a favor of the gods. Aristotle affirmed that beauty was better than all the letters of rec- ommendation in the world, and yet none of these distinguished authorities has left us even a hint of how beauty Is to be perpetuated, or the ravages of age and disease defied. Time soon blends the lily and the rose into the pallor of age, disease dots the fair face with cutaneous disfigurations and crimsons the Roman nose with unsight- ly flushes, moth, if not rust, corrupts the glory of eyes, teeth, and lips yet beautiful by defacing the complexion, and fills the sensitive soul with agony. If such be the unhappy condition of one afflicted with slight skin blemishes, what must be the feelings of those in whom torturing humours have for years run riot, covering the skin with scales and sores and charging the blood with poisonous elements to be- come a part of the system until death? It is in the treatment of torturing, disfiguring humours and affections of the skin, scalp and blood, with loss of hair, that the Cuticura remedies have achieved their greatest success. Orig- inal in composition, scientifically com- pounded, absolutely pure, unchange- able in any climate, always ready, and agreeable to the most delicate and sen- sitive, they present to young and old the most successful curatives of mod- ern times.

He had at first intended making her Mrs. Browne, and sharing her comto..,able little villa; but finding she was unable to touch any of her capital, he thought the plan would not answer, so had made other arrange- amts.

Mr. Dale immediately started ci to secure the release of Harry Mayne, and brought him back to Miss Sinclair's, weak from loss of blood, but strong with indignation at the treatment he had received. He had had an unexpected two days' holiday, and started otT\ immediately to visit his lady-love. Putting up at the Laxton Hotel, he had walked on to Miss Sinclair's, and finding the door ajar, and his ring unheeded—Sophy, busy cner the supper, had not heard it—walked into the hill. Barely was he inside when he was seized in a powerful grasp—Harry himself was far from being a muscular Christian—draggel into a room, and flung violently down. He remembered striking his head against the sharp angle of the sideboard, but knew nothing more until he awoke to conscious- ness at the police-station. The next time Miss Sinclair saw Mr. Browne was some weeks later, when she was called upon to give evidence concerning the robbery at her Louse. He had been arrested for a series of cleverly-executed burglaries, which he and his colleague—the woman who had summoned the vicar to his sick parishioner—had successfully accom- plished. This woman proved to be a former servant of Miss Sinclair's, whom she had dis- charged for dighonesty some months previously; and from her Mr. Browne had evidently learnt all the details which had enabled him to play his part so well. One thing alone appeared to be true of the many stories he used to confide to Miss Sinclair during their confidential chats together— he had really seen better days. She sighed pro- foundly when the sentence of penal servitude for five years was passed upon her former favourite. "Ah, Nora, to think of that exquisite voice being wasted in prison! Perhaps—who knows ?—if he Lad married me he might have been saved—have lived to make his name famous, and delighted the world with his musical genius ? " "But, aunt," cried Nora in dismay, "surely you would never have married him ? "

The Unionist cause UNIONIST does not seem to be EMBARRASSMENTS. multi refreshed by the victory of Mr. HARRY IVIARKS. Says the "Tines ": "The return of Mr. HARRY MARKS as member for Thanet will be regarded by thelnation in the same way as they regarded the return of Dr. KENEALY for Stoke, on no better ground than his advocacy of the cause of the Tichborne claimant." The only objection we can make to this illustration is that it casts an unmerited slur on the memory of Dr. KENEALY, who had the mis- fortune to be out of his mind. Mr. MARKS, on the other hand, is as sane as ARTHUR ORTON always was. But the " Times' rightly adds that the election for Thanet "leaves the reputation of the new member exactly where it stood, and it , may, we fear, do something to weaken the cause which he represents.' Mr. BALFOUR'S part in this unfortunate business scarcely redounds to his credit. "'We believe," says the "Times," "that the PRIME MINISTER would have been advised, and have served his party better, had he openly spoken I out in condemnation ofj the choice made by the local organisations of Thanet. He would, no doubt, have caused much tem- porary irritation amongst them; but he would have satisfied the feeling of the better part of his party throughout the country." Mr. MARKS has explained the hostility, of the " Times " as inspired by malignant jealously of his exceeding virtue. This comfortable theory can scarcely be applied to the "Standard "too. "In the interests of the purity of public life," says that journal, " we hope that Mr. MARKS, before he is allowed to take any active part in the work of Parliament, will be afforded an opportunity by the House of Commons to clear his character of the grave aspersions cast upon it by a Judge of the Supreme Court. That is all we have to say upon the Thanet election." The "Daily Telegraph" is discreetly dumb; and the "Morning Post" re- marks that a good many Unionists are" far from pleased with the result," although it adds the singular reflection that the Thanet electors have put the stamp of popular approval upon Mr. BALFOUR'S scheme for an Imperial Conference. Mr. ASQUITH'S opposition to that device moves the " Standard " to sorrow. Is it possible that the Liberal leaders can be so ill-advised as to hinder an attempt to achieve Imperial unity ? Will Mr. ASQUITH and his colleagues take the line we have repeatedly enforced, that a conference engineered in deference to Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, and by his inspiration, oan do no more good to Imperial unity than Mr. BALFOUR'S policy of Retalia- tion can do to the cause of Free Trade? The " Standard " sees clearly enough that the whole basis of any Conference must be, from the Colonial point of view, the policy of Preference. All the Australian statesmen especially Mr. WAVON, have made this clear. At the same time, Mr. WATSON admits that " the decision with respect to Preferential trade rests primarily with the people of Great Britain. We neither suggest nor desire," he adds, "that they should take any steps in that direction involving any sacrifice of their own material interests." This disposes of MT. CHAMBERLAIN'S lugubrious predictions of a dissolution of the Empire if we offend the Colonies by rejecting his nostrums. They have not the smallest inclination to be offended. They recognise our primary right to study our own material interests; and when the electors of the United Kingdom declare these to be incompatible with the poli ..:37 of preferential trade the Colomie3 will acquiesce in this decision. After that, what useful object will be served by calling a Conference? The " Standard " admits that these are" grave difficulties," and is not "con- fident that they will be ultimately overcome " ; butithinks that all the same we ought to go into a Conference in order to fini " whether any solution is possible of this great Imperial problem." When the British:electors have definitely rejected Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S Fiscal solution of the problem, there will be nothing left that can be profitably talked about.

Though the fiasco of last week deprived the Bewsey electors of an election contest which they looked forward to with relish, the turn of events has resulted in an election this week which can only be described as remarkable. Mr. Dodds was unable to withdraw his nomination in time, and consequently the polling booths had to be opened in order that votes might be recorded for Mr. T. Wright, and Mr. Dodds himself, if the electors chose to ignore the latter's published declaration not to stand. For nearly four hours yesterday the two booths in Bewsey Ward were open, and then as business was very slack the shutters were put no, Vie boxes sealed, and con- veyed to the Town Hall. There was no count un- til after 8 o'clock, which seems a peculiar piece of etiquette seeing that the poll was so summarily closed. But tist after eight the seals were broken and the contents of the boxes disclosed. They were almost like Mother Hubbard's cupboard, but not quite,and three voting papers were found. It was as exciting as if tha number had been 3,000 and amid breathless suspense there was a careful count and Mr. T. Wright was found to be returned by two votes to one, the Labour Party thus gain- ing" a glorious victory." 'Tis not given to many men to secure a two to one majority so easily and we congratulate Councillor Wright on this signal and singular proof of the confidence of his con- stitutents.ll,-lad pi,zl,l3;;;3,:gtakill OEM OE • e e..

During the week there has been a curious development of the Warrington Bridge question. Though the tender for the alterations has already been let, Messrs. James Fairclough and Sons are still striving to protect their own interests from what they consider serious menace. It is rio secret that the firm has opposed the widening scheme from the very iirst,and Alderman James Fairclough has on more than one occasion championed his firm's cause in the Town Council —a course of action which hardly redounds to his credit,seeina that he was returned to the Council to represent the ratepayers. But the Council has been undeterred by threats of an injunction, and Messrs. Fairclough have shifted their ground somewhat. A letter to the Town Clerk written by them on Octcber 10th encloses an offer of £l,OOO towards the extra cost of a suggested alteration to the structure, which would facilitate their carry- ing trade. It is rather late in the day for pro- posals of this kind, and fortunately the corporate mind does not need to trouble itself with the scheme, for Mr. J. J. Webster, the engineer, has already condemned it as impracticable. Alderman Smethurst and other ardent advocates of the widening by cantilevers may therefore go on their way rejoicing.

The Warrington Trades and Labour Council is capable of performing an important and valuable part in the government of an industrial community like Warrington, and as its activity increases so should its usefulness be extended. Much more has been heard of the Council since Councillor Stott undertook the secretarial duties than at any time during its 29 years of existence, and there are now one or two achievements upon which the members can congratulate themselves. In his annual report presented this week Councillor Stott clearly indicates the wide area for legitimate action which is available to the Council. It appears to us that the time is opportune—though we can hardly expect Councillor Stott to take the opportunity—for pointing out to the Trades Council the necessity for keeping a single eye to the interests of labour pure and simple. There has been rather too much ultra-Socialistic and advanced I.L,P propaganda apparent at the more recent meetings of the Coun- cil to the exclusion of considerations which affect more directly the interests of the democracy. Why should not the Trades aLd Labour Council be broader in its constitution than at present ? It seems to us that a body with such a title ought to be capable of finding room for or co-operating with all societies which aim directly at improving the social order and uplifting the wasses, instead of being the servant almost entirely of the Trade Unions, the Independent Labour Party, and the Socialists. Only on such a basis as the one we have indicated can there be any hope of organising the forces of Labour wisely and effectively. Of course the Trades Council is not entirely oblivious of the needs of the worker whether he be a party man, a trade unionist, or a mere unattached toiler, and we are glad to note that the Council thoughtfully suggests a joint con- ference of representatives from the Town Council, the Guardians, the Chamber of Commerce, and its own members in order to consider the vast pro- blem of the unemployed. A hard winter indus- trially is prophesied, and we hope some such joint action of responsible authorities will be practicable in Warrington.

We have not been in entire accord with all that the Rev. H. Dunnico has written in thE columns of the " Examiner " anent the Labour Party, but we do think he has had the better of the argument with regard to the L.R.C. He has proved incon- testibly that the L.R.C. constitution is against the best interests of Labour and, as we have ourselves contended for some time, it is also impracticable. The L.R.C. is ploughing the sands when it attempts to tie progressive men to a policy which ostensibly aims at the furthering of Labour interests and at the same time compels aloofness from all other movements of a utilitarian character which may be sponsored by other political organisations. Then there is the proven charge of inconsistency against the L.R.C. in regard to men who are prominent mem- bers and who have seen the wisdom of co-operating with Liberalism against class interests and retro- gressive forces. A body such as the L.R.C. should be either independent of party altogether—and by party we include the I.L.P. and Socialists —or it should be satisfied with the half-loaf where nothing better offers. That it needs to be free from narrow prejudices is emphasized by a ref 3rence to a manifesto of Mr. Kier Hardie's which Mr. Dunnico quotes this week. The firebrand of the I.L.P. herein boldly plots the destruction of the Liberal party, and con- cludes a sinister proclamation with the remarkable words : "This of course requires .considerabie sacrifice of principle, but the Socialist troubled with scruples of conscience is not worth his salt." Are these the methods by which the sacred cause of Labour is to be advanced? Labour repres.mta- tion is a laudable aim but it will never be attained by unsavoury intrigue and foolish restrictions on the freedom of action of the

Our good friend "The Dawn" this month deliberately shatters all existing political idols and plumps for the Conservative Labour Party, that hybrid production which has lately been endeavour- ing to foist itself upon a democracy already sick unto death of the multiplicity of parties all 21amour- ing for the support of the great British public. In summary fashion "The Dawn" disposes of all other political organizations. rue Labour party "are altogether too one-eyed,' " the Liberals are hop3lesB ' "the Socialists are quite impossible un6il they have discovered the existence of the British Empire," and "the Conservatives "—yes the Conservatives—" are a set of fossils with exaggerated notions of the rights of property, the use of Bishops and the majesty of the Bung." "Save us from our friends ! ' we can imagine the Tories of Warrington saying to themselvas, and at the same time looking somewhat askance at the new off-shoot of Conservatism which is to do everything worth doing in much less than no time. "if it will but:llive up to its name," says "The Dawn "hopefully of its new protigee ! Well if it does there will soon be another sad wreck on the political Goodwins. What breadth of aspiration can there be under the title of "The Conservative Labour Party "? The very name is a ridiculous and blunt contradiction of itself, and if "the Dawn" did not habitually lookc at things through blue-tinted glasses we should marvel more at the hysterical adulation of a party born uuder such incongruous auspices.

I "To educate, to agitate, and to operate" is the three-fold object of the Social Service League which was duly inaugurated in Warrington on Wednesday i evening. When It s added that the principle for which the league stands is the re-adjustment of the present social order, it will be seen at once that the league has set itself a task from which many a stronger organisation would willingly shrink. But there are many brave spirits connected with the newly-formed organisation who think they can do much in the direction indicated, and it would be both unwise and ungenerous to discourage their efforts. There are many local questions—not to speak of national questions—on which a healthy public opinion needs creating, and if the Social Service League succeeds in doing something in this direction it will have justified its existence. Warrington has never been in the forefront, either in the discussion or solution of social questions, but recently there has been an awakening of interest, and the Social Service League intends to keep it alive, and if possible, translate it into action.

A correspondent calls attention in another column to the practice of poor children in picking up and eating scraps of rotten food which the eall- holders in the Market Place throw away as being unfit for sale. It is a sad commentary upon the age in which we live that there are children so hungry and underfed that they will devour rotten fruit and other garbage in order to appease their appetites. Such rubbish does them infinite harm and the suggestion of our corraspondent that the refuse should be carefully collected in bins and kept under lock and key until removed to the

destructors, deserves the consideration of the Markets Committee. Our Medical Officer of Health is doing his utmost to make Warrington a healthy tAvn but such a practice as that alluded to, If carried on to any great extent, must militate against his efforts. Hardly anything can be more calcialated to lead to dysentery and other diseases than the consumption of rotten fruit, and other edibles in a decayed condition. Tie health of these poor mites is often undermined in their early years by the lack of wholesome food an they fail easy victims to disease. We feel sure that the matter needs only mentioning to ensure effective preventive measures being taken by the authorities.

Reckless motorists would do well to give the Warrington district a wide berth. The magistrates seem determined to protect the public fray% drivers who would indulge in the doubtful luxuri of tearing along regardless of the danger to othe.r users of the high road, A flagrant case was dealt with at the Borough Police Court on Monday, for there was evidence that an old luau's life was Inv perilled through a Liverpool chauffeur's reckless- ness. Preparing to board an electric car he w3s dashed into ay a motor going at an excessive rat, and hurled into the airais injuries were seriolls/1° fact the wonder is that the ssquel was not grava' The penalty of 10 and costs imp )sed on the chauffeur did not err on the side of severity bY any means. Another motorist who .vas stated t° have disturbed the peace of Grappenhall driving at the rate of thirty miles an hour 114." also to pay dearly for his disregard of the public safety. It is such cases as these which are apt t° create a general distrust of motorists, and we floPe the magisterial warnings will not go unheeded.

Cairo-street Sunday School Literary and Debating Society.

A meeting of this society was held on Tuesday evening, the Rev. C. Harvey Cook occupying the chair. At the outset, half-a-dozen unsigned letters on subjects chosen by the writers were handed to the chairman by certain members, who had previously undertaken to adopt this mode of expressing their views, and, as each letter was read aloud, its contents were discussed. One ,writer attempted to explain the reason why so many working-men did not attend places of worship. Another questioned the propriety of applying the name of Liberal to a man who sup- ported only his own party. Two of the letters dealt with the charges recently made by the Rev. R. J. Campbell against British working-men, and these led to highly interesting discussions, in the course of which the reverend gentleman's remarks were strongly criticised. The other two subjects dealt with were capital punishment and the Chinese Labour Question.

New Elementary Schools far Cheshire.

The Finance Sub-Committee of the Cheshire County Council has had before them the estimates for elementary education, including the provision of a sum for the purchase of sites and the erection of new schools which it has been decided to build. The following are the schools and their estimated cost : At Cheadle, for 500 children (estimated cost £7,500) ; -at Hollingworth, for 600 children (9,000): at Middle- wich, for 800 children (.12,000); at Helsby, for 200 children (13,000); at Neston, for 250 'children (13,750); at Compstall, for 92 infants, and adoption of old building for 144 children (2,200), at Bred- bury, for 400 children (£6,000) ; transfer by purchase of the school at Compstall, £l,OOO ; transfer by pur- ohase of the school at Minshull Vernon, £450. The estimates for alterations at the following schools are :—Brunner School, Barnton, £4,600; Meadow Bank Council School, Over; £700; Weaverham, •3,00 O Oulton Park, £800; High-street Council School, Over, for 600 children, £3,500. The sub- committee approved the estimates, and instructed the, county accountant to make provision for them.

'Presentation to a Shepherd. At the quarterly meeting of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds(Warrington district)Bro. Lawrence Weir presented P.P.C.S. Bro. Joseph Adams with a handsome marble timepiece, for valuable services rendered to the district during the year 1903-4 as Pro C. Shepherd. The presentation was warmly and -earnestly supported by many brethren. Bro. Adams very feelingly returned thanks. At the same meeting P.P.C.S. Bro. Lawrence Weir was nominated for a seat on the executive committee of the order, and Dis. Sec. Bro. Edgar was nominated for a seat on the Board of Management of the Lancashire and Cheshire Counties Conference. The conference was invited to hold its next meeting in Warrington.

Crosfield's Education Scheme. On Tuesday last, the assembly room in the works of Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Limited presented an animated appearance when over 120 of the boys and girls employed there attended to receive from the firm the school fe.2s paid and the money prizes gained in the past session of evening classes. All these boys and girls had a record of over 85 per cent. of the maximum possible attendances and were therefore entitled to have their fees repaid and in addition, -where the master's or mistress's report was good, to have a money prize eqtial to their fees paid. -No less than 28, or nearly a quarter of the above, missed no classes and each of these received an additional 25.6 d. Altogether, close upon :£6O was distributed: H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING OF TONICS.-6.3, SANKEY STREET, Warrington.---Advt. Civil Service Changes. A Railway Mystery. At the Police Station on Wednesday afternoon, Hr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner, held an inquiry touch- ing the death of Thomas Kale (139),a seaman,of Gee's Yard, Newton-le-Willows—The. evidence showed that deceased left home at 8.39 on Friday evening, stating that hc was going for a walk. Nothing further was seen of him until 6.30 on Saturday morning, when a foreman platelayer named John Wood found him lying on the railway line about 280 yards from Newton Station. He had evidently been struck by a passing train, for his skull and jaw were fractured. He was practically unconscious and could give no account of how he got on the line. He was conveyed to the Warrington Infirmary where he died on Sunday. An open verdict was returned. - -

Smallpox Preferred to Vaccination. At Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday George Frith, Frandly Farm, Seven Oaks, applied for a vaccination exemption certificate in the case of his child. He stated that he believed vaccination to be detrimental to the health of children.—The Clerk (Mr. Henry Greenitll): But you must satisfy the Bench that you have a cluscientious objection.—Applicant : I believe vaccination is detrimental to health.—The Clerk: Why ?—A.p.plicant : It is blood poisoning. I consider my brother is in his grave through vaccin- ation at the age'of 32. He broke out all over scabs after vaccination and had them till he died.—Colonel Ridgway asked the applicant if vaccination had done him any harm.—Applicant: I cannot say that. I would not be done now under any consideration whatever.— The Clerk: And you would sooner your child had smallpox than be vaccinated?—Applicant: I would sooner it have smallpox than be vaccinated.—The application was granted.

SMART FINE ON A SHOWMAN.

Thomas Newton, travelling showman, was charged with showing indecent prints on Widnes fair ground. The police raided the premises, but prisoner was stated to have chewed up the worst of his pictures. which he snatched away from the stand immediately he saw the police. Mr. Knowles, aka defending solicitor, urged that the pictures were no worse than the statues and pictures in public museums and art galleries, and that in the Widnes Technical School there was statuary quite as obscene. The bench imposed a fine of £lO and costs, or two months.—Prisoner said he would go to jail.

THE " SEASON " FOR BURNING PATALITIES.—A WIDNES CASE. Mr. Brighouse (coroner) held an iniuestlon Monday evening on the body of Harriet Pendegrast, aged four, daughter of Patrick Pendegrast, 4, Water-street, Widnes, who died on Friday from shocking burns all over the body, received at the house of her cousin, in Hope- street—Ann Lightfoot, the cousir, said she sent the deceased out to play, while she went upstairs to do some cleaning. Shortly after she heard screams down- stairs,and hurrying to see what was the matter found the child in flames. She was attended to, and afterwards taken to the hospital, where she died the Same day.— The Coroner said this season of the year was just, about the time when burning fatalities commenced. He had spoken about this thing last year, and he would not like to think that the matter was being overlooked. A circular had been sent to all the members of Parliament in Lancashire, asking them ti give the matter eheir earnest consideration, but the legislative wheels moved very slowly. He had suggested that it should be made an offence ,to leave fires unprotected.—A verdict of "Accidental death "was returned.

ST. HELENS

HEALTH OF ST. HELENS.

A very satisfactory feature of the St. Helens medical officer's report for the past fortnight is the exception- ally low death rate from infectious diseases-1.7 against 3.2 There have only been six deaths from infectious disease and twenty-four new cases of sickness, of which i fifteen are from scarlet fever. Typhoid fever s happily rare nowadays. The death rate is 21, against 19.3, and the birth rate has gone up from 37.1 to 12•6.

FIRESIDE''EXPLOSION AT ST. HELENS.

A terrible explosion occurred shortly before ten o'clock on Sunday, at a house in Pecker's Hill-road, Sutton, St. Helens, by which a man, named Edward Grace, was seriously hurt. The explosion took place in the firegrate, and is supposed to have been caused by a dynamite cartridge left in the coal. Shortly after the fire was mended by the addition of a shovelful of ceal the explosion occurred, blowing out the windows and doors of the house, and wrecking all the furniture. Mr. Grace was sitting in front of the fire, and was seriously burnt about the face, arms and chest. Dr. Casey attended to him, and the unfortunate man was subsequently con- veyed to the St. Helens Hospital. YOUNG MAN'S BLACK RECORD. -

John Dings-dairs-,-oi. Bold-street, St. Helens. is a young man, but he has a very bad record, most-of his twenty- one convictions being for larceny and being on enclosed premises for unlawful purposes. At a quarter to two on Thursday morning Police-Constable Smith heard someone in the yard behind 29, Mount-street. He sent Police-Constable Trail to the rear of the pre- mises, and then saw Dingsdale get over the wall and run away. Dingsdale was wearing goloshes, and after a chase he turned round to butt the officer,but he anticipated this and used his baton. The yard in which Dingsdale was found was surrounded by a ten-foot wall, and there were some fowl in the shed. Dingsdale protested that he could not get Work since he was tried at the assizes. —He was sent to gaol for two months' hard labour. RETIREMENT OF THE CHIEF CONSTABLE.

At a meeting of the Watch Committee, on Wednesd Chief Constable Wood, of the borough police force resigned his position, owing to failing health. His retirement was accepted with regret, and he will leave the force at the end of three months on a pension of two-thirds of his present salary, £350. Mr. Wood has had a long and honourable connection with the police service. He joined the Aberdeen City Police in 1863, and in 1868 became associated with the Lancashire County Constabulary, and served in St. Helens, Widnes, and other towns. On the formation of the St. Helens Borough Police Force, in 1887, he was appointed chief constable, and has held that position up to the present time.

OFFICE BREAKING.

A Soldier Caught in the Act at Warrington.

At the Occasional Court yesterday, before Mr. Frost, William Dolan, a private in the South Lancashire Regiment, stationed at Orford B...,rracks, was charged with feloniously breaking and entering the counting house of the Mersey White Lead Co., Limited and steal- inq three pairs of trousers, a jacket, pair of goloshes, pair of socks and two pipes, the property of William Sloan on the 6th of October.

_ Harry Taylor, night watchman at Mersey White Lead Works and residing at 92 Evelyn-street, said he was on duty on the night of October 6th. At 12 o'clock he saw the offices safely locked up. About two o'clock in the morn- ing he noticed a light in the managing director's office. On going there,he found the prisoner crouching in front of a cupboard. He had a poke: in his hand. When asked what he was doing there he replied" I do not know, I want my ticket." He refused to give up the poker, but went downstairs. Witness left him in the yard, and went for a workman named Ernest Stringer. On their return they found prisoner crouching down under the window. On examining the premises he found a window broken and open. A panal of a cupboard was broken and about six locks and handles of cupboards and drawers were broken off. Prisoner was wearing the grey jacket, trousers and the goloshes mentioned in the charge. He had left his military trousers in an adjoining room. Ernest Albert Stringer, labourer, residing in Eliza- beth-street, stated that he was called by the last wit- ness. On searching the yard they found prisoner under the window. Thinking the prisoner was going to strike him with the poker, which he had in his hand, he (wit- ness) struck prisoner on the head twice with a stick. P.C. Wood stated that he was called to the Mersey Lead Works and took the prisoner into custody. He took him to the Police Station and afterwards to the Infirmary, where his injuries were dressed. . Prisoner,who on being cautioned,said "I did it to get my discharge,"was committed for trial at the Liverpool Quarter Sessions.

The boy died an Monday night and the condition of the girl is serious.

Protecting the Children. - The Warrington branch of the National Society for the prevention of cruelty to children, (local office 31 Parker street, Warrington), has during the quarter ended 30th September, 1904, inquired into 55 com- plaints of neglect, ill-treatment and other wrongs of childhood, of which all were dealt with, having been found to be true. 176 children were effected in the cases. There:were 80 offenders; of these 3 were prosecuted • one of whom was convicted 74 were warned, and 2 were dealt with in other ways. The jaenalties inflicted were one month ; of imprisonment 2 of the children died. The society's in- spector made 324 visits of supervision. Mr. W. D. Jeans is the local hon. secretary. The Warrington Branch extends to Earlestown, Newton-le-Willows G-olborne, Penketh and Burtonwood. Inspecto, Hackett will be pleased to receive gifts of clothing tr be distributed to the deserving poor. The approaco of the winter makes the need for a good supply oh garments all the greater.

In October last year 71 Dragoons, the property of Mr. Alex. Wright of Bacup, were sold by auction in the Manchester Coal Exchange and real- ised nearly £930. The famous Belle Vue fireworks are exhibited on Wed- nesday the second day of the Show,a,nd visitors willhave the opportunity of seeing both Show and fireworks on one day. Concert in the Parr Hall.

A deserving object and an exceptionally interesting programme should have attracted a much larger audience than assembled in the Parr Hall on Monday evening for the annual concert in aid of the Railway Benevolent Institution and Railway Orphanage. It must have been disappointing to the promoters to see the array of empty seats in many parts of the hall. Small as it was, the audience was warmly apprecia- tive and there was a consensus of opinion that the concert was thoroughly enjoyable and merited more generous patronage. The artistes are all well known in the musical world, and have appeared at many leading concerts. Madame Eleanor Jones (soprano) is possessed of a voice of pleas ing sweetness and compass and her interpretation of Bishop's" Lo! hear the gentle lark "was artistic and called forth hearty plaudits. The s 'ccess of this item was enhanced by the beautiful flute obligato in which Mr. Eli Hudson kave a foretaste of his striking skill in the manipulation of the instrument. Later Madame Jones was responsible for a feeling render- ing of "She wandered down the mountain side." (Clay). Miss Florence bole (contralto) submitted Dr. Elgar's " Land of Hope and Glory "and Barrat's "My Ships" dreditably, but it was in the latter song that she was heard to the best advantage. Her voice is one of good range and flexibility,and the pleasure of the audience was evidenced by a recall. Mr. Alexander Webster (tenor), favourably impressed the assembly by his interpretation of "I'll sing thee songs of Araby (Clay) and "Eileen Mavourneen " (Benedict), which I were. marked by taste and expression. Mr. Emlyn Davies's rob ust tenor voice had full scope in "The veteran's song" which he gave with fitting verve, but his second effort "The bugler" (Pinsuti) proved the more popular and an encore was accorded. The voices of the party blended well in the quartettes "Brightly dawns our wedding day" and "Good- night, good-night, beloved" and the duets "Tenor and Baritone" (Lane Wilson) by Messrs. Alexander Webster and Emlyn Davies, and "The sailor sighs" (Balfe) by Miss Florence Hoole and Mr. Alexander Webster added to the evening's enjoyment. The artiste who found the greatest favour was Mr. Eli Hudson, solo flautist and piccoloist. He displayed remarkable command over the instruments, and the manner in which the many intricacies were surmounted gave the listeners mani- fest pleasure. Mr. findson received quite an ovation, and the demand for" mnre " was loud and persistent. The whistling solos by Mr. Charles Capper were very acceptable, and did the artiste infinite credit. Mr. Caraclog Evans efficiently discharged the duties e f ccompanist.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 15, 1904.

SAD BURNING FATALITY AT STOCKTON HEATH.

A distressing burning fatality occurred at Stockton Heath on Sunday, the victim being Miss Lousia Brew, aged 34, of 48, Old London-road. The circumstances were detailed at the inquest which was conducted at the Police Station, Warrington, on Wednesday by Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner.-41r5. Deane, deceased's sister, who resided with her, stated that at 7 o'clock on Sunday morning deceased got up to light the fire. A quarter of an hour later, witne.s heard a scream and on going downstairs saw deceased in the yard with her clothing on fire. With assistance she extinguished the flames and Dr. Parker was sent for. He ordered deceased's removal to the Infirmary and she died in the institution at 8.30 the same day from burns about the arms and body. Deceased told witness that she was patting the kettle on the fire when her dress got ignited.—A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FUNERAL.

Amid many manifestations of regret, the remains of Miss Brew were interred in St. Thomas's Churchyard, Stockton Heath, on Thursday afternoon. Miss Brew had been associated with St. Thomas's Church and Sunday school since childhood and for a number of years had faithfully discharged the duties of teacher in the Sunday school. The funeral service was held in the church and a large number of residents and friends assembled to pay a last tribute of respect. The choir was in attendance as well as the Sunday school and day school teachers and the members of Miss Brew's Sunday school class. The coffin, which was covered with beautiful wreaths, was carried from the house to the church, where it was met at the gates by the clergy and choir. The officiating clergymen were the Rev. H. V. Pigot (vicar) and the Rev. C. M. Evans (curate). The bearers were Messrs. J. Langley, W. Mclntyre, E. Cannell and F. Warbur- ton. The organist (Hr. Rose) played several appro- priate voluntaries on the organ prior to the service, and at the close the Dead March in " Saul." The lessons were read by the clergy and the congregation joinedin singing the hymn " On the Resurrection Morn" and the 90th Psalm. At the graveside the choir sang "Thy will be done." The grave had been lined with ivy and chrysanthemums by a sympathetic friend as a last token of regard. The relatives present included Mr. Arthur Dean (nephew), Mr. Watkins (uncle), and Ur. Hindley, of Appleton. Many beautiful floral tributes had been received including one each from the Sunday school scholars, the day :chool scholars, the teachers, ani the members of her Class.

THE BYE-ELECTION IN BEWSEY WARD.

The Labour Candidate has a Majority

of one.

Yesterday, an election took place in Bewsey Ward to fill the vacancy on the Town Council caused by the retirement of Mr. Samuel Gough. It will be remem- bered that on Friday last, Mr. Henry Roberts, J.P. withdrew from the contest. Mr. James Dodds, who had retired from the representation of Town Hall Ward in order to challenge the candidature of Mr. Roberts, then issued an address to the electors of the ward stating that the reason which prompted him to seek their suffrages having been removed by the with- drawal from the contest of Mr. Henry Roberts, he had decided to retire in favour of Mr. Thomas Wright, the labour candidate. Mr. Dodds was too late to hand in the official withdrawal paper within the stipulated time and the polling stations had to be opened yesterday morning and the usual procedure gone through as though an election were taking place. There were two polling stations, one at Hoyle street and the other at Dannett street. Mr. Thomas H. Spinks was the presiding officer in charge of the Hoyle street station with Mr. H. B. Potter, the poll-clerk, whilst Mr. A. Starkey was the presiding officer and Mr. S. Wright the poll-clerk at Dannett street. Only three votes were recorded, but it was neceskary that one hour should elapse without a vote being re- corded ere the polling stations could be closed. The stations opened at 8 o'clock and did not close until shortly before twelve. The boxes were then taken to the Town Elall, where they remained unopened until 8 o'clock. When the votes were counted it was found that the result was as follows :

Thomas Wright ., James Dodds ___

liajority for Mr. Wrig'it.

The result was announced from the Town Hall steps by Alderman Fairclough, the presiding officer of the ward. A small knot of spectators had gathered to hear the result, which was received with a slight ironical cheer.

THE WIDENING OF WARRINGTON BRIDGE

Offer by Massrs. Fairclough and Sons.

We have received a copy of correspondence which has passed between Messrs. James Fairclough and Sons Limited and the Town Clerk with reference to the pro- posed widening of Warrington Bridge. Mersey Mills, Warrington, October 10th, 1904.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
Re WARRINGTON BRIDGE.

Dear Sir,--Xlessrs. Alex. Wilson and Cowie have communicated to us your letter of the sth inst., and we have also seen the account in the newspapers of the proceedings of your Council. We do think that our Appeal deserved more serious and courteous treatment than it has received at any rate at the hands of some of the Councillors, who appeared to think that the matter is of such small importance that we may be left to protect ourselves. On the contrary, it is of very great importance to us. Although we have the greatest possible dislike to throw any obstacle in the way of an improvement to Warrington, we feel very strongly that this improve- ment should not be made in such a way as to damage us most seriously when there are other methods by which it could be carried out.

We now make the following formal proposal :—lf your Corporation will take out the pier to the Lanca- shire side of the central arch (which we understand could be done and the bridge carried across between the other two piers on steel girders without reducing the head room) we would be willing to make a substantial contribution to the additional cost of so doing. We do not suppose this additional cost would be very serious, and we are prepared to contribute £l,OOO towards it.

If this were done we should withdraw any opposition to widening the bridge in accordance with the resolution of your Council.

We beg that you will bring this letter before the Council as soon as possible, and before any steps are taken to carrry out the proposed work. Awaiting your reply, We are, dear sir, Yours traly, JAMES FAIRCLOUGH & SONS, LTD. RICHARD T. FAIRCLOUGH, Managing Director.

"Che Town Clerk, Warrington. 39, Victoria-street, Westminster, S.W. 13th October, 1904.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON BRIDGE.

Dear sir,—Adverting to your letter of the 11th inst. with copy of letter frorn Messrs. James Fair°lough and Sons, I have carefully considered the proposals in the letter for altering the structure and may say, at the outset, that I do not consider theta to be practicable. The three arches of the bridge are supported by two piers in the river and two abutments on the banks; if the pier on the Lancashire side were removed as sug- gested, the remaining pier would be converted into an abutment which would have to take the thr ist of the remaining arch. As this pier is not designed as an abutment,and the stones would be interfered with when removing the other arches, there would be a greater danger of the arch failing. The proposed girders would also present difficulties and the new floor could not be laid until the old structure was removed. thus completely closing the traffic for several months.

The cost of these proposed works would be nearly as much as building a new bridge of the same width, and as utility is not the only point to be considered, I cannot imagine the Council sanctioning the erection of such an unsightly combination. I may mention that my estimate of £17,789 10s. for ft new bridge—which has been magnified in various dis- cussions to from £20,000 to £3o,ooo—was a liberal one, and would certainly not be exceeded, and as competi- tion in the iron trade is now so keen, it would no doubt be built for considerably less.

Yours faithfully, JOHN J. WEBSTER.

J. Lyon Whittle, Esq., Town Clerk, Warrington.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TULIP AND ITS CULTIVATION. . An Interesting Paper at Stockton Heath.

At a meeting of the Stockton Heath Garden Society on Tuesday evening,Mr. C. J. W. Needham submitted an interesting paper on "The Tulip." He pointed out that the tulip probably came from Asia originally. They read of it growing in Persia and flourishing in the Himalayas. It must have been common with the Chinese and Japanese for they found it figured on their old vases and appearing in their pittures. In 1559 it was already a florist's finer with numerous varieties owing their unknown origin to the Turks, the name tulip being the English equivalent iaf the turban— Tolipend (turban) which it no doubt resembled. They learnt that it was in cultivation in Vienna in 1551, and quickly .becoming popular it was introduced into England in 1577. After tracing the growth of varieties of the tulip Mr. Needham touched upon the threadbare story of the curious form of tulipomania—the gambling which took place in Holland when single tulip bulbs were exchanged for houses and land. This gambling found no imitation in England. Cromwell about that time doubtless found men other thoughts to fill their minds. After 1840 the men of the north, for the most part workingmen, chiefly had sway as raisers of the plant, though for some years a strife was waged between southern and northern florists as to the stand- 4rd of excellence of the flower. One of the great Protagonists in the strife and also an authority on the flower as well as one of its most successful exhibitors, was Dr. G. W. Hardy, of Warrington. Dealing with cultivation, Mr. Needham said the tulip was one of the easiest plants to grow in the garden, town or country. It would exist almost in any situation and in any soil, though in order to bring it to perfection, as in the case of any other flower, some little care and attention were needed. A May flower, and coming with the apple blossom, it was well to take advantage of any shelter the garden afforded, especially from the cutting north And east winds. The perlshers that came from the east were quite as harmful to the tulip as to any other plant or shrub. Tulips might be used for decorative purposes with telling effect, by planting in clumps and masses in the ordinary way, the bulbs being laid about two inches apart, and three or four inches deep. As the price of English tulip bulbs of ordinary varieties was now about the same as of good Dutch sorts, there was no reason why greater use should not be made of them in gardens where bulbs were grown. Mr. Needham gave a number of useful hints,and pointed out that if the bloom was intended for exhibition purposes, or it was desired to see it at its best, some pro- tection must be afforded about the time the bloom was beginning to unfold itself, or as soon as the green buds appeared tb be tinged with colour, an awning of thin calico or tiffany should be placed over the bed, six or eight inches higher than the tallest stem. Perhaps the best plan for the tulip enthusiast to adopt was to completely cover the tulip bed with garden lights or frames. But these precau- tions need only be observed by the enthusiastic tulip- pian bent on obtaining a place of honour in some floral competition, or some zealous florist engaged in raising fine seedlings. Left alone to brave all the inclement weather that might come, the tulip would bloom, and bloom beautifully, but perhaps not without speck or spot op its petals. The culture of the tulip was extremely simple, and the veriest tyro in garden culture might, with the exercise of a little care and perseverance, be very successful : 4 yet to the enthusiastic grower the field of knowledge was con- tinually expanding, and the pleasures to be derived from its study and culture boundless.

SOCIAL SERVICE LEAGUE.

Inaugural Muting at th 3 Parr Hall.

REV. H. DUNNICO AND THE AIMS OF THE

LEAGUE.

Warrington has now a Social Service League. This organisation was duly formed at an inaugural meeting held in the Parr Hai on Wednesday evening, when the constitution was adopted and the officers elected. The Rev. H. Dunnico presided over an encouraginc, attend- ance and amongst those present were: Messrs. E. Osborne, N. Cook, G. Plumpton, W. Clatworthy, E. W. Jones,R. Osborne,J.A. Heywood,ll. E. Hawthorne, Joyn- son Pepper, Wilcox, and J. Morton (secretary pro tem.) Circulars were distributed setting forth the aims and objects of the league. The league is affiliated with the British Institute of Social Service, and the principle for which the league stands is the re-adjustment of the present social order. The league consists of all persons irrespective of creed or politics who are in sympathy with its expressed objects, and who shall contribute one shilling per annum to its f unds.Theaims and objectsare:— (1) To EDUCATE (a) by public lectures; (b) by informol conferences ; (c) by the publication of a monthly organ; (d) by the circulation of suitable literature. (2) To AGITATE: Whenever any reform is judged to be feasible, the League shall rouse public opinion by meetings, resolutions, deputations to persons in ' authority, and by such other methods as the committee may determine. (3) To OPERATE: The League shall ' seek to secure the nomination and return of such candidates, irrespective of party, whose views are in harmony with its avowed objects. The League shall see that the powers with which parliament has invested our municipalities for the preservation of the moral and physical health of the people shall be enforced, and shall seek to remove from their high places those who convert public trusts to private uses. It is understood that the League shall Bruit its operations to municipal affairs, and that, for a time at least, it shall play a purely educational part in national politics. Upon questions affecting the general well-being of the corn- munity, it hopes to provide a common platform for the free expression of opinion by representative men of all parties, and of every shade of progressive opinion. The CHAIRMAN,iII opening the meeting, said that at a conference held at Parr Hall three or four months ago a committee was appointed to draw up a constitution and to discuss the formation of a social brotherhood. ' The committee was requested to report to a future meeting.

THE SECRETARY'S REPORT. The SECRETARY, in his report, stated that at a public conference on the social problem held in Parr Hall on Sunday, May 29th, the following were appointed a com- mittee to consider the formation of a society whose aims should be the betterment of social conditions: The Rev. H. Dunnico, Councillor Stott, Messrs. E. L. Jones, J. Elwell, W. Nightingale, E. England J. G. Reeves, J. Edwardson, E. Osborne and J. Morton. Councillor Stott and Messrs. Reeves and Edwardson had taken no part, in the proceedings of the committee. It had been the aim of the committee to devise such an organisation as might be kept clear of all political parties and of all religious sects, offering a common base for all sections of the community. It was clearly recognised that there was a real need in the town for an organisation affording facilities for educat- ing the people on the many phases of the social problem and that the "educational" must be one of the foremost features of the new organisation. The scope offered by the constitution was a aide one and it was hoped that the league would enlist the co-operation of all shades of opinion. An advantage of the league. it was be- lieved, lay in the fact that it would take up many social q.uestions on non-party lines, that whereas an organisa- tion dealing with one item of reform—say the drink question or gambling—was, through lack of interest, more often than not in a state of unpreparedness just at a time when it should be ready to act. The many interests of the Social Service League, would, it was hoped, easure its always being in a state of efficiency. The CHAIRMAN said they all knew his sympathies were with labour. He was practically a son of labour and as a lad many a council of war had been held round his father's fireside in which 'Mr. Sam Woods and others took part. He made up his mind when entering upon the public ministry that he would take an active part in all things affecting the welfare of the community Since commencing his ministerial work he had tried to do so. That had involved extra work and also brought n a large amount of criticism, but he w onld rather lay himself open to the charge of being an agitating parson than to lay himself open to the charge of being apathetic and indifferent upon those questions which affected the wel- fare of the working clisses. TiVi years ago on cooling to Warrington he purposely held himself aloof from political associations. The non-trading moventent had thrown both of the larger political organisations into a state of confusion and practically chaos. There was a possibility also of a labour party bringing out a candidate, so he held him- self aloof purposely to see what developments would take place. Two years passed away and the labour party seemed no nearer obtaining a candidate than at the outset or his ministry. He became more and more con- vinced that the labour party was a party of one idea— and no matter how good that one idea might be, they were bound to recognise that there were other interests in the world besides those of the labour party, which to be a really progressive party must widen its outlook i and include n its anks progressive men of all parties and opinions who were united upon one comqion plat- form of obtaining the greatest good of the greatept number. The idea suggested itself to him of forming a league which should include Liberals, Conser- vatives and Socialists or anybody else who had the welfare of the working-classes and of the com- munity at heart. He desired that the Social Service League should be independent absolutely of either political party, but at the same time the league re- served to itself the right of co-operating with any party which would enable it to further the interests it had at heart. He thought it would be able to do effective work and take a very active part in local politics. One great feature of the league would be educational work, which to him was the most important work of all. It was proposed to hold a series of lectures. A lecture was to be delivered by Mr. Meakin and Ur. John Burns, M.P., had promised to pay a visit. They also hoped to hold a weekly debating society and they were trying to obtain the services of me a of all classes and politics. They further hoped to have a monthly magazinewhick would contain articles on the various aspects of the social problem. It was also hoped to distribute literature and he was hoping to establish a library for young men —a library that would contain standard works on sociology and other subjects. It was not so much the aims of the league to agitate for new laws but to put in force the laws which already existed. There were many laws upon the statute book which were lying dormant simply because their local councils would not or dared not put them into operation and these would tend to relieve them of many things which were objec- tionable. The Chairman invited opinions from those present. Mr. COOK thought the league would help them materially to deal with local matters. One question which they should take into consideration was the education question. (Hear, hear.) Mr. H. E. HAWTHORNE said.the objects of the league seemed to him to be vary abstract. Were the present laws to be altered?

The CHAIRMAN said the only answer was that some of them believed that the present social order or the present condition of society did not Allow a man the full opportunity to develop his manhood, and they desired co bring into existence a social order which should give every man an opportunity of being a man. (Hear, hear.) Mr. 11. E. HAWTHORNE did not think the constitu- tion gave the lines upon which that was going to be carried out. Mr. E. OSBORNE said he took a keen interest in social problems and he was s3,tisfied that if any good work was to be done it would be done—not by the action of the Conservative or Liberal parties—but by such an organisation as the Social Service League collaborating or helping either of those parties to carry out social reform. His experience of political parties was that they were narrow,and they required a social service league to give them a much. needed stimulus. Labour questions were not the only ones that needed consideration. If they considered the interests of labour only they were not doing the best for everybody. The object of the league was to help forward every good work in the town. (Flear,hear). It was not in antagonism to any other social organisation. Alluding to the unemployed qnes- tion, the speaker said the Government ought to be compelled to do something to find work for the unemployed. The local municipality should also be asked to take the matter up. There were many small matters in the town which needed attention and if the league was in earnest he believed a better state of things could be brought about. (Hear, hear). Mr. TROW condemned the administration of the poor laws and thought they ought to have men on the Board of Guardians" who knew what it was to go without a dinner." , Mr. R. OSBORNE referred to the administration of the Education Act in Warrington, alluding specially to the exacting of school fees. The Tories, he said, had passed

a Free Education Act, but the workingmen of Warring- ton did not take advantage of it. The fees in Warrington ranged from a penny to sixpence. The CHAIRMAN said there ought to be na differenc in the fees charged. Education ought to be free a round.

On the proposition of Mr. Mow, seconded by Mr. R. OSBORNE, the constitution, as drawn up, was agreed to.

ELECTION OF OFFICERS. Mr. MORTON moved that thB Rev. H. Darmico be the president of the league. Mr. Dunnico, he said, .was man who could take his ground, and did not keep in the narrow or exclusive paths that some ministers kept. Mr. N. Cook seconded, and Mr. E. OSBORNE and Mr, H. E. HA.WTHOR.NE supported. The proposition was carried unanimously. The CHArrtatax, in acknowledging his election, said he was not going to say he was surprised. He would have been disappointed if they had not elected him to the position,for he believed that at the present juncture no man could fill the position so well as himself. He did not think anyone was so enthusiastic in the work as he was. At the same time he asked them to give him their loyal support. He intended 'during the next 12 months to visit every workshop in Warrington and appeal for members during the dinner-time. If the league was to be of use they must make it strong. The aims and objects might seem somewhat vague, but they ' did not want to aim at the stars. They were expecting to deal with local questions as they cropped up and make the League effective. Mr. J. MORTON was elected secretary and Mr. E. Osborne, financial secretary. The following were. appointed on the committee: Messrs. E. Lloyd Jones, J. Elwell, J. Heywood, Nightingale, H. E. Hawthorne,. E. W. Jones, R. Osborne, Speakman, J. W,ilkß-, Wilcox, Joynson, Trow, Duttonand Pepper. The appointment of vice-presidents was left to a future meeting. A discussion ensued as to whether the league should. seek to be 4epresented on the Labour Representation Committee. It was decided to defer the matter and that in the inpantime, a debate be held sia the sabie3t.

THE NOVEMBER ELECTIONS. This Week's Developments.

Further. developments have transpired this week in connection with the forthcoming November elections. Several ward meeting have been held and details appear below. At the meetings of the Trades and Labour Council on Tuesday evening, it was announced that it was the intention of the Labour Representation Com- mittee to bring forward candidates, but ths names were Hot divulged. Mr. Oswald Buckley, an enthusiastic) member of the Trades Council and Labour Representa- tion Committee, has declined an invitation to contest a ward. Councillor Brough will again be the Liberal candidate for Whitecross Ward. and the Liberal Committee of St. John's Ward after going very carefully into the question of Councillor Thornton's position with regard to the Conservative party have decided to offer no opposition to his return. It is stated that the Conservatives intend to bring for- ward a candidate in opposition to Councillor Thornton. The Conservatives will not place a candidate in the field in St. Austin's Ward, where Councillor Burt )n has again been selected to champion the Liberal cause. At Conservative meetings in Fairfield and Litchford Wards no choice of candidates was made. THE NON-TRADERS AND THE ELECTIONS.

A meeting of the Committee of the Non-Tradin; Association was held on Wednesday evening, when . was decided that the vacancies in Orford, Bewsey, and Latchford Wards should be contested. This means that Councillors Wilkinson Rogers,and Ellison may have to fight candidates wtio, if not official Non-Traders possess Non-Trading sympathies. The names of the prospective candidates have not yet been divulged.

FAIRFIELD WARD.

The Conservative Committee 'met on Tuesday evening under the presidency of Alderman Hutchinson. It was reported that a deputation had waited upon a certain gentleman to ask him to contest the ward, but he had declined the invitation.

Farther action was postponed. __ LATCHFORD WARD. _ _

Mr. G. PILLING presided at a meeting of the Con- servative Committee of this Ward on Wednesday even- ing. It was decided to leave the question of contesting the ward in the hands of a small sub-committee.

ST. JOHN'S WARD. COUNCILLOR THORNTON'S POSITION."--" NOT A CONSERVATIVE AND NEVER INTENDS TO BE." An adjcurned meeting of St. John's Ward iberal Committee, was held on Monday night in Brick-street School Mission Room, Mr. W. Furness presiding over one of the largest ward committees ever held in St. John's. In the course of his opening remarks, Mr. Furness said he thanked the committee and the electors of St. John's Ward for the honour they desired to con- fer upon him by nominating him as a candidate for their ward in opposition to Councillor Thornton. He was induced to consider the offer under the impression that, as stated by several members of this committee, Councillor Thornton had joined the Conservative ranks. To make quite sure of Mr. Thornton's position, he had had an interview with him; and asked him if he was a Conservative, and if he was •coming out under the auspices of the Conservative Association. He - (Mr. Furness) added that if that was so, as chairman of this ward committee he should deem it his duty to oppose him. Councillor Thornton, in reply, said : "He was not a Conservative, never had been, and never in- tended to be." Under these conditions he thought it would be very inconsistent with the policy of the com- mittee to offer any opposition to Councillor Thornton. As they were aware, the committee was organised with the object of supporting Liberalism in the ward and tho town generally, and • was broad-minded and liberal enough to give any gentleman who had its confidence credit for having the best interest of the town at heart. They did not ask him who or what section he followed when in the Council, but he must at least be a Liberal before they could utilize their organization in his interest.

After a long discussion it was decided to ask Coun- cillor Thornton to meet the committee on Wednesday night. A further meeting was held in the Brick-street Mission on Wednesday, Mr. William Furness in the chair. There was again a very good attendance. The chairman, in opening the meeting, explained that they were assembled to meet Mr. Thornton and give him a full opportunity, to explain his position in connection with the Con- servative Association, any member having a perfect right to ask Mr. Thornton any question relating to his political life either municipally or parliamentarily, and in order to give the meeting every opportunity he would at once throw the matter open. A number of questions were asked Mr. Thornton as to his presence at the Walton Park garden party and his general attitude towards the junior and senior Con- servative Associations.

Mr. Thornton, in reply, said he had consented to come before that meeting to fully explain his position, a course that had never in his knowledge been taken in connection with any ward in Warrington before. He thanked the gentlemen for the straight and perhaps personal questions they had put to him, and to his utmost ability he would give them honest, straight- forward replies. He reiterated the statement already published that he "was not a Conservative, never had been, and never intended to be," and furthermore he " could not possibly be a Conservative under any con- siderations?. Whatever the decision of that meeting, he would still be as staunch a Liberal as any six gentle- men they couldname from that committee. He further stated that he was not aware that he was, a member of either the junior or senior Conservative Association, and if his name did appear on their books it was without his consent or knowledge. After a very searching and lengthy. discussion, Mr. Thornton retired at the request of the Chairman. On Mr. Thornton's withdrawal the meeting resumed the discussion, and it was moved, seconded, and carried with one dissentient that f‘ After hearing the explana- tions given by Mr. Thornton, this committee decide not to oppose him at the forthcominr, election." Mr. Furness explained that if further developments warranted it, he would at once call the committee together to consider the question. The Chairman thanked the committee for their attendance and for the searchine way they had gone into the matter. In view of the general election, it was necessary to find out as far as nossible who were their friends and he hoped they would all sink their personal feelings at the great fight, and go in for a grand Liberal victory.

THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

It is seldom that any discovery of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of the age, while they have proved directly beneficial to thoti.sands, have also, at least for a time, injured other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations, however much they may do to remove one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or love it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they may injure another. Now the great need of the age is some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the pre- valent diseases of this country, which will be certain to do good when fairly tried, which will be equally adopted to the needs of the merchant and the workman, the professional man, and he who wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the student, the clerk, the factory hand, the miner, and the roadside labourer. It should be, too, such a pre paration as contains no injurious ingredients, and which may be taken with impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady, as well as by the stronger constituted man. Such a discovery would deserve to be called The Perfection of Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved, it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilyin' Evans' Qainine Bitters, The Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried, has been always recommended, and has proved successful when all other medicine has failed to give relief, and we may say further, that it has proved per- manently beneficial, when other preparations at best gave only temporary relief. It is strongly recommended as The Best Remedy of the Age for Indigestion in its different forms, such as Sick Headache, Pains in the Side, Giddiness, Loss of Appetite, also for Nervousness and Nervous Disorders, Sleeplessness, Neuralgia, Low Spirits, and all kinds of Weakness. It has often proved very beneficial to persons suffering from great Weakness, either after an illness, long con finement to ill-ventilated rooms or any othe cause. IN strikes at the source of the Disease, removing the cause of the illness, and strengthens those parts of the system which have been weakened by it, and therefore most liable to colds and other ailments. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters being purely vegetable, it is a Natural Tonic, and suited to all ages at all seasons of the year. It is everywhere acknowledged to be a powerful aid in restoring the body to a healthy state when deranged by disease. If you suspect that your health is beginning to fail, brace up your nerves, and fortify your constitution by taking now a course of this excellent Tonic, which is sold everywhere in bottles 2s. 9d. and 4s. 6d. each, but should any difficulty be experienced in procuring it, the Proprietors will forward same for the above prices, carriage free. Avoid Imitations. When purchasing,see the name, " Gvrilym Evans," on Label, Stamp, and Bottle. Sole Proprietors: Quinine Bitters Manufac- ming Company, Limited, Llanelly, South Wales.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

THE Suspicious CONDUCT IN ORFORD-LANE.

At the Warrington Borough Police- Court on Monday before the Mayor and other magistrates, Patrick Brady, a labourer, described as last from Prescot, appeared on remand charged with being a person of suspicious char- acter, and found on the enclosed premises of Mr. Edward Gibson, Orford-lane, for an unlawful purpose. —The prisoner was remanded on the previous Monday in order that his antecedents might be inquired into. It will be remembered that prisoner was seen by Mr. Gibson to approach the front window and try the fastenings. Sergeant Smith came on the scene and took him into custody. On the way to the police station he assaulted the sergeant —The Chief Constable now stated that the Irish police knew the prisoner very well and gave him a very bad character.—Mr. Gibson was called and said the prisoner shammed being drunk when the police came on the scene.—The magistrates sentenced the prisoner to six months' imprisonment. AN UNFIINCED FLY-WIIREL.—The St. Helens Cable Company, Bank Quay, were summoned for committing a breach of the Factory and Workshops Act by neglect- ing to securely fence the flv-wheel and other parts of an engine.—Mr. A. Lewis, Chief Inspector of Factories for the district, prosecuted, and Mr. Edwin Peace, of Liverpool, defended. Mr. Lewis stated that on January 21st. Mr. Eatock, factory inspector, visited defendants works during the night, and was conducted over the premises by a foreman. He found amongst other pieces of machinery that were unfenced the engine mentioned in the summons. He called the foreman's attention to the engine and on January 25th, sent a circular to the firm pointing out the requirements of the Act and call- ing upon them to securely fence certain machinery, in- cluding the following wheel, crank itc., of the small engine in question.—Mr. Peace said he did not want to avoid any facts going to the bench, but the firm had no knowledge of the receipt of the circular—Continuing, Mr. Lewis stated that on January 29th, Mr. Eatock again inspected the factory and was accompanied by the manager. He called attention to the engine and left under the impression that the work would be done. When he paid a visit on March 3rd, along with himself (Mr. ILewis) the engine was still unfenced. It was stated that there had been changes at the works and a promise was given that the engine should be attended to. On September 9th they again went to the factory and to their surprise found the engine was unfenced. Having regard to the caution received by the firm, the promises made to carry out their obligations, and the serious nature of the neglect, the case was one that should be considered as very serious and not as a trivial or techni- cal offence.—Mr. Eatock gave evidence in support of this statement.—For the defence Mr. Place said it hail never been the firm's intention to avoid their obliga- tions under the Act. They had done work which amounted to many pounds in order to satisfy the inspector's requirements, and the fencing of the engine was a " flea bite" amongst it. In March there were changes in the management, and his instructions were that the matter was not brought before the firm until September 9th. They did not receive the circular on January 25th and in a second letter from the inspec- tor the engine was not specified. Immediately after the visit on September 9th the work was put in hand. Had it been within the knowledge of the company or of those responsible for the working of the firm that the inspector required the engine to be fenced, the matter would have received attention before.—Mr. Makinson, works maintenance manager, having given evidence, a fine of 20s. and costs (£1 lls.) was inflicted

ATTEMPTED SUICIDE.

An elderly man named Hugh Cowap was charged at the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday with having on the 9th October attempted to commit suicide by stabbing himself with a knife.—Thomas Barber, farm labourer, Dutton, said that on Sunday morning, October 9th, in consequence of something his little boy said to him he went into a field at Bartington, and there saw the prisoner lying face downwards in a ditch. He called to him but there was no answer. A man named William • Savage assisted him to lift the prisoser out of the ditch, and a policeman was sent for.—P.C. Charles Nicholls, stationed at Whitley, said he examined prisoner and found a cut in his throat and a punctured wound on the left breast. Neither was deep or dangerous. He asked the prisoner who had cut him, and he replied "Myself.' He found two knives, one a clasp knife and one a shoe- maker's knife. The prisoner said to. him " I wish I had finished myself. I am better dead than alive." The old man, who was removed to Dutton Hospital, had been in the Workhouse off and on for six or seven years. —Dr. Selby said Cowap knew perfectly well what he had done. He used to be a doorkeeper at the Work- house. He had been studying." Pearson's Weekly," and working out puzzles, and Cowan said that that "had turned his head a little bit." He had promised witness never to repeat the offence.—Prisoner was discharged on the understanding that he would go back to the Work- house.
