FRACAS ON THE FOOTBALL FIELD.

_ A sequel to the Warrington anff Runcorn match en- gaged the attention of the magistrates at the Borough Police Court on Monday. Two brothers named Patrick Ward, 24, Dolman's lane, and Peter Ward,4, Litherland's yard, were charged with having been drunk and dis- orderly on the Warrington Football Field on Saturday afternoon. They pleaded guilty. Patrick was also charged with assaulting Sergeant Smith and Constable Hughes, and Peter with assaulting Constable Sweeney. —The Chief Constable stated that the Case arose out of the Warrington and Runcorn football match. The Warrington Club, he might say,went to considerable ex- pense in engaging special constables to keep order and restrict the patrons of the game to their proper portions of the ground. One trouble was that certain spectators on the popular side would persist, when the match was over, in jumping the fence, and run- ning across the field. What they gained by it was difficult to say, but the Football Club suffered in the turf being damaged, and rendered almost unfit for play. The police, therefore, received special instructions to prevent that sort of thing. The man Peter was in an advanced state of drunkenness, pnd when spoken to by P.C. Sweeney about being on the turf he threw off his coat and vest and dashed at the officer. assaulting him violently. The other officers came up and were also assaulted. Patrick also joined in the fray and endeavoured to make footballs of the police. However, he was not so bad as the other pri- soner, who lashed out right and left. Of course, a very large crowd gathered, and one of the spectators who endeavoured to assist the police received a violent kick. He might say he had received a letter from a citizen giving a version of the occurrence. but he had informed him that if he had anything to say in the case the better course woule be to come and give evidence.—The Chief Constable's statement was borne out by the police officers mentioned and Mr. Thomas Locker, a mem- ber of the Warrington Football Club Committee. Mr. Locker stated that he was on the stand and saw one of the prisoners engaged with the police. The ground had been damaged by people walking about it, and instructions had been given to the police to prevent it, if possible.—Superintendent Monaghan said both the prisoners were drunk when brought to the police station, though he had seen them in a worse condition.—Patrick Ward said he had only had one pint on the field and had been teetotal previously for two months.—Peter Ward inquired how it was, if the offence was so serious, none of the Football Club officials had come forward to give evidence. He admitted that when he got drink he lost his head .—The Chief Constable stated that both prisoners had bad records, having been convicted many times.— Patrick Ward was sent to gaol for one month and Peter for two months.

ST. PETER'S CHURCH.

Annual Sale of Work.

The annual sale of work in connection with St. Peter's Church was opened in the schoolroom, John-street, on Thursday afternoon by Lady Greenall. The room looked bright and attractive and the stalls which were neatly arranged, were in charge of the following :Vicarage stall, Mesdames Percival, Joseph Taylor, John Taylor, James Greaves, Hebbs, East and Juson, and Miss Young ; refreshment stall, Mesdames Balmforth., Lingley, Webster, Skelland, Stanley and Burdett; tea- room, Mrs. F. Webster, Misses Barlow and Stanley; sweet stall, Misses Hughes and Percival ; flower stall Mr. and Mrs. Clayton, Miss Boardman and Miss Webster; advertisement stall, Mrs. Cowdell, Mrs. Wilkinson, and Mr, Kay; bran pie, Misses Juson and , Dunn. There was a good attendance at the opening ceremony and the Vicar (Rev. B. Percival) who presided, took the opportunity of heartily thanking all who had con- tributed in any way towards the sale. They had a great number of friends outside the parish who lent them valuable help, and he was exceedingly grateful to all who had endeavoured to make the sale a success. The parish was a very poor one, composed.. entirely of working people, and he was sorry to say work had not been very good for a long time. Perhaps that would account for the fact that their funds were rather short this year. The two main objects they had to support were the curate's fund and the lay workers' (Church. Army) fund. They were responsible for £B5 a year for those funds after receiving grants. Last year he gave £77 of the sale of work proceeds to the curate's fund, £l5 to the Church Army Captain's fund, £l5 to the church wardens for special church repairs, and £7 145., to the fund for ex- tending the boundary wall. Their needs this year were no less, for they had still the curate's and lay workers' funds to .support. Then agaiu as the parish grew organizations had to be increased, and money was re- quired to maintain them. He would therefore be glad of all the help that could possibly be given. Lady GREENALL, who had a cordial reception, alluded to the increase of organizations. They were, she said, a continual source of anxiety to those connected with them, because she was afraid that in many cases the funds did not grow correspondingly with the organiza- tions. She hoped the bazaar would raise sufficient to enable their parish organizations to be carried on successfully and that there would be no debt left on! them whatever. (Applause.) On the motion of Mr. JOSEPH TAYLOR seconded by I Mr. KAY a vote of thanks was accorded to Lady r Greenall and the sale commenced.

The Warrington branch of the National Society for the prevention of cruelty to children, (local office 34 Parker street, Warrington), has during the • quarter ended 30th September, plaints of neglect, ill-treatment and other wrongs of childhood, of which all were dealt with, having been found to be true. 176 children were effected in the cases. GREA.T SALE OF BOOTS AND SHOES now on UNDER THE CLOCK.—A4Vt. The Claims of the S.P.G.

The Bishop of Madagascar, who on Monday even- ing was shouted down at Liverpool, because he wore a large cross on his breast, received a cordial welcome and an appreciative hearing in the Parr Hall on Tues- day evening, when he addressed the annual meeting of the Warrington Branch of the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in foreign parts. The pro- ceedings were presided over by Councillor W. J. For- shaw and he was supported on the platform by the Rector and most of the local clergy. A. united choir drawn from the various churches led the singing under the conductorship of Mr. Percy .Randall.—The Chairman said that to carry the Gospel to the heathen must be a very difficult and arduous work and deserved the earnest support and encouragement of all true Christians. Many missionaries connected with the society who were sent out into heathen lands to preach the Gospel, the true life, and the way to gain eternal happiness, ran very great risk, and they, as Christians, ought to sacrifice something in order to render to the.S.P.G. some financial aid and assistance. —The Bishop of Madagascar said .he was glad the church in Warrington took this missionary work very seriously. In an interesting account of mission work in the diocese of Madagascar his lordship said the number of native church communicants there was 3,000, and they had also ten native priests. Successful efforts were being made to improve the standard of the native teachers, and progress was. made not only in the spread of Christianity among the natives, but in the training of them in agriculture and handicrafts such as carpentry. The Bishop paid a tribute to the impartiality of the French authorities in their attitude I towards the Roman Catholic and Protestant missions in Madagascar.--An address was also delivered by the Rev. F. J. Taylor, who spoke upon the work of the society in India. England .he said, had done a great deal to improve the condition of India, but its I work would not be finished until it had given the ,people Christ.—A collection taken on behalf of the I society's funds realised :SS 10s. TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

Annual Meeting and Election of Officers.

MR. PLINSTON AND THE " ADVANCED " INFLUENCE.-STRONG COMMENTS.

The annual meeting of this Council was held in Bold- street Wesleyan Schools on Tuesday evening, under the presidency of Mr. J. Podmore, junr.

THE ELECTIONS.

Mr. 0. BUCKLEY wrote acknowledging the Council's invitation to contest a ward at the forthcoming municipal elections. He was sorry to say that his posi- tion would preclude him from accepting it at the present time. ANNUAL REPORT. CORPORATION CONTRACTS, FISCAL POLICY AND EDUCATION QUESTIONS. " MISREPRESENTATIONS " CORRECTED.

The SECRETARY (Councillor Fred Stott) sub- mitted the twenty-ninth annual report as follows : There is little change to report in the constitution of the Council as compared with last year. The Wire- weavers and Filesmiths Associations have become defunct, the former as a result of the lengthy dispute which was referred to in our last report, and the latter association having disbanded some months ago, owing to the fact that there were no prospects of gaining new members, there being practically no young men coming into the trade as apprentices. The Warrington Branch of 'tailors not having been represented at any meetings held during the year, it becomes necessary to remove their name from the list of affiliated societies. We deplore the loss of these branches, as they have been members for a considerable number of years. No ex- p:anation has been given of the absence of the tailors representatives, and we trust that their secession will be only temporary. The additions to the Council are the Postmen's Federation, and the Bewsey Ironworkers, which have resulted in an increase of seventy in our membership during the year. There are yet several important branches which are not affiliated, and we trust that each delegate will do his utmost during the ensuing year to try and prevail upon these organisations to join us, so that the Council may become representa- tive of the whole of the trade unionists in Warrington. Owing to pressure of work, your secretary has been unable during the past year to devote the necessary time to the work of organization, but he hopes, with the assistance of the delegates to take in hand this most important work during the year now entered upon. .

===== 0002601_19041015_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADES HALL.

The question of building a Trades Hall has been abandoned for the present owing to lack of support by the affiliated members. We are however, of an opinion that a Trades Hall would be of considerable value as a centre where the various trade union organisations could meet, instead of as at present holding their meet- ingslin various parts of the town,in the majority of cases licensed premises. IVlany of the branch meeting rooms are utterly unsuitable, and cannot be either individually or collectively beneficial to the members.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
CONDITION OF TRADE.

We regret to have to report that trade during the year has continued to be bad, and there is little prospect of any immediate improvement. The local building industries have suffered considerably owing to lack of employment, and the percentage of unemployed has been greater than for many years past. There is every pros- pect of a bad winter as far as empldyment in the build- ing trades is concerned. Some slight improvement has taken place in the iron and cotton industries, which we trust will be permanent, but employment in the engineering and wire drawing trades continues very unsatisfactory. We cannot expect any improvement in the latter industry until a more settled frstate of affairs exists regarding the working conditions. From the Board of Trade returns we find that the proportion of unemployed has shown a steady increase until at the present time, there is a greater percentage of unemployed members of trade unions than there has been for a considerable number of years. The prospects for the coming winter are not very bright, and we trust ,that steps will be taken to provide temporary employ- ment for some of those who are in need of it. The question of providing work for the unemployed is a difficult one and requires careful consideration. We venture to suggest that a conference should be held composed of representatives from the Town Council, Board of Guardians, Chamber of Commerce, and the Trades and Labour Council for the purposo of discussing the question of providing temporary work for unemployed Warrington citizens. If such a conference as suggested was not as successful as we would wish, we believe that a consideration of this question could not be other than beneficial.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
DISPUTES.

We are pleased to report that during the year no serious dispute has taken place in any of the large industries in Warrington. Two small branches of trade, however, have been engaged in disputes with their employers. The skip and basket makers were engaged in a dispute with one of their employers for many months, at a great financial cost to the men's union. We are enabled to report that the dispute was settled in such a way that there was apparently little dissatis- faction at the terms of settlement on either side. The dispute in the Flint glass trade was of short duratior, except in one instance where the employer refused to agree to the terms of settlement accepted by the other employers. Several months having elapsed since that occurred, and no report having come to hand, we are unable to state whether a settlement has yet been arrived at, and the whole of the men re-instated.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
CORPORATION CONTRACTS.

The question of Corporation contracts has again been considered by your Council during the year. With regard to the penalty clause in the new fair wage Standing Orders, your Council endeavoured to meet the views of the Town Council by reducing the number of years to two, but we regret that the clause was again rejected at the Town Council meeting. We trust how- ever that they will accept a clause with a penalty suffi- ciently severe to prevent contractors from breaking the terms of a contract. It is our firm opinion that if the new Standing Orders are administered by the Coun- cil as they should be, much of the dissatisfaction which has existed in past years will be avoided.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
APPOINTMENT OF MAGISTRATES.

A considerable addition having been recently made to the local magistrates' bench, an effort was made by your Council to secure the appointment of workingmen representatives. Unfortunately we were too late in making application to the Chancellor of the Duchy. We trust that when the next addition is made to the magistrates' bench your Council will be successful in securing the appointment of some of its ilominees.

MUNICIPAL BOWLING GREEN.

It is with much pleasure we are able to report that a municipal bowling green has at last been provided. Your Council is to be congratulated on the share which was taken in the agitation for municipal greens, and we trust that the one provided will be successfnl so that other districts may have the same facilities granted to them.

EDUCATION.

_ Mr. Edwin Plinston, your representative on the Edu- cation Committee, at the April meeting, gave an inter: sting report of his first year's work which met with the unanimous approval of the delegates present.lt does not appear to be well-known amongst the working-classes, that they can claim elementary education for their children, or there would certainly be more applications made for the privilege to which they are justly entitled under the Act. Your Council expressed them- selves in favour of all the public elementary schools in the Borough being made free. TRADE UNION BILL.

With the objeci—Wies-tO-ring—the. status of trade unions a Bill has been introduced in the House of Commons during the year by Mr. D. Shackleton; M.P. The second reading took place on April 22nd, and was carried by a majority of thirty-nine. We consider it our duty to draw your attention to the fact that our local Member of Parliament, Mr. Robert Pierpoint, M.P., voted with.phe minority against the second read- ing. In our opinion the Bill was not carried because the House of Commons approved of it, but because a number of its opponents stayed away from the House, and many of those present Who voted for it, knew the Bill would be blocked, and prevented from making further progress. In the meantime the Courts continue to give decisions contrary Ito the Trade Union Acts passed by Parliament thirty years ago. We must not therefore, expect to have any satisfactory amendment of the law until the House of Commons contains many more labour representatives than there are at the present time. We trust that after the next election there will be a strong "Labour group" independent of either political party, but prepared to work with, or oppose any party as circumstances demand. It will then be difficult for the House of Commons, no matter which party is in power, to refuse to pass a Bill on the lines of the one introduced during last session.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
CHINESE LABOUR.

Another important question which has received the serious consideration of your Council is the importation of Chinese coolies into the Transvaal. The proposals were unanimously condemned by the Council when the Ordinance was being framed, and at a public meeting held in the Co-operative Hall, a strongly-worded letter of protest was carried unanimously. We consider it to be both a danger and a disgrace to introduce yellow labour into the South African mines, tinder such obnoxious conditions. The Chinese have been introduced to " blackleg " the Kaffirs who refused to work for a considerably less rate of wages than they had been receiving previous to the war. We venture the opinion that there was no necessity to import Chinese labour, believing that sufficient white and' Kaffir labour would have been forthcoming if reasonable wages had been offered, and which judging from the returns of dividends, could have been well afforded.

"FISCAL REFORM."

Your Council cannot yet see any reason for a change in their opinions regarding the question of Fiscal Reform advocated by Mr. Joseph Chamberlain M.P., and his friends. The proposals have been almost unani- mously condemned by the Trades Union Congress, the National Labour Representation Committee, and the Lancashire and Cheshire Federation of Trades and tabour Councils. For many months, the question has been very little in evidence, but with the advent of autumn we may expect it to be resurrected, and the old arguments re-hashed by the leaders of the movement. Believing as we do in "Free Trade "as a policy, still we are not satisfied with the present conditions under which we live, when it has been authoritatively stated that over twelve millions of people are on the verge of starvation. We are prepared to resist most strongly a tax on our food stuffs, and deny the statement that "an increase in the cost of living well result in a pro- portionate increase in our wages." We believe in "pro- tection " but not the protection advocated by the Tariff Reform League. We require proteation from the land- lord, the railway director, and the extractor of mining and mineral royalties and this can be accomplished by the nationalisation of the land, railways, and mines. Mr. PLINSTON moved the adop‘ion of the report, and coupled with his proposal a vote of thanks to the Secre- tary. He was, he said, exceedingly pleased in reviewing the work of the year to find that the Council had undoubtedly done a great deal of good. They could show the public that they had taken questions in hand and worked them out satisfactorily not only for the benefit of the working-classes but, he believed, of the whole of Warrington. Mr. Plinston alluded in com- plimentary terms to the work of Mr. J. Ball, elective auditor, who, he considered, had proved himself one of the best officials in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) He was delighted to think that a workingman could be found sufficiently capable to discharge the duties, and with sufficient courage to support his convictions. The report Mr. Ball contributed to the local Press was a remarkably good one,and he thought the Council might hope to be able to convince the town by further repre- sentation that trade unionists were able to deal satis- factorily with matters requiring attention. (Hear, hear.) _

The motion was agreed to, and Councillor STOTT re- turned thanks.

THE FINANCES.

Mr. RATHBONE submitted the balance sheet which showed an income of £33 10s. After paying expenses there was a credit balance of £2 7s. 2d.

Mr. YORKE commented upon the satisfactory way in which the accounts were kept. The balance sheet was adopted. ELECTION OF OFFICERS.

Three candidates'were nominated for the presidency— Messrs. J. Podmore. (retiring president), E. Plinston and W. Yorke—and the voting resulted in the re-election of Mr. Podmore. Mr. T. Plumb was elected vice-president, Mr. A. Warburton, treasurer (uuopposed), and Mr. F. Stott, secretary (unopposed). The following were elected members of the Executive Committee :—Messrs. 0. Buckley, A. Jolley, E. Plinston, T. Plumb and Bullock, Messrs. 0. Buckley and F. Stott were appointed dele- gates to the Labour Representation Committee, but Councillor Stott expressed a desire to be relieved of the duties and the appointment of another representative will be considered at the next meeting. Councillor Stott was re-elected delegate to the Lancashire and Cheshire Federation of Trades and Labour Councils. MR. PLLNSTON AND 1` ADV,ANCED " INFLUENCE.

Mr7P-LIN-S-TO-14- Was nominated for +he vice-presidency but received only eleven votes as against thirteen for Mr. T. Plumb who was therefore elected. When the figures were announced Mr. Plinston congratulated Mr. Plumb upon his election. At the same time, he pointed out that t the delegates had taken an unusual course in rejecting him (Mr. Plinston) after he had served for a year as vice-president. He took their action as typical of what had, unfortunately, been taking place in the Council for some time—that any man who did not see with and belong to the advanced portion of the Council was not considered worthy of occupying an office. That was not the policy that would lead to a success- ful future of the Trades Council. Whatever opinions members had outside the Council, they ought not to dominate the working of the Council. A person had a right to belong to the Independent Labour Party or any other organization, but if he did not identify himself with the advanced section it was not sufficient justification for a vote of want of confidence—and the action of the Council amounted to that. He had for some time felt his presence was not as acceptable to the members as it used to be. People might think he had erred in one respect, but he would not enter into that matter although he would be able to prove that he simply acted according to the dictates of his society. He had always tried to the best of his ability to carry on the work of the Council as a Trade Council representative of the workers, and he took it that the members' want of confidence in him was simply and solely owing to the fact that he did not belong to a section who were more advanced in their opinions than he happened to be. Mr. YORKE did not think Mr. Plinston was doing himself justice. He for one, and he was sure many others, would like to hear Mr. Plinston's version of one affair that he had hinted at.

Mr. BUCKLEY thought that the matter was entirely out of order. The very fact that Mr. Plinston had come within two votes of the successful candidate showed there was confidence in him on the part of some dele- gates. Mr. PLDISTON : That I admit. A DELEGATE considered it childish for Mr. Plinston to raise objections after he had been defeated.

The CHAIRMAN 'said Mr. Plinston had taken the matter in the wrong light. Mr. Plinston's position on the Education Committee showed that the Council had every confidence in him. (Hear,hear.) Further discus- sion would be out of order.

The matter then dropped. _ A VOTE OF SYMPATHY. _

On the motion-oi-t-h-e CHAIRMAN, -seconded by Mr. PLINSTOIi, and supported by Mr. YORKE, the Council passed a vote of sympathy with Mr. J. Edwardson, ex- president, who is recovering from a long and serious illness. Reference was made to the earnestness and enthusiasm which Mr. Edwardson threw into trade union work during the period in which he held office. _

LABOUR REPRESENTATION. WHAT IS BEING DONE IN REGARD TO PARLIAMENTARY

AND MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

Mr. BucKLEir reported upon the most important matters which had engaged the attention of the Labour Representation Committee during the month. For a long time, he said, they had been in negotiatiln with a society regarding a Labour candidate for the Borough. A certain gentleman had visited the local committee and the members had expressed their confidence in him. If calledlupon he would be willing to contest theßorough even if there was a three-cornered fight. The Executive Committee of that gentleman's society had been approached, but satisfactory terms could not be arranged. At the last meeting of the Labour Representation Committee a letter was submitted,and in consequence it was decided to end the negotiations with the society. The com- mittee had not, however, entirely dropped the matter and directly the municipal elections were over it woul4 be further considered. With regard to the municipal elections the majority of the societies affiliated with the committee were in favour of contesting the elections, and it was decided, with two dissentients, to bring forward labour candidates. The selections of wards and candidates was proceeded with, and it was agreed as a preliminary to bring forward Mr. Wright, who was nominated by the Independent Labour Party and re- presented the Wiredrawers' Association on the Com- mittee, as the candidate for Bewsey Ward bye-election. Mr. Buckley concluded by asking the Council to do all they could to secure adequate labour representation remarking that in Warrington the iron was hot and only needed striking. Councillor STOTT pointed out that it was never the intention of the Labour Representation Committee to invite a gentleman to contest Warrington on behalf of Labour. They weresnot prepared to take that responsi- bility. It was intended to hold a public demonstration to be addressed by the prospective candidate and after- wards the trade union branches would be invited to meet in conference with the other affiliated societies and decide whether the gentleman should be brought forward.

Mr. SMITH denied that Mr. Thomas Wright repre- sented the Wiredrawers' Association on the Labour Representation Committee.

The CHMRMAN saia-Yfrr-Wright represented a large number of the Association mills, but he did not actually represent the Association. In the course of further discussion Mr. Buckley said no preference had been shown to the Independent Labour Party in chosing candidates.

CORPORATION CONTRACTS.

- _ The fair wage standing orders were again under con- sideration, and it was pointed out that the penalty clause had been rejected by the Town Council. Mr. PLINSTON said the result of the adoption of the standing orders had been an increase of wage to some carters. _ .

The CHAIRMAN said it was untrue to state that the clauses were aimed at respectable people. Their object was that respectable employers should have the prefef- ence. (Hear, hear.) Many members of the Council said trade ought to be kept in town, and he considered they would best serve that end by having a large num- ber of work people properly paid. There were close on 3,000 trade unionists :in Warrington and most of them were responsible householders and voters. Mr. JOLLEY thought the penalty dlause should be made a test question at the elections. The suggestion that candidates should he asked to state their views on the clause appeared to meet with general approval, and the re-framing of the clause was left in the hands of the secretary.

A SON SUES HIS MOTHER.

Action at the Warrington County Court.

REMARKABLE REVELATIONS.

Before his Honour, Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C., and a jut& at the Warrington County Court on Thursday, Albert John Wood, of Haydock, claimed £25 damages against Gabriel Moon, auctioneer, Earlestown, for alleged illegal distress. Mrs. Sumner was brought in as a third party. Mr. Browne represented the plaintiff, Mr. E. A. White, the defendant, and Mr. Griffiths, barrister, Liverpool (instructed by Mr. R. W. Thomas, St Helens) appeared for Mrs. Sumner. Mr. Griffiths explained that there was no dispute between the second and third parties. Mr. Browne: It resolves itself into a dispute between the first and third parties. His Honour: Second class is abolished. (Laughter) Mr. Browne, addressing the jury, said the plaintiff was a traction engine driver for the Warrington and District Threshing Machine Company Limited, and the defendant was Mr. Gabriel Moon, an auctioneer at Earlestown, who had brought in a third party, named Mrs. Sumner, the plaintiff's mother, who was a farmer and threshing machine proprietor at Haydock. The point at issue was whether a cottage occupied by the plaintiff was to be rent free together with his wages, or whether he had to pay his mother rent for it. Unfortunately there was a dispute between the son and his mother—the mother having married a second time. The plaintiff was employed by the St. Helens Corporation as the driver of a traction envie at 30s- per week. Mrs. Sumner told him that if he would come and look after her farm and her threshing machine she would not only give him 30s. a week, but she would also allow him to live in the cottage on the farm rent free. For some time he refused the offer, but subsequently he consented and gave up his work at St. Helens. He remained in her employment for two years. In the meantime, Mrs. Sumner got a separation from her husband, but she subsequently took him back again. The plaintiff entered into an agreement to purchase the threshing machine and a dispute had arisen in regard to it. An action was pending in the Palatine Chancery Court respecting the agreement and the plaintiff found it was impossible to continue in Mrs. Sumner's employ. Shortly after the action had been entered, to the astonishment of the plaintiff, Mr. Gabriel Moon' auctioneer, destrained on the plaintiff's goods for 34 weeks rent at 38. a week. Mr. Moon was told that no rent had been paid by the plaintiff and he asked the two parties if they could not settle the matter. Mr. Moon also came to Warrington and saw him (Mr. Browne) who wrote him that he had never paid or agreed tolpay any rent for the cottage Mr. Moon very properly took:the precaution of coming out of possession' but a few days afterwards he came again stating that he had had a letter from Mrs. Sumner's solictors, telling him that the distress must go on. Acting on the advice of himself (Mr. Browne) the plaintiff paid 4s. under protest. The families had got across but in this case the plaintiff had been wrong- fully distrained upon. The plaintiff had never been asked for rent and had never paid a single penny. Until the action was started in the Palatine Court not a single word was heard about it. The distraint for rent had injured the plaintiff's reputation and he was entitled to damages. Albert John Wood, the plaintiff corroborated Mr. Brown's statement.

Cross-examined by Mr. Griffiths plaintiff denied that he had ever received a rent book or that he had burnt two rent books.

A portion of a rent book was handed up to the plaintiff but he denied having seen it before. His Honour scrutinised it and said there was no name on it or anything to identify it as belonging to the plaintiff. It might be Admiral Togo's rent book from all that one could glean from it. (Laughter). Sarah Ellen Wood, wife of the plaintiff, deposed that Mrs. Sumner told her that she could live in the cottage rent free if she would come. ___ _ . _ _

Mary Wood, wife of Thomas Wood (Rochester) stated that Mrs. Sumner informed her that she was going to get Albert to live at the cottage rent free and give him 30s. a week.

A. Hartley (Newport).—The five stroke which you made should not have been added to your score. Your ball left the table, and your opponent counts two for his, and both cannot count points from the same stroke. RECKLESS MOTORING AT WARRINGTON.

Liverpool Chaufeur Smartly Dealt With.

A FOOT PASSENGER SERIOUSLY INJURED.

The Warrington magistrates marked their sense of the seriousness of reckless motoring in the public thorough- fares by inflicting a severe penalty on William Brooks, chaufeur of 43, Roliely-street, Liverpool, whom they found guilty of driving a motor car recklessly and to the danger of foot passengers in Church-street, on October Ist. Mr. Steel watched the proceedings on behalf of James Lowe, of Padgate-lane, who was knocked down by the defendant's car and seriously injured. The Chief Constable briefly detailed the facts of the case. It was alleged that the defendant was driving at a reckless pace up Church-street and knocked Mr. Lowe down. At one time Mr. Lowe's life was despaired of, and he was not sure he was out of danger even aow. Sergeant Barlow was sent to Liverpool to make inquiries respecting the driver of the motor car and it was found that the d fendant was driving a motor car to the use of which he had no right,and that he had taktm the motor car away without the knowledge of the owner. He thought he could prove that the defendant was driving "like a meteor gone mad." John Knowles, of Whalley-street, driver of the tram- car which was in the street at the time of the accident said he left the Central Station to go to the Cemetery at about five o'clock. He saw the old man Lowe signal to him to stop. and at that timehe would be about 100 yards past the General Wolfe and about a yard from the footpath. Witness was driving at the rate of about eight miles an hour. Witness drew up, and at the same time a motor car whizzed past him, and he then saw Lowe in the air. Lowe dropped with his head on the;rail, and if the rails had been greasy witness would not have beenable to pull up in time. Witness took his hat off and waved it to the man in charge of the motorcar, in which there were half a dozen people. They all stood up in the car, saw witness on the floor with the old man but the car did not return ; and it drove off as fast as it could. The old man was placed in a cab and taken to the Infirmary. Councillor Tinnion : Did they bring the motor to a standstill? _ _ _

Witness: No; they slowed up to the rate of two or three miles an hour, almost to a standstill. In answer to the Chief Constable, the witness said he should think the motor car was being driven at the rate of 30 miles an hour. It was a very dangerous speed. It went as fast as any motor he had ever seen. Defendant denied that anyone in the car stood tap. Harry Rogers, an electrician, said he was in the street at the time of the accident near the General Wolfe Inn. He saw Lowe put his hand up so that the conductor could stop the tram car. The motor came down Church- street at a great speed and Lowe was carried-a few yards and lilted into the air, after which he fell on his face. He sustained a compound fracture of the right leg and injuries to the face. Witness got to know the number of the car and reported it.to the police. The Chief Constable: Have you formed an opinion as to the rate of speed at which the motor was going? Witness: I should think it was not less than 30 miles an hour. The driver was going recklessly and to the danger of passengers. Sergeant Barlow stated that he visited Liverpool and ascertained that the motor belonged to Messrs. Lunton, wholesale bakers, Liverpool, and not to the defendant. It had been taken away without the consent of the firm.

Defendant said it was impossible for the car to go 30 miles an hour. How was it he was not stopped? ' The Mayor said that there was a bylaw in Warrington that motorists must drive at a reasonable speed. Defendant: It was quite a reasonable speed. The Clerk: It does not matter what speed you go at if you are reckless. Defendant: I was not driving recklessly. The Clerk: Have you any witnesses? Defendant replied that he had, and that he could call witnesses. He took the motor out to test two new plates that had been placed on the motor. ,Richard Lang, Harriet-terrace, Liverpool, said the motor car was not going 30 miles an hour; it was not going half that speed, and he was used to motor cars. The Chief Constable: Are you one of the men who looked back and then drove on? Witness: I did not see. I was on the off-side. Did you know something had happened ?—Witness I could nottell you. - - - -

Is it a fact that you slowed up never knew of the accident till we were on the way home. How did you know then ?—Because someone directed our attention to it. -- -

_ e*Did you slow up at all ?—We slowed up two or three times in Warrington. I don't remember slowing up at the Parish Church. You were all sober?—Yes, sir. _ -

- Questioned as to the names of the inmates of the car he said one lady was a Miss Inglis, of 8, Harriet Terrace, Liverpool. He could not tell the name of the other lady. One of the occupants of the car was a friend of his. _

In answer to the Mayor, witness said they did not ride on to Manchester. When they had gone some distance out of Warrington; they turned back and came through Warrington again. They left Liverpool at ten minutes to two and passed through Warrington at five. It would be about twenty minutes afterwards that they came back. They had a puncture, but it was repaired at Cront on.

The Chief Constable said that on behalf of the Watch Committee and the police, he must press for a severe penalty in this case, as a warning. He would leave the matter with confidence in the hands of the Bench. The Mayor: The decision of the Bench is that you be fined £lO and costs, or two months' imprisonment, and the licence endorsed. Defendant: Will you allow me time to go to Liver- pool to get the money? The Chief Constable: I would not allow you a minute. (To the Bench) : We had to serve the sum- mons at once. He is a man of straw. The fine and costs amounted to £ll 15s. Defendant went to gaol.

ENGLAND'S FATTEST POLICEMAN.

Police-Sergeant Narramore, of Rock, Worcestershire who is said to be the fattest policeman in England, is etiring after twenty years' service. Although only sft. 91n. high, Narramore weighs over 20st. A country magistrate has asked "Truth" whether those papers which are devoted to the cause of automobilism are justified in publishing notices of "police-traps," and in recommending drivers to scatter a particular-coloured confetti on the road wherever they discover such traps? These warnings, his worship observes, "defeat the ends of justice."

But" Truth" points out, and very rightly, that there seems to be some misapprehension as to the duty of the police and the ends of justice. The first duty of the police is to prevent breaches of the law, not to let breaches occur so that they may pounce upon the delinquents. That is so. And, therefore, the persons who give notice of police traps are suggesting to the possibly thoughtless driver that, at the spot indicated, it is well to drive quite within the limit allowed by the law since it is probably a dangerous or an awkward place for a fairly high speed, or the police would hardly be in ambush there, and in this way it may be said that they are combining with the police, as all good citizens should, to see that the Act is duly observed.

We hear rumours of a light four-cylindered car shortly to be placed upon the market at a price which will appeal to every autqpiobilist.

There is certainly no doubt that the single-cylinder voiturette has practically had its day. It served us well enough for a time; but it may now besaid to have out- lived its usefulness and the sooner it is decently interred in the Limbo of discarded mechanical contrivances the better for us • all. At least a twin-cylinder engine is required for even a small car, if the comfort of driver and passengers is to be ensured; but there is little doubt that next year will see a boom in multi-cylinder engines upon moderate-priced cars.

From a strictly !mechanical standpoint, Mr. Moffat Ford, the managing director of the Motor Car Co., Ltd., is a strong believer in the future of the five- cylinder motor. Five cylinders are not quite enough, he argues, whilst six are unnecessary. In his opinion, a large and high h.p. vehicle should be provided with a five-cylinder motor in order to secure the acme of com- fort and supreme sweetness and smoothness of running. The three-cylinder motor, on the other hand, he con- tends will never become really popular.

According to the daily papers, motorphobia has reached an acute stage in Long Islan I. During the recent international race for the Vanderbilt Cup, the competitors were" booed "awl hooted by many of the spectators who lined the roads, whilst one fatal accident was directly attributable:to the:action of anti-motorists. Part of the course had been maliciously strewn with broken glass and nails, and in the fourth round Mr. Arents who was driving a Mercedes, was startled to hear a loud explosion. Leaning over the side to see what had happened—as a matter of fact a tyre had burst owing to the presence of the glass and nails—he unconsciously swerved his steering and was jerked right out of his car.

His mechanic instantly grasped the steering .wheel and pulled the car round again, but the wrench was too much for the steering gear, which immediately broke. The car ran on, struck an obstacle and turned turtle,the unfortunate chauffeur being pinned underneath. Three hours later he died in the hospital. Mr. Arents him- self is fearfully injured, and at the time of writing, it is feared be cannot possibly recover.

Now the chauffeur's death is practical murder, by some person or persons unknown. But the responsi- bility of it goes farther back still. It is not so long since a New York magistrate advised shooting scorch- ing motorists on sight; and here in England to-day, although with one exception, we have no such violently inclined authority, many of our daily newspapers are really responsible for n tich of the bitter feeling which exists in some quarters against motorists. Foolish and intolerant letters, which had far better never been written, have been published ad. lib. in order to start an autumn correspondence, and in some intances, it is stated, the.replies have been suppressed. Each sidehas a right to a fair hearing—if any hearing at all is advis- able. But the editors of the daily newspapers can do nothing towards rectifying any grievance which may exist, whilst, by publishing the excessively silly vapour- ings of the obviously feeble-minded, they are really damaging the cause they attempt to champion. Far better would it be to stick to "The Handicap of Marriage," which is a nice, safe subject, affording delirious delight both to the married and the unmarried of both sexes, and it has the great advantage of doing no real harm whilst the vast possibilities of the debat- able point render the newspaper a welcome one in every home.

HOCKEY.

WARRINGTON ROYAL V. OWENS COLLEGE IST. There was an interesting match between these teams at Walton, on Saturday. At half-time the Royal were leading by three goals to nil, Clarke having scored twice and Whittle once. Shortly after the restart F.Plinston put another go,tl through. The play then favoured the visitors for a while, and they reduced the Royal's lead by scoring three goals in rapid succession. Clarke eventually scored again for the Royal, thus giving them the victory by five goals to three. For Owens College Wardleworth (centre half) played a capital game.

HUYTON v. STOCKTON HEATH. These teams met at Huyton, the visitors pressing at the start. From a free hit the home forwards attacked bard and from a corner. Saunderson had hard luck in not scoring. The Huyton team gave the visitors' defence plenty of work, but half-time came with no score. On changing over Stockton Heath made a dash for the home goal, and, after some fine combination, Bond scored. Huyton again attackpd and shot after shot was saved by the visiting goalkeeber, but at last Harrison equalized. The home team now had the best of the game, and excellent play by Kenna, Rogers, and Saunderson ended in Huyton again scoring ten minutes off time. Result: --Huyton, two; Stockton Heath, one.

STOCKTON HEATH 2ND v. HUYTON 2ND.

At Stockton Heath. Stockton Heath showed the better combination, and had hard lines in being beaten by four goals to three. ECCLES v. LATCHFOR D. •

At Eccles, on Saturday, the match, resulting in an easy win for the visitors by five goals to two. Although the visitors played a capital game all through, only one goal had been scored up to half-time, and this by Pear- son sor the visitors. On resuming, however, Latchford played with great dash and soon increased their lead, goals being scored by Cliffe (two),'Spenseley (one), and Banks (one). Eccles broke away on a few occasions, and managed to put the ball into the net twice, but always looked like a beaten side. The game unfortunately developed on the rough side, and although several were slightly hurt, nothing of a serious nature happened, and when the whistle was blown for time the score read Latchford five, Eccles two.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS%

Authors of letters intended for publicatioa must in all cases send tkeir names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee .3f good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on cne side collo of the paper and sent in, if possible, not later than thursday.

The Guardians and the Lowest Tender. Sir ---Seeing from all accounts that trade is anything but brisk and there is a likelihood of hard times with the approach of winter, one would expect that our Poor Law Guardians would be among the first to retrench and prepare for a possible drain upon them. It seems from the report of the meeting of the Board held at Warring- ton last week that our. own --authorities can afford to ignore this however and, in several instances tenders for goods and work have been accepted over-riding lower tenders from ratepayers in the district. At that meeting a tender for decorating the Relieving Offices, Earlos- town was accepted which was considerably in excess of our tender for the work. As a rule we are content to let matters of tenders rest upon their merits and trust to the fair dealing of our public men' but where the interests of the ratepayers are affected it is only right that the facts should be made known. We trust this matter with the others referred to at the meeting of the Board will be thoroughly investi- gated and punlished,otherwise it can hardly be expected that bona fide tenders will be made by respectable tradesmen.—Yours truly. THOMAS ALLEN & SONS.

" Republican's" Last Word..

Sir,—The letters in ycur last issue call for little if any comment. They simply reveal that on educational matters the writers are lamentably ignorant, and it is useless to prolong the controversy. The Church of England has ever been the enemy of all progress, and if she has made any attempt to educate the 'children she 11,14 done so from selfish motives, and in order to prevent the thing being done more efficiently by others. The reference with regard to the Rev. Mr. Carleton is paltry, but if "Churchman" is anxious to hear a debate on the Education Act, and can prevail upon the gentleman named or any other Warrington clergyman to take up the matter, then I venture to prophesy that the Rev. H. Dunnico will not cry off. With regard to the Rev. H. Dunnico and Councillor Stott I can only say that as he has offered to debate the subject with him or any other man in Warrington, it is no credit to the "Good old Church" that some of its members should make a cowardly and altogether uncalled for attack upon him. It appears that the " Good old Church" will confederate with brewers, slaveowtters, socialists, atheists, in fact anybody, against any man who declines to pay volun- tarily for the propagation of pernicious doctrines. Such attacks do the Rev. H. Dunnico no havn, in fact they do him good; and hundreds if not thousands of War- rington people have learned to respeot and admire one of the few men In their midst who has the courage to stand by his opinions regardless of public criticisms. Yours truly,

REPUBLICAN. P.S. So fares I am concerned this is my closing letter on the above matter. •

The Health of Warrington. • Sir,—For some time past I have been considering the advisability of drawing public attention to our markets. In two or three places on Wednesday last I noticed small heaps of rotten fruit lying about, having appar- entl7 been thrown there by stall-holders. Now I am of opinion that the health of many poor young children is seriously endangered because of this. •Timeand again these children come round the market to pick up odds and ends and how any good can be done to their bodies after eating all , sorts of filthy stuff passes my compre- hension altogether. ' Not only ought these heaps of rubbish to be placed in a proper space specislly provided, but the bins ought also to be looked after. There are two or three of these bins for rotten and waste fruit, etc.. hut they are not sufficietrt for their purpose. Et .peatedly have I seen them overturned, and the waste strewn on the floor as though some children had been rummaging to pick waste fruit up. Obviously no good can come to these children from eating such rotten stuff as is thrown about the market and in the bins. This matter ought to be attended to, and that at once. No wonder we see such sights in our streets as unhealthy children. This nuisance cannot be passed by lightly and for the sake of the poor child life of our town some steps ought to be taken to protect the children. Where are our Town Councillors and Market Inspectors that these things are not looked into? Can we expect anything more from our Labour men who are to repre- sent us on the Council? Surely some scheme can be suggested to remedy the present evils. A good idea I think would be to have several bins in necessary places provided with locks. Let the Market Inspectors and staff be provided with keys and also the stall-holders who reqiiirs to use • the bins. By these means the refuse would then be secure and out of the reach of the children. Between them the market officers and the stallholders could keep the bins locked up. Some of the stallholders throw about their refuse indis- criminately. This should not be allowed and the rules as to this ought to be especially strict. Cleanliness, we are told, is next to godliness. If this is so,and the condition of the fish and fruit markets is a reflection of the cleanliness of the stallholders and officials, then all that can be said is that these people cannot be particularly godly. Perhaps an abler pen than my own will take this question up.—Yours. L. W. E.

Rev. H. Dunnico and the Labour Party. Sir,—' decline te take further part in the abo vediscussi on I offered to meet the Socialist Labour Party in open de- bate providing they could find any man in the Borough of Warrington wilting to represent them. They have failtd to find a man willing to undertake such ahopeless task bet instead of frankly admitting such they have done their utmost by misrepresentation and falsehood to madden the unthinking section of the community against me. This of course is quite in keeping with their declaration of policy. Keir Hardie has for years declared that Demccrats and advanced Liberals who refuse to become socialists of a particular species must be destroyed as political forces in order to hasten the arrival of the socialistic millenium. T am still and ever shall be a strong advocate of gennine labour repre- sentation but I strongly resent the subtle trickery whereby trade union funds are being used to propagate a special brand of Socialism and to keep men in luxury who have never subscribed a penny to the funds of trades unions. The aims of trade unionism and social- ism are absolutely distinct and in the opinion of many Socialists hostile. The Socialist party is a political party and has no more right to representation on the Labour. Representation Committee than the Conservative and parties. In other words if one Liberal • Representetion Committee candidate is justified in receiving £2OO a year whilst a member of the Independent Labour party another man has a perfect right to retain his member- ship with the Conservative or Liberal Party. T have no quarrel with Socialists as snch. If they will take an honest stand and carry on their ownpropaganda at their own expense then they are entitled to the respect which all must feel for earnest men. But when they adopt dis: honest and immoral tactics in order to capture trade unions and other organizations they deserve to be treated with contempt. During the past few months I have haen in corres- pondence with many of the great labour leaders includ- iug John Burns, M.P., Richard Bell M.P., Sam Woods and others and my statements are based upon informa- tion received from them. These men without a single exception assert that the narrow exclusive and negative constitution of the Labour Representation Committee is detrimental to the best interests of labour. The hallway servants at their last conference not only upheld Richard Bell in his refusal to sign the Labour Representation Committee constitution but have decided to ask the committee to amend their constitution. Could anyone imagine a party in a more ridiculous plight than the Labour Representation party is mat present? It is absolutely without a programme and a party without a programme has hitherto been unknown among political combinations. Tne absence of a pro- gramme suggests that either its members do not know what they want or that they are too crafty to let their constituents know. Fancy paying a man's election expenses and if successful a salary of £2OO per annum for no other reason than this,—that he signs an aereement "not to support the candidature" of any man—even though he hold identical views—unless he term himself "Labour." When one takes into consideration that there are in the United Kingdom 670 constituencies, and that; the com- mittee are unable to put more than 40 or 50 candidates in the field, thus leaving untouched 620 constituencies. then such a rule savours more of comic opera than practical politics. Let the Labour Representation Com- mittee draw up a definite programme of labour legisla- tion and administration. This programme could then form a test at all Parliamentary elections. In those constituencies where it is impossible to place a labour candidate in the field, support the man irrespectivo ,of party most favourable to the labour progranrr.e. In fact I question whether ttere are a dozen constituen- cies in the Kingdom that could return a labour man without thel active co-oDeration of other progressives who do_not belong strictly and specially to the labour- inc classes. It was absurd for Councillor Stott to state as he did at the last meeting of the Trades Council that certain persons were "willfully misrepresenting the Labour Representation Committee." There is no necessity to misrepresent it. The whole constitution has broken down ridiculously and any criticism I have passed upon it is amply justified. The most prominent officials have violated and the committee itself has broken its own constitution. The London Trades and Labour Council only a short time ago passed a resolution condemning the constitution of the Labour Representation Coin-

raiztee. If Councillor Stott considers such a miserable constitution an ideal one why does he not come out and defend it? He prefers to-adopt the favourite policy of his party i.e., bluff. In conclusion I desire to say that as the source of all the mischief lies in the Socialists—men who have no necessary connection with trade unionists—the best service they can render to the cause of labour and Trade Unionism is to • let it alone. A trades union organization, pure and simple, can become a potent factor in politics. drit this attempt to I capture it by the Socialists 'will sooner or, later lead to a revolt on the part of Liberal and Conservative Trade Union- ists (who form by far the greater section of trade unionists in this country) and the" Labour Representa- tion movement" will come to grief. I thank you Mr. Editor for having allo sed me to make this protest in your columns and I trust that those who have the welfare of the nation at he irt will seek to guide the masses of thiscountry towards the goal of peaceful and orderly self-governmenE.—Yours, Scc., HERBERT DUNNICO. P.S.—The following manifesto with special preface by Mr. Keir Hardie M.P.,was issued by the Independent Labour Party some time ago. "Members of the Indepandent L;abour Party should get into Trades Unions, Trades Councils, Co-operative Societies Ste. Special endeavour should be made to get on the Liberal Associations—not of course with the foolish idealof trying to stiffen the Liberals up—but in order that the Independent Labour Party may have a firm body of friends in the enemy's camp. A firm body of Socialists could prevent a Liberal candidate being brought out to oppose a labour man. . . . or failing that could vote for the most re-actionary candidate and then by resigning in a body a few days before the elec- tion and publishing a manifesto could annihilate his chances. But above all things efforts should be directed to prevent Liberals bringing out advanced candidates. Any infusion of new blood into the Radical body must by prolonging its existence act to the detriment of the Independent Labour Party. End them not mend them must be our motto. This of course requires consider- able sacrifice of principle, but the Socialist troubled with scruples of conscience, is not worth his sail." IComment upon this is needless. Tine italics are my own.—H.D.

The Property-Owners' Association.

Sir,—The Property-Owners' Association meeting on Wednesday evening was a great success and from the report furnished by the committee it is evident that the Association has justified its existence. Several of the members have already received direct benefit and cer- tainly every property-owner ought, as mentioned by several of the speakers, to become a member. It already contains Aeveral prominent men of the town, four or five of whom, I believe, are members of the Town Council, so it can easily bt, seen that the Associa- tion cannot fail to be of some weight and influence in the towni understand the membership roll now numbers upwards of 130. Several very interesting speeches were made. Mr. Henshall whom I am glad to see has been appointed this year presidant, started the meeting off in capital style, his speech being one of good tone. Those who afterwards followed, certainly had the courage of their convictions and it is evident that despite high rates some of them are prepared to adopt a go-ahead policy believing that such a policy will pay Warrington best in the long run. The cattle market question is undoubtedly worthy of the Council's serious consideration and there is, to use a common phrase, "money in it." It does not neces- sarily mean that the formation of a cattle market would involve a large expenditure of money, for the chief requirement would be a large open space of ground. What is wanted is that a good available site should be obtained convenient to water and rail. Of course there are different views as to the widen- ing of streets, but no one can deny, as one speaker pointed out, that the town wants making more Mt:ac- tive to outsiders and wide streets is one method. These are matters that will have to be boldly faced, for it is not wise to stand still. A judicious expenditure of money brings a good revenue and I have no hesitation in saying that the town in many ways needs 'opening out.—Yours, etc., ONE PRESENT.

ODDFELLOW3' BOWLING HAM?. -- Cup and Prizes Presented by Captain

Crosfield.

MORE ABOUT NATIONAL DEFENCE.

One of the most interesting events of the bowling season locally has been the handicap promoted in con- nection with the Prince of Wales Lodge of the Indepen- dent Order of Oddfellows (Manchester Unity). The members entered Into the affair with spirit, and after many exciting games Bro. G. F. Freeman had the honour of carrying off the handsome cap and gold medal presented by Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, who is an esteemed member of the Order. There was a happy gathering at the Pelican Hotel on Thursday evening, when the suc- cessful competitors received their awards at the hands of Captain G. It. Crosfield. Almost at the last moment Mr. A. EL Crosfield who had promised to attend was obliged to cancel the engagement, owing to an attack of neuralgia, and his brother willingly stepped into the breach. The function was attended with complete success, the arrangements having been in the hands of the following; committee : —Messrs. N. Sykes, H. Maddock, J. Massey, T. Wadsworth, T. Reddish, W. Cottam, N. King, T. P. Bradshaw (chairman), and G. F. Freeman (secretary). The CELtIRNIAN expressed regret that Mr. Arthur Crosfield could not be present, and cordially thanked Ctptain Crosfield for taking his place. The bowling handicap, he ' continued, was not promoted for financial ends, but purely and simply with the object of bringing the lodge members into friendly contact, and inducing them to take a greater interest in the lodge business. They had a large number of mem- bers on the books and they could not maintain their position as the premier friendly society in Warrington unless all took their part in the work. There was great responsibility attaching to each member of the lodge. They had to contend with the ever-growing tontine clubs which at the present time were practically taking the town by storm. A means of contesting them would have to be seriously considered by those responsible for the Manchester Unity. That was the first Mine Captain Crosfield had been amongst them, and it would not be a bad idea if he followed his brother's example and be- eamea member of the lodge. (Applause.) They would be very pleased to welcome him as an Oddfellow. (Applause.) Captain CROSFIELD, who had a very cordial reception, thanked the Chairman for the invitation to become a member of the lodge—which he w iuld be most happy to accept. (Applause.) T'nev would not want to listen to a long speech from him, hat when a min hal certain opinions, and held then pretty stron;ly, it Ives his duty to speak out. He was not going to talk about bowls, because they knew far more about the gams than he did, but he would address them on a subject in which they were very closely interested, and that was insurance. What wonderful power man had when they worked together ! They in that society clubbed together and contributed so much per year in order to spread over the majority the accidents which would always happen, and thereby minimise the result to the individual. That was a very good work, and he was very much in favour of that kind of thing. Ha believed in old age pensions by the State. (Hear, heir.) We had been too afraid of what was called grandmotherly legislation, but he believed we wmitedl a great deal more of it. (Hear, hear.) He could assure them that Vangland had a great deal to learn from Germany in many ways. In that country every man was insured against old age, accidents, and diseise. He called that a very fine thing, and it was done years ago by Bismarck. People said that if they forced every- one to save they wou:d do away with thrift. That very objection was brought against Bismarck's scheme, but Bismarck said they would provide a pension which would keep a man out of the Workhouse but would not , provide him with I..xurie in his old age,so that there would still be an incentive to save. The scheme had worked out capitally. With the money raised by it the ' Germans had been able to provide workmen's dwellings before the pensions had to be paid, and they had proved a God-send to the working; populace. We wanted a little more legislation like that in this country. Another matter which he had very much at heart was insurance against war. He had given the question careful attention, and without wishing to be an alarmist he said they had got to look at history, and learn from it. They had to recognize the fact that times altered and our position was very different now from what it was fifty years ago. The best way to pre- vent war was to be prepared for war. (Hear, hear.) Ofl course every sane person recognized that the fleet was the first thing for us, but he maintained that to put all our eggs into one basket was not a sound policy. We wanted a backing to the fleet in the shape of a national citizen army. The ex- cellent results that could be obtained had been well I shown in Switzerland where they had a few months' training in the summer and an admirable national militia. Stich a scheme affected the physique and character of the nation beneficially. Our politicians were not going to bring the question of national defence forward, be- cause, he maintained, they did not guide the nation brit were very anxious to say pleasant things. He said "Give us the truth before everything." (Hear, hear.) It was necessary that the workin7 classes should guide their politicians, and show that they understood what was right, just, and necessary. They lia.d a means of doing that in the National Service League. They wanted that organization to become a mighty power throughout the land—like the German Navy League which had a membership of 600,000 and an annual income of £30,000. Before distributing . the prizes. 1 Captain Crosfield said he was delighted to hear that the members were so keen on bowls, because it was a game 1 in which all could join. They did not need to look on, but all could run aboutthe green. (Applause). Captain CROSFIELD than handed the handsome Cros.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

(By " Ovalist.")

The somewhat despised " Linnets" proved to be very sturdy foes at Wilderspool on Saturday, and Warrington were somewhat fortunate in scraping home by 5 points to 3 paints. Thpy had the better of the game to a coa-,iderable exteat but combined so poorly that they Ivirdly deserved to emerge victorious. The lighter Runcorn men played with great deter- mination and were frequently within an ace of obtain- ing a winning advantage. On the day's play they were the smarter team and deserves encouragement for the excellent fight they made against such powerful opponents as Warrington are usually considered to be at Wilderspool.

It is no secret thlt I.,Varrington expected to beat the Rune rn men by a handsome majority. The corresponding match last season resulted in favour of the home team by something like 18 points to 5 points, and a repetition of the performance was looked for by the Warrington supporters. The "Linnets " were never a side, however, to bow the knee humbly, and buoyed up with a handsome victory over Salford they came to Warrington fully determined to make a great effort. They had a slice of luck in obtaining three point=, but it was fully deserved and a little steadiness near the . close would have snatched the game out of the.fire.

Warrington obtained possession from quite two- thirds of the scrummages, but the backs have seldom been seen to worse advantage. The passing was so slow and illtimed that the smart Runcornians had little difficulty in checking each inroad. In fact they turned Warrington's slovenly work to their own account, for Harrison pouncing on a dropped pass among the home threequarters got clear away and gave among a soft try, which was not improved upon. Three shots at goal from easy positions were also missed by the visitors, and then Warrington used their superior strength with such effecE that tha game was carried to the Runcorn line. Belton had a clear opening when he was deliberately obstructed and everybody expected Mr. ArCutcheon to allow e try. InsteadAhowever, he gave a penalty kick and Fish wiped off two points .of the arrears. Half-time was near at hand and Runcorn retained their lead of one point to the interval.

There was yet n 1 concern among the home support- ers. They were confident in Warrington's ability to put on points when they liked, but some anxiety was apparent before the honr3 !ot got the upper hand. They started off with the appearance of-eyer,vhelming the "Linnets," whose forwards-were quite out of the arAument. The Warrington backs got hold repeat- edly but their progress towards the line did not cul- minate in anything tangible. 'in fact the backs bun- gled so many chances that there seemed a reasonable possibility that Runcorn would win after all. Just when people were feeling least hopeful the forwards took matters into their own hands. From a scrummage, near the line they secured the ball and bore down upon the Runcorn custodian in irresistible fashion and Boardman scored far out, thus eiyititr Warrington, instead of Runcorn a lead. The cheering was a clear indication of the relief felt by the hone Crowd. Once ahead and all Appeared plain sailing for Warrington. The visitors, however., rallied v.!ry pluckily and contested the game in the home quarters repeatedly. Near the close Richardson got away cleverly- and with all his men well up a try appeared eertain. But for once the smart half back made a sad mess of his opportunity. Instead of pass- ing or trying his own luck in a rush, he kiCked and the ball went • smack against the rails before a man could ge near it. It was an awful error, and the Runcorri players are to .be sympathised l. with. They had victory within their grasp and saw it slip away in. the most mortifying way possible—by the faalt of one of their own smartest men.

Needle.;-; to say, Warriogton took charge of affairs with both hands after this, and kept their enterprising opponent.; on the defensive till the referee's whistle proclaimed that the victory wail won. •

Angling.

The annual contest in connection with the Past and Present Olcers of the Warrington Anglers' Association was fished in the Bridgewater Canal at Agden on Satur- day afternoon last. The climatic conditions which prevailed were anything but of a favourable character and consequently sport was not at all of a brisk nature. Three hours' fishing was allowed, and the competitors were at liberty to fish where they pleased. Result—lst, W. H. Gandy. 10oz. lldrs. ; 2nd, T. Nixon; 3rd, F. J. Emson ; 4th, N. Cook, and sth, W. H. Podmore.

In comparison with other past week-ends of the season, that of last week showed a considerable diminution in the number of anglers who had ventured out in pursuit of sport amongst the various classes of course fish. Probably the "gale "of Saturday after- noon deterred many. What anglers were out did not, as a rule, record any sport of but a very moderate character. The river Gowy was practically a blank and the Old River at Lymm did not sustain its reputation, Mr. Sam Thomas being about the only and most success- ful angler engaged. The Old Quay Canal has been fairly well patronised of late and at odd times some fairly good fishing has been registered amongst the roach. Nfr. W. Shaw had three nice fish from here last week end. The lower length of the river Bollin has been productive just lately of decent roach fishing and the rains have freshened up the river somewhat. No reports are to hand from the rivers Dee and Weaver. Several good specimens of pike have this week come into War- rington having been captured in certain private waters in Cheshire.

I.overs of match fishing are reminded of the open competition to be fished at Broadheath to-day and for which entries will be accepted on the bank up to three o'clock. The first prize is R 1 10s. and from all accounts the first venture of the Broadheath and District Angling Society will prove a success. It should be noted that only standard sized fish are eligible for weighing.

Oa Saturday next the 22nd inst, another open match, under the control of several prominent members of the Association will be decided in the Bridgewater Lymrn, near to the Bridgewater Arns. Entries will he received up to 2.45 p.m. on the bank.
