PROPERTY OWNERS AND THE RATES.

ANNUAL MEETING

OF THE WARRINGTON ASSOCIATION.

Would a Cattle Market Pay?

The annull meeting of the Warrington and District Property Owners' Association was held in the Cairo- street schools on Wednesday evening,Mr. J. Lunt presid- ing in the absence of the president (Mr. Joseph• Moan- field). There was a fair attendance, including Coun- cillors Lord and Pemberton, Messrs. E. England, R. Henshall, C. D. Parkinson, W. Throssell, W. H. Stans- field, and J. Skelland. Mr. A. J. WILLETT, solicitor to the Association,wrote regretting his inability to attend." The question of rates at the present time" he added "is a very serious matter and I feel that the Association ought to have the hearty support of all property Owners. It is only by combina- tion that you will achieve success." THE REPORT AND BALANCE SHEET.

The Committee, in their second annual report, stated that there was a balance in the bank of £6 18s Bd. Many members had availed themselves of the advan- tages of the Association, useful service having been ren- dered to theta both as regarded assessments for rating, and the bringing about of amicable settlements of affairs in dispute, which were of vital importance to property- owners in general. The Committee were desirous of impressing upon the members the importance of co-operation and of further increasing the membership, thereby adding to the strength of an association which was non-political and formed absolutely with one inten- tion, namely, the safe-guarding of the interests of property owners generally. On the motion of the CHAIRMAN, seconded by Mr. J. SKELLAND the report was adopted.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTION OF PRESIDENT.

The CHA.TRMAN informed the meeting that IVlr.Monn- field, their president, desired to be relieved of the office, owing to indifferent health. It was, however,his intention to continue his membership of the Associa- tion. The committee had decided to ask Mr. Robert Henshall, who was an enthusiastic member. to accept the presidency, and he had great pleasure in moving that Mr. Henshall be elected.

Mr. THROSSELL seconded and the motion was heartily carried.

'ate • • •-••• Mr. HENSH .A.LL then took the chair, remarking that he accepted the office with a conviction of duty and a sense of responsibility as a fellow-sufferer. He did not want to pose as a martyr. but as one who had felt accutely the present condition of civic affairs—which Was unhappily, not limited to Warrington.—he was willing to " all he could to further the interests of the Association. (Hear, hear.) He had a great deal of faith in the town, and the situa- tion of W,7irrington was such that perhaps no town in Englani had greater facilities for expansion and develop- ment, if it was rightly governed. He did not wish to say that the town was not rightly governed, but it had not reached that state of perfection which needed no improvement. If it were necessary to the health and education of the town that the rates should be main- tained and even increased, he did not object very strongly. As a member of that Association, however, he was anxious that the rates should be equitably dis- tributed. He was not sure whether the anxiety on the part of those who had governed the town in past years to keep the rates lo ,A at any cost, had not, as it, were, incurred a mortgage which they were now called upon to discharge. The Association stood for econo:uv compatible with efficiency in government, but above all they wished to bring about equitable taxattion. The margin of profit left to the cottage property owner, as the result of his speculation and care,ha,d been constantly narrowed, until it Was almost an unknown quantity. 'The Association should if possible devise a scheme for the benefit of the relatively poor and thrifty owners. It had been suggested that a scale should be drawn up to ensure that in proportion as the rates increased, the rent or money that was paid for the rates should be altered. For the objects he had alluded to, he was prepared to give all the time and ability he possibly could, and he hoped he could confidently rely upon the loyal support of the members. (Applause). THE COMMITTEE AND OTHER OFFICERS. COUNCILLOR PEMBERTON AND UNFAIR TREATMENT OF OWNERS.

The following were elected members of the corn- mittee :—Messrs. E. England, W. Throssell, J. Skelland, Robert Lee, F. Pendlebury, J. H. Gittens, T. Barton, T. Critchley, W. Cooper, C. Longshaw' W. Hickman, John Pemberton, H. Winstanley, T. Harding, J. T. Whittle, W. H. Stansfield, J. T. Barton, R. Dale, Stephen Dale, and A. B. Marsh. Regret was expressed that the secretary (Mr. A. H. Clive) was desirous of resigning and Mr. G. T. Diggle was appointed to succeed him. Mr. ENGLAND moved the re-election of Mr. J. Lunt as treasurer, and said they could not have a better man for the post. It was distinctly creditable to him that there was a balance on the right side. Councillor PFABFIRTON seconded and said Mr. Lunt had done all he could in the interests of the society. (Hear, hear). Warrington, he should think, was the lowest-rented town in England. For the purpose of convenience the property owners many years ago agreed to pay the tenants' rates. Unfortunately for the ' owners, the rates had been continually increasing while the rents hal not. If the property owners would com- bine to take action, they would get the rants they were justly entitled to. It had been a great deal for the convenience of theCorporation,and to save that authority trouble and expense, that the owners had paid the rates in a lump sum. In many cases they lost Part of the rates altogether, because the houses happened to become untenanted: Every member of the Association should do his utmost to induce others to join. (Hear, hear.) As a member of the Corporation, he could tell them that the representations made by the committee of the Association had received proper attention, and he was glad they had. The owners would have no one but them- selves to blame if they did not get the justice they were entitled to. They were treated unfairly in many ways—in fact they seemed to be the "fair but" of everyone. At times they were nut to unnecessary expense by Corporation officials. If three-fourths of the owners of Warrington would combine they would be a real working force, and there was not the slightest doubt that the organization would act as a check on the expenditure and the rates would not be as heavy. He agreed with efficient government, but they should get it a great deal cheaper. His experience of the Town Council was that no trading concern in the world could make a profft if its business was conducted in the same way that the Warrington Corp oration's business wad. • The proposition was carried unanimously. ' In responding Mr. LuNT said the apathy of Warring- ton property owners was most disheartening. Why they should lack enthusiasm he did not know. He hoped that next year the Ass ,ciation would be stronger and that they would he able to present a bolder front. Messrs. GRIFFETEIS and ilnieff were appointed auditors.

INTERESTING SUGGESTTONS. TEIE TAXATION OF LAND VALUES. WOULD A CATTLE MARKET PAY?

A general discussion then took place. ' Mr. ENGLAND thought the members would be agreed that they Lad made a very happy choice in their president. He thought it augiirel well for the year's work. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Henshall had alluded to War- rington's geographical advantages, and the town was probably as well situated as any in England for industrial purposes. He was also very keen on the equitable distribution of rates. It seemed to him (Mr. England) that as a Property Owners' Association they could do some amount of good in helping forward the movement, which was very much to the fore at the present time, for the taxation of ground values. They were all well aware that It was mere than a party question, and he believed it was a matter that the association could very well take up. Land in towns ought to bear an equal rate with house- hold property, and the association should strengthen the Corporation to move in the matter. From time to time the Corporation were asked to assist in the move- ment, and he thought the association ought to ask them to help it forward. Other things could be done in Warrington to help on the progress of the town. Any- thing that could bring money into the town, and help to pay the rates, would be of assistance not only to property owners, but to everybody in the town. It seemed to him that Warrington would be a very good centre for a cattle market. The chief local expert on the matter of markets was Mr. Elijah Place. and he stated that he considered R 2,000 could he obtained by tolls from a Warrington cattle market. He (Mr. England) thought the Association would be wise if they asked the Corporation to take the subject into their serious consideration. He believed it would be to the welfare'of all the inhabitants of Warrington. If they could obtain £l,OOO a year for the rates from a cattle market it would be a great deal, and the very fact of detalers and farmers visiting the town would cause money to be spent. The tradespeople would thus be benefited. He might say that he was a believer in a vigorous policy. He did not think Warrington could afford to stand still in spite of the high rates. He, of Course, svmpathiberl with the property-owners and ratepayers, but he thought they might take it for granted that they got. to some extent. Ivalise for the rates. Tt would be money well snent if the main streets were .widened at once. They could not expect people to come to Warrington to shop if the shops were not attractive, and it seemed impossible for many shop keepers to make their places attractive-owing to the narrow streets. Mr. HICKMAN said that as far as the society was con- cerned, a more important matter than land values or cattle markets was the strengthening, of their member- ship. Every property-owner in Warrington ought to be a subscribing member. In past years there had been great inequality and laxity in the system of rating in Warrington, but he was happy to tell them that that was passing away. Since the revaluation of the town many people who had not been paying anything like

the amount they ought to have paid had been. pulled up to concert pitch. At the present time, however, he believed there were many who were not rated as they ought to be. Mr. STANSFIELD pointed out that the membership of the Association was nearly 150. He sympathised with Mr. England to a great extent on the question of ground values, but he did not share his views with regard to a cattle market. Warrington was not far from Birkenhead and butcher:s ware able to buy their stock from that town. Then again even if £l,OOO was received from a market a great deal might be swallowed up in officials'salaries. Property-owners were too much in the hands of Corporation officials, and he did not think it would be a bad thing to put in a polite hint that officials should live in the town and help to pay the rates. (Hear, hear.) Councillor PEMBERTON said th 3 Town Council had in hand at present a scheme for opening a cattle market. Such a market would be to the advantage not of Warrington butchers alone, bat of the farmers in South Lincashire and Cheshire. Corporation officials, ha contended, were too highly paid and there were too many of than. The Property Owners' Association ought to be dire3tly represented on the Town Council. Efforts should be made to advertise Warrington—to point out the advantages which the town presented to manufacturers. It was well served with, railways, was near the coal fields, and had a big working class population. Every tenant ought to pa y I his rates direct, as was the case in Oldham. Then there would not be one half of the present apathy of rate- payers in regard to municipal affairs. The CHAIRMAN pointed out that land not far from Warrington which might be suitable for industrial development, was very highly priced. After farther discussion the m ieting was closed.

===== 0002601_19041015_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE fiONDOLIERS."

NEXT WEEK'S PERFORMANCES.

A Chat with Mr. Thornley-Dodge.

When it became known that the Warrington Musical Society had been successful in again securing: the services of Mr. Thornley-Dodge as stage manager and dramatic instructor for the fortlic ming perform- ances of "The Gondoliers," it was felt that the success of the opera from a dranntic point of view was assurel It is just a year ago since lie piloted the society through the dramatic intr:gicies of light opera and the success which attended the perform- ances of" The Ye.unen of the Guard " was such as to enhance the reputation of Mr. Thornley-Dodge as a dramatic instructor. Those who witnessed the per- formances were warm in their praises of the work of Mr. Thornley-Dodge, and they shlwed their appre- ciation by a loud recall laefor- t:Ic; curtaih. ! The town is .now expectantly awaiting the raising of • the curtain on the second venture of the Warrington 'Musical Society in operatic work. Encouraged by the success of last year's light opera, the members, have gone a step further this year, and on .Tuesday. they will be seen for the first time in comic opera. " The Gondoliers " by Messrs. Gilbert and Sullivan has been chosen for representation. Byron in one of his poems gives us 'rather a sombre picture of the: gondolier : • "In Venice, Tasso's echoes are no more ' And silent rows the songless gondolier." In the hands of Messrs. Gilbert and Sullivan, the gondolier assumes a very different character. The- two gondoliers represented in the opera sing, dance and make merry— . " Two there are for whom, in dub, Every maid in Venice sighs " For sOrne months past, the members of the musical society. have bee engaged in practising the libretto, and music of this collaborated work, and it was with the object; of obtaininc, a few impressions from Mr. Thornley-Dodge on the work of the members that a reporter of "The Examiner" sought an interview with. him. Mr. Thornley-Dodge professes a strong disregard' for the interviewer, but he was induced to engage in a brief chat. In reply to a preliminary question as to - his opinion of the forthcoming performance of "The- Gondoliers," Mr. Thornley-Dodge said "The rehearsals are going along swimmingly and we are getting through some hard work. lam feel- ing quite happy as to the performances. Every- body seems enthusiastic for its .sucaess. They enter thoroughly into the Spirit of the thing and endeavour to give a liveliness and reality to the / scenes. lam particularly pleased with the way theY' have entered into the spirit of the dancing, whiell. forms so characteristic a part of the "The Gondoliers. It was a matter of great gratification to me the other evening when the two gondoliers and their wives danced their part remarkably well at the end of an- hour's instruction."

" Were you pleased with the last performance by the members?" asked the reporter. " Very. It was in every way creditable. Vocally - the Warrington society is the best I have ever coached. The music is perfectly safe in the hands of gr. Cross- ley, the conductor, who thoroughly understands his business. We get on very well together," "Do you think this year's performance will be. equal to that of last ?" " Well you ,cannot compare the two. It wou!d. be rather unfair to do so. Last year's work was light opera whilst " The Gondoliers " is distinctly a comic opera. "The Yeomen of the Guard" calls for acting. of the highest order, whilst " The Gondoliers " re- quires as much vivacity and humour as can be put into it." " What is your opinion of the cast for " The Gondoliers ?"

"It is 4 very strong one. Nearly all the artistes who appeared last year are included, and the new- comers,xvill I feel sure, more than exceed expectations -- I would not lik3 at this stage to particularise, but venture to predict that the audience will not only be delighted with the performance of their- local artistes' but there will be some personal successes made. If the opera does not go well there will not he a more disappointed person in the hall than myself." Mr. Thornlev-Dodge is an extremely busy man, and the demands upon his time and services are eon- stan.tly increasing. He has already in hand, in addi- tion to" The Gondoliers" at Warrington," The Scrap of Paper Waterfoot ; Mr. Kilvert's production at Brooklarids of "San Toy," where he will make his bow to the audience in the character of Li ; "The Yeomen of the Guard " and " H.M.S. Pinafore" at Bury, and arrangements are also in progress for the production of" The Gondoliers" at Buxton. Besides , his work as dramatic instructor, Mr. Thoruley-Dodg@. has large calls upon his time for concert work. As a • humorist he has gained a high reputation. Our only , fear is that his increasing popularity may lead him to quit the role of coaching amateur dramatic societies- , and adopt the wider field of the stage for the display of his undoubted talents.

ST. PETER'S SALE OF WORK.

[Continued from Page C.] YESTERDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

Yesterday afternoon" the sale was re-opened by Coun- cillor John Roberts—The Vicar announced that Thurs- day's proceeds amounted to .£53 17s. 9d. In asking Councillor Roberts to open the sale, the Vicar said he had been ,:onnected with the Church for many years, in fact he was a churchwarden long before the'present church' was opened. He had stuck to them through good and evil report, and had given most generously for the advance- ment of the work. (Applause). Councillor ROBERTS gave a number, of in- teresting reminiscences of the days when the services were conducted in that schoolroom and alluded to the self-denying efforts which led to the erection Of St. Peter's Church. He paid a tribute to the Work of the clergy, his fellow-warden (Mr. Balmforth), and the sidesmen, and said they at St. Peter's were a ver-v happy family. On the motion of Mr. J. Balruforth seconded by gr. T. S. Steel a vote of thanks was accorded to Councillor Roberts and the sale was resumed. In the evening a band under the conductorship of Mr. T. Boothroyd gave selections. Tbe total proceeds of the sale were over .£92.

FRIENDLY MATCH. PA.D G ATE JUNIORS V. LONGFORD Am ATE tirts.— These teams tried conclusions at Padgate, in fine weather, and the match resulted in a draw. Losing the toss, Padgate had to face a stiff breeze, and Longford commenced to attack. After a spell of even play, the Amateurs scored an unconverted try. Padgate were penalized, and Longford kicked a. goal. Half-time arrived with Longford five points ahead. On resuming, Padgate soon reached their opponents' line. A fine dribble by the forwards ended in Deely scoring, Cham- berlain kicking a goal. Padgate had hard lines in not scoring main,the ball being kicked dead. Time arrived with a score agave points each.

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED,. tutions. He was consulted and trusted by Parliamenst, Kings and Emperors, and he numbered among his per- sonal friend€l some of the wisest and best men of his SOCIAL SERMONETTES own generation. He died in 1831, at the ripe age of FOR seventy-six, and they buried him among the dust of Kings and nobles in Westminster Abbey. WORKING MEN. Thomas Telford was a good example Of how poor boys often become great men. He started life as poor as anybody. He roughed it, and endured hardship, but he made the most and the best of his talents and BY JOHN TAYLOR. opportunities. The secret of his success is to be found Author of "Search Lights on Land, Labour in his character. All through life he was famous for aad Social Problems." , his industry, intelligence, and integrity, and these qualities never fail to pull a man through. He lived to -- THOMAS TELFORD. fulfil Solomon's prophecy: " Seest thou a man diligent in his business? He shall stand before kings he shall not stand before mean men." "Telford had a long, successful, and useful life. Every step in his upward career from the 'poor peasant's hut in Eskdale, to Westminster Abbey, was nobly and valorously won. He was laborious, pains- taking, skilful; but what was better, he was honest and upright. He was a most reliable man, and hence he came to be extensively trusted."—Smaes. Thomas Telford, the famous engineer, and roadmaker, and bridge-builder, had a romantic career, and the story of his life reads more like a fairy tale than a sober record of facts. He started life in a poor shepherd's hut, near Dum- fries,in 1757, and shortly afterwards his father died, and he was left to the care of his mother, who earned a scanty pittance by working on a neighbouring farm. He picked up a little learning at the village school, and he gained such a reputation for cheerfulness and good temper that the boys nicknamed him ' Laugning•Tam." I.—TELFORD'S EDUCATION BEGAN WITH HIS APPRENTICESHIP. When he was fourteen years of age he was appren- ticed to a stonemason of high character and great ability. This man knew there was some sterling stuff in the lad, and he set himself the task of developing all that was brightest and best in him. He made him a capable and efficient stonemason, and he improved his education, and he moulded and fashioned his character. He not only taught him the art of hewing stone,. and building houses, but he trained him to use his eyes, and his brains, and his conscience, as well as his fingers. He taught him "the necessity of making himself acquainted with every detail in the procuring, and preparing, and employing of every kind of material, whether it be the produce of the forest, or the quarry'' or the forge." Telford in his latter years looked back to the days of his apprenticeship as the brightest and most helpful period of his life, and he was never tired of singing his master's praises. In his own biography, he tells us that "the habits he formed, and the training he received during his apprenticeship, were worth more to him than a University education. He not only became a work- man who could hold his own with any man, but he gained a reputation for industry, and intelligence, and integrity, that opened his way to the highest places in the land. IL—TELFORD SOON LEARNED EVERYTHING WORTH KNOWING. When he had finished his apprenticeship, he was offered a good situation in Edinburgh, but he did not stay there very long. As a matter of fact men would not let him stay long anywhere, and he had no desire to tarry anywhere, if he were not picking up some new ideas. As soon as ever he knew as much as his foreman or his employer, he thought it was about tune to be moving again and trying his luck elsewhere. His friends blamed him for his restlessness and told him that "a rolling stone gathers no moss." His 'retort was "a sitting hen never gets fat." His next move was practically a Government appoint- ment. Sir William Chambers had some large contracts for the building of Somerset House in London, and for the extension of Portsmouth Dockyard, and he was sorely in need of a trustworthy, capable builder, who could be trusted to carry outhis work in anyemergency. So he captured" Tam Telford" and senthim to London to help to build Somerset House. The youth soon distinguished himself there, and the following year he was promoted to be foreman of the building operations in Portsmouth Dockyard. This appointment brought him into close contact with the highest Government officials, and it gave him an oppor- tunity of studying the construction of docks, and quays, and wharves, and bridges. That was a liberal education for him. It enabled him to study the plans and methods em- ployed by the ablest engineers and architects in the kingdom, for overcoming some of the most formidable engineering difficulties, and it opened out a new field of operations for his restless energies. Next to the knowledge he gained during his apprenticeship, he valued the hints and helps he picked up during his sojourn at the Portsmouth Dockyard. When Sir Wi 1 ianiChambers had completed his contracts for the Government, the officials were anxious to retain the services of Telford, and to employ him permanently as inspector,and engineer,and adviser,for their harbours and quays, and bridges. But other employers were on his track,and competing keenly for his services. Offers of work with tempting wages were pouring in from all quarters, and before he was I,hirty years of age he was appointed surveyor of public works for the county of Shropshire. His new duties gave him the oversight of the roads, and bridges, and public buildings in that fair county. I need not remind you what our highways were in those days, and as for our bridges, well, we were content to ford rivers, and drive through streams, tn save expense, While Telford was putting those crooked things straight he entered on his career as a bridge builder. He built the Montford bridge over the Severn, near Shrewsbury, and] the Buildwas bridge, at Colebrook Dale, from new and original designs, and they are both fine examples of his genius. Each rew success simply inundated him with fresh applications for his services. He never had to seek a job. The jobs were always seeking him. The demands on his time and his intelligence multiplied so fast that he gradually drifted into a consulting engineer, who planned and devised great schemes,and watched the men who carried them out. lII.—TELFORD CARRIED OUT SOME DARING ENTERPRISES. It may be said of him as it was said of Sir Christopher Wren, the architect of St. Paul's Cathedral,—" if you seek his memorial, look around." If you would know what kind of man Thomas Telford was, go and study some of his mightiest works. In 1793, he was appointed sole agent, engineer, and architect for the Ellesmere Canal, which unites the waterways of the Mersey, and the Dee, with the river Severn. This canal is one hundred and three miles in length, and to avoid changes of level and the use of locks, Telford carried it over two fine aqueducts and through some lengthy tunnels. I was in the Vale of Llangollen a short timeago, and the fame of those aqueducts tempted me to go on a pilgrimage to see them. The one at Chirk carries the canal and towing path across the 'valley seventy feet above the level of the river. The other across the Dee is higher and longer still, and as I looked at their tall slender columns and graceful outlines, and remembered how long they had stood the'wear and tear of time, I was amazed at the daring genius, and the enduring work , of the poor Scotch lad who conceived and erected them To get over the difficulties of leakage in these aque- ducts be did not line and puddle them after Brindley's example. He struck out a new line of his own, and built a trough of cast iron plates, and fixed it over the masonry to keep it watertight. This is characteristic of the man. Difficulties were to him only things to be got over, and he always managed to get oyer them in his own original way. In 1801 the Government sent him to Scotland to inspeet the harbours and suggest plans for repairs and extensions. After he had done this they asked him to construct the Caledonian Canal from the north-east to the south-west coast of Scotland. This was a formid- able undertaking, for he had to build twenty-eight locks large enough to hold a sailing ship, and he had to cut a ship canal twenty-three miles in length. This undertak- ing cost nearly a million and a half of money, and it greatly enhanced Telford's reputatiou. While he was on his Scotch engagements he also made more than a thousand miles of road, and erected twelve hundred bridges, besides building harbours, docks, churches, and other public works. In 1808 he went to Sweden to superintend the making of the Gothic Canal, connecting Lake Wener with the Baltic. His next great work was the construction of the road from London to Holyhead. Our English roads were bad enough in all conscience, but those in North Wales were a great deal worse, and the passage of Menai Straits was risky and dangerous. The tides there often rise thirty feet, and rush past the rocks like a mill-race, and men, horses, and conveyances had to be carried from the mainland to the island of Anglesey by ferry boats. Telford designed a suspension bridge, with a span of five hundred and eighty feet, and a roadway one thousand feet in length, and hung it over the Strait. It is one hundred feet above the level of high water at spring tides, and vessels of three hundred tons can sail beneath it without lowering their top-sails. The first stone of the bridge was laid in 1819, and the masonry gave little trouble. But the hanging of the chains was another matter. The workmen climbed like monkeys to the most dangerous and dizzy heights, and three of them actually walked over the first chain as soon as it was fixed. The work lasted six years, and it cost £231,498. It opened in 1826, and the London and Holyhead mail-coach was one of the first vehicles to pass over.it. But time would fail me to tell von half the plans and schemes that passed through Telford's busy and fertile brain. He designed the Conway Suspension Bridge, and the Broomielaw at Glasgaw, and the Dean at Edin- burgh, and the Over at Gloucester. He designed St. Katharine's Dock at London, and improved the harbours of Peterhead, Banff. Fraserburgh, Cullen. and Kirkwall. He engineered the Glasgow and Paisley Canal, and the Macclesfield and Birmingham and Liverpool Junction, and the Gloucester and Berkeley, and several other similar waterways. And yet he was a good example of the truth that "the busiest men have always the most leisure." He found time to complete his own education, and to write articles to the Edinburgh Encyclopcedia. He learned to speak two or three other languages besides his mother tongue, and he wrote the story of his own strenuous life. In his biter years honours fell thick and fast upon hitu The Royal Societies of England and Scotland elected him to their membership,and he was feted and honoured by other learned, and philosophical, and scientific insti-

RUNCORN.—The Warrington club entertained the Runcorn organization on Saturday at the local club rooms Bibby's Restaurant, Market-street The game proved a rather one-sided affair, as the visitors came shorthanded. Preparations had been made for a team of from 14 to 20, but only seven put in an appear- ance, to the great disappointment of the Warrington players. The visitors received a hearty welcome, how- ever, and prior to commencing play were entertained to a substantial repast. Score : WARRINGTON. RUNCORN. Wins. Draws. Wins. 1 F. Dunne 1 H. Dobson 2 W. Davies 2 4 CITY OF BRADFORD EXHIBITION, The Bradford Exhibition authorities have been endeavouring for some days past to arrange for a meet- ing between the Hebburn Colliery and Besses-o'-th-Barn Bands at one of the Exhibition bandstands, in order to gave the public a special opportunity:of comparing the merits of the two bands about whom such warm con- troversy has raged since the award of the first prize to Hebburn Colliery at the Crystal Palace. The sugges- tion that they should do this came from an influential source in London, and the Committee took the matter up with eagerness, and offered every possible facility. Not unnaturally, it was found impossible to bring the bands to terms for a competitive !meeting, but the Exhibition autholities have completed an arrangement which is the next 'best thing. " Besses " has been engaged to render a full programme on the afternoon and evening of Saturday, the 22nd. Thus] an excellent opportunity is afforded to visitors of hearing both bands within a few days, and forming their own con- clusions upon the vexed question of whether or not the awarMat the Crystal Palace was merely due to an error in regard to the number.
